**Author:** Joseph D. Kirchner
**Date:** Draft of November 2026 (version 0.6; the text of 4 November 2026)
**Word Count:** about 62,300 words of text and 449 footnotes (Appendix A's table not counted)

## Abstract

A corporation is not a being the law made but a rule the people made: a sovereign's permission that a set of natural persons may act under one name, with two incidents no private agreement can create because each binds strangers, the separation of the body's debts from its members' and the body's survival through every change of them. A name in a rule holds no right against the rule's makers. The being is the Court's own addition, "existing only in contemplation of law" in its definition of 1819, and the word Johnson's Dictionary gives for a thing that so exists is *phantasm*, "something appearing only to imagination"; the rule is real, the people made it, and the article's quarrel is with the being alone, as the holder of a right against the rule's makers. Every constitutional right the Court has attached to the corporate name rests on a presupposition the article's second claim names and tests: that the corporation is bound to a sovereign of one will, a king who can grant, answer and be bound in his own person; the Constitution has no such sovereign. The people grant corporate existence through their constitutions and general laws and keep its terms in the hands they reserved; a right in the name against the people they can grant in one way only, by Article V. This article argues both claims from the history of the rule, from the common law's own law of fictions, and from the pages of the United States Reports. Part One traces the rule from Justinian's Digest, the sixth-century compilation of the Roman jurists' writings, to the state constitutions of the nineteenth century, which grant corporate existence under general laws, reserve in fifty-two of them the power to alter, amend or repeal in the same section, and define in twenty the thing granted; and it recovers the rule the common law stated from the bench between 1591 and 1625, and carried in the books from Coke to Blackstone, for every fiction of law, that it be of a possible matter, for a necessity, and work no injury to any man. Part Two sorts every right a corporation has been held to possess into two sets, the rights natural persons already hold and carry through the corporate name as a medium, and the privileges of the grant, and shows that nothing falls between them. A census of the terms on which the corporate constitutional right rests, "artificial being," "person," "corporate democracy," "associations of citizens," finds that each is a coined term or a transplant from the law of corporations, entered in a holding, and afterwards cited as though it were a term fixed by the text. Where the Court could not reconcile the name with the clause, it supplied by default what only the sovereign's positive grant could supply; in 1844 it confessed the inability in terms and held the corporation a citizen in the same opinion. From the census the article proposes a test. A holding that rests on a coined term alone has granted by ordinary means what the people's instrument grants only by Article V, and the test is removal: strike the term and look for the clause. A term that survives was a tool of construction; a term that takes the holding down with it was held as law, and a fiction of the impossible, established as a truth by one or some and held as law against the people's instrument, is what the Constitution's supremacy over ordinary means forbids. Run on *Citizens United*, the test finds the First Amendment for the members and nothing for the treasury. Run on *Griswold*, whose judgment the author would keep, it finds the Ninth Amendment and the word "liberty," which is why it is a test of law and not of a side; a holding that survives it is not thereby validated. The remedy is not Article V, which is what was bypassed, but the measurement, holding by holding, of the distance between what the Court said and what the people wrote.

---

## I. Introduction

In 1809 Chief Justice Marshall wrote that a corporation might be "considered as a mere faculty," or as "a company of individuals, who, in transacting their joint concerns, may use a legal name," and that the name "is considered as an inhabitant, when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it."[^1] Ten years later he wrote that a corporation "is an artificial being, invisible, intangible, and existing only in contemplation of law," possessing the properties its charter confers "either expressly, or as incidental to its very existence," among them "immortality, and, if the expression may be allowed, individuality."[^2] The first description is a rule. The second is a being, and the request for leave to use the expression is attached to one of its incidents, not to the being itself. What "existing only in contemplation of law" says, the dictionaries fix: contemplation, in Johnson's entry, is Locke's "keeping the idea, which is brought into the mind, for some time actually in view," the faculty "opposed to the power of action," and the law dictionary of 1891 names a thing so existing an *ens legis*, "a creature of the law; an artificial being, as contrasted with a natural person." American law took the being, dropped the request, and spent two centuries deciding what rights it had. A name in a rule holds no right against the rule's makers.

The second claim lies under the first, and the article states it here because every later part depends on it. The corporation the Court has been describing since 1819 is bound to a sovereign of one will, a king who could hear a petition, give a reply and be bound by it, and the Constitution has no such sovereign; Section II.E states why a rule written for a king who can answer in his own person cannot be applied to a sovereign that cannot, the Constitution the people ratified, and why the one right the people can grant against their own instrument they grant by Article V alone. The article's companion on the immunity of the sovereign's officers finds the same presumption under the other great doctrine the common law bequeathed to a republic that had abolished its premise.[^3]

This article returns to the first description and asks what follows from it. The answer is that nothing the Court has attached to the corporate name beyond the rights of the natural persons behind it has a source in the Constitution, and that the holdings which attached those rights did so by a method the Constitution does not allow: a coined term, entered in a holding, and afterwards cited as though it were a term fixed by the text. The same holdings carry a second mark, and the Court itself named it in 1844: where it could not reconcile the corporate name with a clause, it held by default, supplying what, in the system of positive law it professed to apply, only the grant could supply; Section III.B reads the confession.

The argument runs in two parts, and each Part opens by stating its own, so the roadmap is short. Part One is the history of a rule and not of an institution: the Roman jurists' *conceditur*, the canonists' *persona ficta* as a rule of treatment, the king's consent in the English books, the colonies that were corporations before they were States, the Convention's two refusals, and the people's constitutions from the 1840s, which grant corporate existence under general laws, reserve the power to alter, amend or repeal, and define the thing granted. Part Two asks what such a rule can confer. Section III is the census: every term on which a corporate constitutional right rests, read in the order of its entry into the Reports, each entering as a figure of speech or a transplant. Section IV sorts everything a corporation might claim into two sets, what natural persons already hold and what incorporation alone confers, and finds no third. Section V takes the derivative theory to its edge, where no member's consent can be found. Section VI runs *Citizens United* through the two sets. Section VII derives the test, that a holding resting on a coined term alone supersedes Article V, and refines it. Section VIII runs the test on *Griswold*, whose judgment the author would keep. Section IX states the scope and the remedy, which is measurement, and Appendix A is the census of the state constitutions.

The article coins nothing. The rule of treatment is Marshall's of 1809, and two New York courts stated it in the article's own grammar before the article did: that a corporation "is a mere conception of the legislative mind" which "exists only on paper through the command of the legislature," and that it "is more nearly a method than a thing."[^4] The derivative theory is the Court's own from *Deveaux* to *NAACP v. Alabama*, and the modern literature has stated it as a theory of what corporations may claim.

The asymmetry between the two results is the article's conclusion, and Section VIII.F states it on the texts: the people's unenumerated rights have a textual home, because the text was written by and for the people, and the name's have none, because the name is a rule the people made. The remedy for a holding that has granted by ordinary means what only Article V could grant is not Article V, which is what was bypassed, but measurement, in the forms Section IX.C states.

---

# Part One. The Rule: How Sovereigns Permitted Natural Persons to Act Under One Name, Rome to 1907

Part One is the history of a rule and not of an institution. It follows one permission through six settings: the Digest's *conceditur*, the canonists' *persona ficta* and the common law's bounds on every fiction it would admit, the king who alone could incorporate and alone could answer, the English franchise held of its grantor, the colonies that were corporations before they were States, and the people's constitutions from the 1840s. In each the shape is the same. A sovereign permits natural persons to act under one name, attaches to the name two incidents the persons could not create for themselves, and keeps the terms. Nothing in the history gives the name a right against its grantor, and the sovereign of the United States, the Constitution the people ratified, can be bound against the people only by its own amendment. The Part closes on the two sentences of 1809 and 1819, where Part Two begins.

## II. The Rule: How Sovereigns Have Permitted Natural Persons to Act Under One Name, from the Digest to the State Constitutions

### A. What a Corporation Is, Stated as a Rule and Not a Being: A Name That Functions in a Sovereign's Rule

A corporation is a name that functions in a sovereign's rule. The rule permits a set of natural persons to act under one name for stated purposes, and attaches to the name incidents the persons could not create for themselves: that the body's debts are not the members', and that the body persists through every change of them. That is the whole of it. There is no being behind the name, no organism with a will, and nothing to be acted upon as true for every purpose. There are natural persons, property, and a rule of the sovereign about how courts will treat them. "The corporation" is a word in the rule.

The common law itself called such a rule a fiction of law, and the article accepts the word in the sense the common law gave it, which Section II.D recovers: a lawful fiction is a disposition of the law against the truth, of a possible matter, for a necessity, and working no injury to any man. The difference between a lawful fiction so bounded and a fiction acted upon as true for every purpose lies in what each licenses. A fiction acted upon as true for every purpose carries into law whatever follows from the truth of it. A rule of treatment, which is what a lawful fiction is, is bounded by the purpose that made it: the name is "considered as" a person, in Marshall's words, "when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it," and not otherwise. The first licenses everything; the second licenses what its maker's rule requires and no more. The two meet in the third of the terms Section II.D defines: a fiction acted upon as true for every purpose is sooner or later acted upon for an impossible one, and against someone, and at that point a lawful fiction has become what the common law said it would never make, a fiction of the impossible held as law. The whole of Part Two can be stated as the finding that the Court, from 1819 onward, took the first license while citing the second rule, and that a rule cannot be true against the instrument that made it.

The article states this at the outset because the vocabulary of the subject resists it. From the thirteenth century the corporation has been called a *persona ficta*, a fictitious person; from the eighteenth, an "artificial person"; from 1809 in the American Reports, an "artificial being," a "legal entity," a "creature of the law"; and in the historical school of the nineteenth century a "living organism" with a "group-will." Every one of these is a figure of speech, and the figures are not neutral. A "creature" has a nature from which incidents follow. An "organism" has a will. An "entity" is a thing. Each word carries a metaphysics, and the metaphysics does work in the holdings that use the word.[^5] The article's claim about those holdings cannot be made in their vocabulary. So the chapter below tells the history of the rule, not of the creature, and it does not say that the corporation "arose," "emerged," or "was born." It says where sovereigns began enacting rules of this shape, what each rule permitted, and to whom.

The alternative is not the article's invention. Chief Justice Marshall offered it in the first American case on the subject, in 1809, before he offered the creature in 1819. A corporation, he wrote, might be "considered as a mere faculty," a capacity, or as "a company of individuals, who, in transacting their joint concerns, may use a legal name."[^6] And the mechanism by which the law treats such a name as a person he stated as a rule of treatment and not a fact about a being: the name "is considered as an inhabitant, when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it."[^7] Two New York courts said the same thing in their own grammar. Judge Vann, for the Court of Appeals in 1912: a corporation "is a mere conception of the legislative mind. It exists only on paper through the command of the legislature that its mental conception shall be clothed with power. All its power resides in the directors. Inanimate and incapable of thought, action or neglect, it cannot hear or obey the voice of the legislature except through its directors."[^8] Justice Bijur, in 1927: "a corporation is more nearly a method than a thing," and the law "has no need of defining it as a person or an entity, or even as an embodiment of functions, rights and duties, but may treat it as a name for a useful and usual collection of jural relations."[^9] Maitland, in 1900, gave the position a name, the "Bracket Theory" of the corporation, in which personality is "a mere labour-saving device, like stenography or the mathematician's symbols," and "the existing corporators" are "the real 'subjects' of those rights and duties that are ascribed to the corporation."[^10] He named it and doubted it on the same page, asking rather than asserting whether the shareholders, "these men of flesh and blood," are "the real and only sustainers of the company's rights and duties," and he noted that American writers, Morawetz among them, had already tried "to open the Bracket and find therein nothing but contract-bound men."[^11] The article takes Marshall's rule and Maitland's name, and not Maitland's bracket: Section IV will put the members inside the bracket for their own rights and outside it for the name's debts, which is neither the fiction theory nor the aggregate. It coins nothing.

### B. Rome: The Digest's *Conceditur*, the Name, the Two Incidents, and the Republic as the Model

The rule is older than any theory of the corporation, and the Roman jurists stated it in the verb from which the oldest theory takes its name. The Digest is the compilation of excerpts from the classical Roman jurists that the emperor Justinian promulgated as law in the sixth century, one of the four parts of the codification the later civil law called the *Corpus Iuris Civilis*, and the channel through which the jurists' rules reached the canonists of Section II.C and the English writers of Section II.F. Its title on suits brought in the name of a body opens with Gaius:

| **Dig. 3.4.1 pr. (Gaius)** | Translation |
|---|---|
| *Neque societas neque collegium neque huiusmodi corpus passim omnibus habere conceditur: nam et legibus et senatus consultis et principalibus constitutionibus ea res coercetur. paucis admodum in causis concessa sunt huiusmodi corpora: ut ecce vectigalium publicorum sociis permissum est corpus habere vel aurifodinarum vel argentifodinarum et salinarum.*[^12] | Neither a partnership nor a college nor a body of this kind is conceded to everyone at large; the matter is restrained by statutes, by decrees of the senate, and by imperial constitutions. Such bodies are conceded in very few cases: thus the companies that farm the public revenues are permitted to have a body, and those of the gold mines, the silver mines and the salt works. |

The verb is *concedere*. The "concession theory," the name given in the nineteenth century to the view that the corporation exists only by the sovereign's grant, is the Digest's own word for the rule, and the rule is a sovereign's.

The next fragment says what the concession permits:

| **Dig. 3.4.1.1 (Gaius)** | Translation |
|---|---|
| *Quibus autem permissum est corpus habere collegii societatis sive cuiusque alterius eorum nomine, proprium est ad exemplum rei publicae habere res communes, arcam communem et actorem sive syndicum, per quem tamquam in re publica, quod communiter agi fierique oporteat, agatur fiat.*[^13] | Those to whom it is permitted to have a body, in the name of a college or a partnership or any other such, have as their own, on the model of the republic, common property, a common chest, and an agent or syndic, through whom, as in a republic, what ought to be done in common is done. |

Three things are in that sentence. The body exists for those to whom it is *permitted*. What is permitted is to act *in the name*. And the model is the republic itself: the jurist describes the permitted body by analogy to the body politic, which is the analogy the English and American law would carry for the eighteen centuries after Gaius wrote, in the second century.

Ulpian, later in the same title, states the two incidents the permission attaches. "*Si quid universitati debetur, singulis non debetur: nec quod debet universitas singuli debent*": what is owed to the body is not owed to the members, nor do the members owe what the body owes.[^14] And: "*In decurionibus vel aliis universitatibus nihil refert, utrum omnes idem maneant an pars maneat vel omnes immutati sint*," in a town council or any other body it makes no difference whether all the members remain, or some, or none; and if the body comes down to a single member, that member may still sue and be sued, "*cum ius omnium in unum recciderit et stet nomen universitatis*," since the right of all has fallen to one and the name of the body stands.[^15] The separation of the body's obligations from the members', and the body's identity through any change of membership: these are the two features that distinguish a corporation from a partnership, and the Roman law states them not as properties of a being but as rules of the permission. What persists through the change of members, in Ulpian's own word, is the *nomen*: the name under which the permission runs.

So the Roman chapter of the history is complete in one title of the Digest: concession, name, the two incidents, and the republic as the model, all stated as rules. And one fragment from another title shows that, before anyone had called the body a person, the jurists had said what it does. Florentinus, in the Digest's title on sureties, allows a surety to be taken, after a debtor's death and before his inheritance has been entered upon, for what the inheritance now owes, "*quia hereditas personae vice fungitur, sicuti municipium et decuria et societas*," because an inheritance functions in the place of a person, as do a municipality, a decury and a partnership.[^16] The body is described by its function and not by a nature. It stands where a person would stand, for the purpose at hand, and the standing is a rule about the purpose; that is the rule of treatment in the jurists' own grammar, a thousand years before the canonists coined a person for it (Section II.C), and the plainest statement in the sources of what Section II.D calls a lawful fiction of the possible, a function the law performs and not a being the law asserts.

### C. The Canonists' *Persona Ficta* as a Rule of Treatment: A Person by Fiction, Made for the Purpose of Holding, Kept Within the Sovereign's Gift and Denied Natural Rights

The theory came later, and from the Church. The Roman jurists had stated the rule without a theory of what the permitted body was. The canon lawyers of the thirteenth century, who had before them "as the typical corporation, no medieval city, village or gild, but a collegiate or cathedral church," supplied one.[^17] The phrase was *persona ficta*, and according to Gierke "the first man who used this famous phrase was Sinibaldo Fieschi, who in 1243 became Pope Innocent IV."[^18] Maitland summarizes the doctrine: "The corporation is a person; but it is a person by fiction and only by fiction."[^19]

What the *persona ficta* was for is the point. A cathedral chapter had to hold land across generations without the land passing by inheritance at every canon's death. If the chapter were a real person it could be excommunicated, which was absurd; if it were only its members, the property would scatter. The *persona ficta* let the law treat the chapter's affairs under one name for the purpose of holding and not for the purpose of sinning. It was, in other words, a rule of treatment, the same thing Marshall would describe in 1809 as the name "considered as" a person "when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it." The *persona ficta* did not assert that there was a person. It asserted that the law would proceed, for stated purposes, as if there were; and the purposes were the measure of the "as if."

Two things the *persona ficta* carried with it should be marked, because they return in the American law. First, it kept the body within the sovereign's gift. Maitland: the prince's "right to suppress *collegia illicita* was supplemented by the metaphysical doctrine that, from the very nature of the case, 'artificial personality' must needs be the creature of sovereign power. At this point a decisive word was said by Innocent IV."[^20] The *persona ficta* was the concession theory's companion, and Maitland's word for the companion is "metaphysical," which is the article's word for every figure in this field. Second, its later form denied the fictitious person any natural rights. Of the Savignian corporation Maitland writes: "Really and truly the property of a corporation—for example a city or university—belongs to no real person or persons … Of 'natural rights' there can here be no talk, for 'artificial persons' can have no natural rights."[^21] That sentence, written in 1900 about a German theory of the 1840s, is the position the American Reports had stated in 1869 and would abandon in practice after 1886.

### D. The Common Law's Law of Fictions: The Three Bounds of a Lawful Fiction, the Three Terms the Article Uses, the Line the First Bound Draws Between the Parts, Holmes's "As If," and Gierke's Organism as the Other Figure Struck

The English law received the canonists' device and bound it, and the bounds are the measure this article applies throughout, because they are the law's own. Blackstone states them at the close of his account of the courts: fictions of law, "though at first they may startle the student, he will find upon farther consideration to be highly beneficial and useful: especially as this maxim is ever invariably observed, that no fiction shall extend to work an injury; it's proper operation being to prevent a mischief, or remedy an inconvenience, that might result from the general rule of law. So true is it, that *in fictione juris semper subsistit aequitas*."[^22] In a fiction of law, the maxim says, equity always subsists. His note gives the civil law's definition, from Godefroy on the Digest: against a fiction no proof is admitted, "for what would proof of the truth effect, where the fiction feigns against the truth? For a fiction is nothing else than a disposition of the law against the truth, in a possible matter, from a just cause."[^23] The sources Blackstone cites are the bench's own, and they are older than the Commentaries by a century and a half. In *Butler and Baker's Case*, decided in 1591, Coke reported that "the law will never make any fiction, but for necessity, and in avoidance of a mischief," that "relations are fictions in law, which will never do wrong," and, in his own note to the reader, that fictions of law "are to certain respects and purposes, and extend only to certain persons."[^24] In *Liford's Case* in 1614 the maxim appears as "*in fictione juris semper æquitas existit*," in a fiction of law equity always exists, the reason a fiction that deemed a freehold continuous could not make a stranger who came in by title a wrongdoer.[^25] In 1625, in *Sheffeild v. Ratcliffe*, Doddridge J. gave the definition and the bounds together from the bench of the King's Bench: a fiction of law is a disposition of the law against the truth, of a possible thing, from a just cause; and every fiction must be of a possible matter, "for the law imitates nature"; must be for the preservation of a ground of law, and "is never introduced but when necessary"; and must be no prejudice to any man, "for an act in law shall never be a wrong."[^26] Coke's *Institutes* in 1628 state the rule in English for the owner of land: "a relation or fiction of Law shall never worke a Wrong or charge to a third person, but *In fictione iuris semper est Equitas*."[^27] In a fiction of law there is always equity.

Three terms follow from the common law's statement, and the article uses them in this sense and no other. A *hypothetical* supposes something possible, for argument, and is tested by its consequences. A *lawful fiction* disposes against the truth, but only of a possible matter and from a cause, and works no injury. The chapter treated as one body to hold its land, the freehold deemed continuous in *Liford*, and the ship sued in rem are lawful fictions. A *fiction of the impossible* is the supposition the common law said it would never make. The *persona ficta* of the canonists and the rule of treatment of the Digest are lawful fictions in this sense. The article accepts them as the common law accepted them, as far as their purposes run. Bentham's objection, that a sovereign who can make a rule has no need of a fiction, "when the legislator desires to accomplish his object, he needs not feign, he commands," draws the same line from the other side: a disposition of a possible matter from a cause is a rule of treatment under another name, and only the supposition of the impossible is a fiction in the sense the maxims forbid.

The first bound draws the line between the article's two Parts. Part One's history is the history of a lawful fiction of the possible, and it runs as far as that fiction's purposes ran. Part Two begins where the Court supposed the impossible. It measures the Court's corporate "person," the being of Section I held as the holder of a right against its maker, against the same three bounds, and finds it failing each. It is not of a possible matter, since no name is born or naturalized, has a conscience or casts a vote. It is not necessary, since the grant already held the land and sued in one name without it. And it works the injury the maxim forbids: on the members whose consent it usurps, on the taxpayers who bear the burden it shifts, and on the persons it reached through the name. A fiction that works an injury has, by the common law's own rule, ceased to be a lawful fiction. The Court did not extend the fiction. It broke the law of fictions.

The third bound cannot be certified from the bench for a right of the name against the people at all. To every right the law assigns a correlative duty, owed by all others, not to infringe it; whether a right in the name works no injury to any man is therefore a weighing of every right the people retained against it, and the Ninth Amendment forbids the enumeration that weighing would require. The common law's test could be run where it was made, by a court weighing one subject's injury under a sovereign who could answer; against the people's instrument it cannot be run, and a fiction whose test cannot be run is not settled law but accepted usage.

The strongest modern statement against the distinction this section draws is Holmes's, in a letter of 1923: he regarded corporate personality as a fiction, "but as I said when young, on the Massachusetts bench, the very meaning of the fiction is that you are to act as if it was true."[^28] If that were the whole of the law of fictions, the Fourteenth Amendment's "person" would follow from the first "as if." It is not the whole, and the Court's own uses of the fiction say so, beginning with Holmes's for the Court seven years later, in a tax case in which the fiction served the State. Kentucky had taxed a corporation and taxed its shareholders on the full value of their shares; the shareholder called the corporation "a mischievous fiction" and claimed an interest in its property. "But it leads nowhere to call a corporation a fiction. If it is a fiction it is a fiction created by law with intent that it should be acted on as if true. The corporation is a person and its ownership is a nonconductor that makes it impossible to attribute an interest in its property to its members."[^29] Holmes did not retreat from the letter; "The corporation is a person" is its doctrine on the bench. The bound is in the sentence beside it, "created by law with intent," and the "as if" ran for the sovereign's purpose and in the sovereign's favor: the State taxed both the name and the members, and the nonconductor kept the members' interest out of the name's property so that both could be reached.[^30] Stone, C.J., quoting the sentence in 1945, drew the consequence the article draws. "Since the corporate personality is a fiction, although a fiction intended to be acted upon as though it were a fact, it is clear that unlike an individual its 'presence' without, as well as within, the state of its origin can be manifested only by activities carried on in its behalf by those who are authorized to act for it. To say that the corporation is so far 'present' there as to satisfy due process requirements, for purposes of taxation or the maintenance of suits against it in the courts of the state, is to beg the question to be decided."[^31] The name acts only through natural persons, and to use the fiction's word as if it decided a constitutional question is to beg the question. In the Reports the fiction's "as if" ran for taxation and for service of process, the sovereign's own purposes, and never once as a right of the name against its maker.

Against the *persona ficta* stood Gierke's own theory, which the chapter records because it is the other figure the article will strike. The German fellowship, Maitland reports Gierke as holding, "is no fiction, no symbol, no piece of the State's machinery, no collective name for individuals, but a living organism and a real person, with body and members and a will of its own."[^32] The "organism" is Spencer's metaphor of 1860 and the historical school's, and nobody observed a corporation's will. Maitland, translating it, declined to say whether it was true, and left the question as one "who can only guess": "if the reality of this will be granted by the philosopher, can he pause until even the so-called one-man-company has a real will really distinct from the several wills of the one man and his six humble associates?"[^33] The article does not pause there either. It observes that the real-entity theory's object is a metaphor, and that a theory whose object is a metaphor cannot supply the pre-legal existence the theory needs; in the common law's terms, it is a supposition of the impossible, which the law imitates nature in refusing to make. Remove the organism and what is left is either the members and their agreements, which is the aggregate theory, or nothing distinct from them, which is the lawful fiction of the canonists. Real-entity collapses into one of its rivals as soon as the metaphor is asked to be literal.


### E. The Sovereign of One Will: The King Who Could Answer, the People Who Consent Only by Article V, and the Fiction of No Redress

Every fiction has a maker, and the common law's lawful fiction of the corporation had one maker only. Blackstone states it in the chapter on corporations: the founder "in the strictest and original sense is the king alone, for he only can incorporate a society."[^34] The forms of consent Section II.F recounts were four, and each was a form of his. The grant ran from a single will; it could be conditioned, answered and resumed by that will; and the bond between the corporation and its sovereign ran to a person who could hear a petition in his own name, reply in it, and be bound by the reply. Every incident of the English corporation presupposes that person. So does every doctrine the American Reports built on the English vocabulary after 1819: a grantee that holds rights "against" its grantor presupposes a grantor who can be sued, who can answer, and who can be bound in his own person by what his courts decide.

The Constitution has no such grantor. The sovereign of the United States is the Constitution the people ratified: the people are sovereign through that instrument and in no other way, and they act through the courts and legislatures it ordains. The instrument has no ears, eyes or mouth of its own, and what hears for it cannot consent for it: no organ the Constitution ordained can grant a corporation a right against the instrument that ordained it, because the only act that binds the people against the Constitution is the Constitution's amendment. The people grant corporate existence through their constitutions and general laws and keep its terms in the hands they reserved; a right in the name against the people they can grant in one way only, by Article V. A corporation chartered by a State is therefore bound by its grant to the people of that State on their standing terms, and the right it claims against the people's instrument runs to a sovereign that cannot be petitioned in person and cannot be bound except by its own amendment. The presupposition that the corporation answers to a sovereign of one will is the presupposition under every entry of the census in Section III, and the article names it here, once, so that Part Two can measure it entry by entry. It is the same presupposition the companion study finds under the immunity of the sovereign's officers, where the maxim that the king can do no wrong was carried into a republic whose sovereign cannot be wronged in person and cannot answer in person, and the two doctrines fail for one reason.[^35]

The Constitution's own history states the incompatibility before the article does. American independence was the repudiation of a political will formed by one or by some. The colonists claimed, and Blackstone had written, that "no subject of England can be constrained to pay any aids or taxes, even for the defence of the realm or the support of government, but such as are imposed by his own consent, or that of his representatives in parliament," and that in dealing with a subject's property the public "is now considered as an individual, treating with an individual for an exchange."[^36] The Declaration charged the king with "imposing taxes on us without our consent."[^37] The remedy was the whole instrument, speech, the Ninth and Tenth Amendments, the Fourteenth, Article V, and the Supremacy Clause binding them together; and Marshall named the structure that made the remedy work: "The only security against the abuse of this power, is found in the structure of the government itself. In imposing a tax, the legislature acts upon its constituents. This is, in general, a sufficient security against erroneous and oppressive taxation," because the means of the Union "are given by all, for the benefit of all, and upon theory, should be subjected to that government only which belongs to all."[^38] A sovereign of many wills has its redress in its structure. A sovereign of one will has none against the sovereign in person, and must suppose that it needs none.

That supposition is the second fiction of the impossible, a sovereign who cannot be wronged, and it is the one that protects the first. Blackstone gives the reason for the maxim that "the king himself can do no wrong": "since it would be a great weakness and absurdity in any system of positive law, to define any possible wrong, without any possible redress."[^39] Then he says what kind of statement that is. Oppressions that would subvert the constitution "are cases which the law will not, out of decency, suppose; being incapable of distrusting those, whom it has invested with any part of the supreme power; since such distrust would render the exercise of that power precarious and impracticable"; and "wherever the law expresses it's distrust of abuse of power, it always vests a superior coercive authority in some other hand to correct it."[^40] That is a disposition of the law against the truth, from a cause named "decency," which is Godefroy's definition of a fiction, and on the next page Blackstone admits the redress the fiction hid: "the prudence of the times must provide new remedies upon new emergencies," and "whenever the unconstitutional oppressions, even of the sovereign power, advance with gigantic strides and threaten desolation to a state, mankind will not be reasoned out of the feelings of humanity; nor will sacrifice their liberty by a scrupulous adherence to those political maxims, which were originally established to preserve it."[^41] The redress was always the people. The judge's immunity is the same fiction of the impossible, with the redress named in the sentence that denies the proof,[^42] carried into the American Reports in 1872 in the same form,[^43] and the companion study measures it. Against a fiction no proof is admitted, and no redress is possible, are one disposition stated as a rule and as a reason. A fiction cannot survive proof, so it must forbid proof, and the forbidding is itself a fiction of the impossible, a wrong that no one can redress. The article will find the same self-protection at the birth of the corporate "person" in 1886, when the Court declined to hear argument on the question it then decided, and it is why the remedy of Part Two is measurement and not proof: proof against a fiction is excluded by the fiction's own law, and the only act that reaches it is to set it aside and read the text without it.[^44] The redress against the corporate fiction of the impossible, like the redress against the king's, was always in the instrument: the people of the States reserved it in their constitutions, Congress holds it in the power to condition the grant and the exception, the sovereign's oldest writ asks by what warrant the members exercise their power, and a court may decline to carry a word where no clause carries it.

The first fiction has the same birthplace as the second. Plowden's report of the Duchy of Lancaster case, in 1562, gives the king "two bodies, viz. a body natural, and a body politic," and the second "is a body that cannot be seen or handled, consisting of policy and government, and constituted for the direction of the people," "utterly void of infancy, and old age, and other natural defects and imbecilities"; in *Wroth's Case* eleven years later that body "is perpetual, and has perpetual continuance, and never dies," and an abbot and his convent, "all the corporation, which has perpetual continuance," are reasoned from it on the same leaf. Marshall's definition of 1819 is Plowden's body politic described: "cannot be seen or handled" is "invisible, intangible," "never dies" is "immortality," and "consisting of policy and government" answers to "existing only in contemplation of law." The corporation of the Reports is the king's body politic, carried into a republic that had abolished the king; what came over with it, and what stayed behind, Section III.A measures.

### F. England: The King's Consent, the Franchise Held of Its Grantor, and Blackstone's Gloss on the Civil Law Corrected

In England the rule took the form the American colonies inherited, and Blackstone stated it at the opening of his chapter on corporations. The law has "found necessary, when it is for the advantage of the public to have any particular rights kept on foot and continued, to constitute artificial persons, who may maintain a perpetual succession, and enjoy a kind of legal immortality." These "are called bodies politic, bodies corporate (*corpora corporata*), or corporations," and they exist "in order to preserve entire and for ever those rights and immunities, which, if they were granted only to those individuals of which the body corporate is composed, would upon their death be utterly lost and extinct."[^45]

Three things again. The purpose is continuity, the incident the Roman law called the body's identity through change of members. The rights are "granted," to the individuals or to the body, by someone. And the body is, in the same sentence, a "body politic": the republic-as-model of the Digest, carried into the English books as a matter of ordinary nomenclature. Then the rule of creation: "with us in England, the king's consent is absolutely necessary to the erection of any corporation, either impliedly or expressly given." The forms of consent were four, by prescription, by common law, by act of Parliament, by royal charter, and all four were forms of the sovereign's grant; prescription was a presumed charter, and the common-law corporations, the king and the parson, were the sovereign's own. The act itself, Blackstone says on the next page, "is usually performed by the king alone, in virtue of his royal prerogative," and its words are "*creamus, erigimus, fundamus, incorporamus*."[^46] And when the chapter turns to ask who may visit a corporation, it names the grantor once more, in the sentence Section II.E has quoted for the one will: "The founder of all corporations in the strictest and original sense is the king alone, for he only can incorporate a society."[^47] Consent, act and incorporation are one person's. The page then names a second founder for one class, the private endower of a college or hospital, whom the law calls the founder of the dotation, the gift of revenues, and not of the body: the king is "the general founder of all colleges and hospitals" in the sense of the incorporation, and "the sole founder of all civil corporations." The chapter's account of the body runs from him; the endower's part is property, which Part Two finds in the persons' hands (Section IV.E).

Blackstone also records a contrast that the chapter must correct. "Corporations, by the civil law," he writes, "seem to have been created by the mere act, and voluntary association of their members; provided such convention was not contrary to law."[^48] The Digest's first fragment, which Blackstone's own note at the same page cites for the *illicitum collegium*, says the opposite: such bodies are not "conceded to everyone at large" and are "conceded in very few cases." Blackstone was reading the later Roman law of the *collegia tenuiorum*, the burial societies of the poor that a senatorial decree had licensed by class, and generalizing it. The chapter reads the Digest against the gloss, and notes the gloss as one source of the "voluntary association" vocabulary that would reappear in the Massachusetts Constitution of 1780 and in the Supreme Court's reasoning in 1888.

The Digest also has a text for free association, and it belongs on the same page, because the two fragments together are the two sets in one book. Gaius, on the Twelve Tables: "*Sodales sunt, qui eiusdem collegii sunt … his autem potestatem facit lex pactionem quam velint sibi ferre, dum ne quid ex publica lege corrumpant*," members of a college may make among themselves whatever pact they will, so long as nothing in public law is broken, a rule Gaius traced to a law of Solon.[^49] That is the first set, what the members can make among themselves. To have a body, *corpus habere*, to act under a name with common property and an agent, is what Gaius's fragment says is "conceded in very few cases," and that is the second, what binds strangers. On the same page of the Digest Marcian records the imperial mandates against the *collegia sodalicia*, the leave given to the poor to pay a monthly subscription so long as they meet once a month and no illicit college forms under the pretext, and the rule that an illicit college is dissolved and its common fund divided among its members: the sovereign governing the form, and the members' own money returning to them when the form is withdrawn.[^50] Blackstone's gloss read the first fragment as if it were the second.

What the English rule did not do is as important as what it did. The chartered body was a franchise, a portion of the Crown's prerogative granted out to subjects, and a franchise is held of the grantor. It was held for a purpose as well. The "general duties of all bodies politic, considered in their corporate capacity," Blackstone writes, "may, like those of natural persons, be reduced to this single one; that of acting up to the end or design, whatever it be, for which they were created by their founder," and the King's Bench said the same of the end in 1789, when Ashhurst, J., described a town's corporation as "originally created for the public good, and not merely with a view to the private interest of individuals," and held that when it "can no longer perform those functions which is a condition incident to its incorporation," the Crown may grant it anew.[^51] The grant was bounded by the design that made it, which is the bound Section II.D states of every lawful fiction, here stated of the English franchise in the books the American courts inherited. Nothing in Blackstone's chapter attributes to a corporation any right against the king who consented to it. The body politic within the realm was, as Hobbes had put it a century earlier, a "system subordinate," lawful only by the sovereign's authority and for that reason not a rival to it.[^52] The whole point of requiring the king's consent was to keep "lesser commonwealths" subordinate to the greater one. The American law would inherit the consent and, for one class of corporation, forget the subordination.

### G. America: The Colony as Corporation, the Convention's Refusal, and the States' Grant in the People's Words

#### 1. The Colonies Were Corporations Before They Were States

The American States were corporations before they were States. The Virginia Company and the Massachusetts Bay Company were chartered trading corporations whose charters became the colony's frame of government; Connecticut and Rhode Island governed under their royal charters of 1662 and 1663 until 1818 and 1842 respectively, long after they had become States of the Union. The body politic and the body corporate were, in the colonial charters, literally one document. When the Massachusetts Constitution of 1780 defined the State, it did so in the vocabulary of the corporation: "The body politic is formed by a voluntary association of individuals: it is a social compact."[^53] The identity between corporation and body politic that the Digest had drawn by analogy and Blackstone by nomenclature was, in America, the founding fact.

Two consequences follow for everything after. The first is that when the colonies became States, the Crown's power to consent to corporations passed to them as successors to the Crown's prerogative, and the people of each State then gave themselves a constitution, in convention or by ratification, or kept a charter as one, vesting legislative power in a legislature. The state corporation therefore rests, from the beginning, on a grant by the people of the State. The second is that the American law inherited, with the power, the fear. The chartered body was a "lesser commonwealth," and the Jacksonian objection to special charters, to banks, and to monopolies was Hobbes's objection in a republican idiom: an *imperium in imperio*, a body politic within the body politic, holding privileges the people had not granted to everyone.

#### 2. The Convention Asked for the Power and Refused It, 18 August and 14 September 1787

The federal Constitution grants no power to create corporations, and the omission was not an oversight. The Convention considered the power twice and declined it both times.

On 18 August 1787 Madison submitted a list of additional powers to be referred to the Committee of Detail, among them a power "To grant charters of incorporation in cases where the public good may require them, and the authority of a single State may be incompetent"; Pinckney's list of the same day included "To grant charters of incorporation."[^54] The Committee's report did not return either. On 14 September, three days before the signing, Franklin moved to add to the enumerated powers "a power to provide for cutting canals where deemed necessary," and Madison, Farrand's notes record, "suggested an enlargement of the motion into a power 'to grant charters of incorporation where the interest of the U. S. might require & the legislative provisions of individual States may be incompetent.'" Randolph seconded. "Mr King thought the power unnecessary."[^55]

The next page gives the debate. King: "The States will be prejudiced and divided into parties by it— In Philada. & New York, It will be referred to the establishment of a Bank, which has been a subject of contention in those Cities. In other places it will be referred to mercantile monopolies." Wilson answered that mercantile monopolies "are already included in the power to regulate trade." Mason "was for limiting the power to the single case of Canals. He was afraid of monopolies of every sort, which he did not think were by any means already implied by the Constitution as supposed by Mr. Wilson." The motion was narrowed to canals alone and lost, three States to eight. "The other part fell of course, as including the power rejected."[^56] McHenry's notes of the same day are shorter: "moved by Virginia To empower Congress to grant charters of incorporation in cases where the U. S. may require them and where the objects of them cannot be obtained by a State. Negatived."[^57]

The record settles three things. The power was asked for, in terms, by the Convention's most careful member, and declined: the motion was narrowed to canals and lost, three States to eight, and the rest of it fell with the vote. The reasons given were the Bank and "monopolies of every sort," that is, the fear of the lesser commonwealth. And Mason's remark that the power was not "already implied by the Constitution" is Mason's answer, recorded three days before the signing, to the argument the Court would accept thirty-two years later. The sequel is the article's to tell before an adversary tells it: the First Congress chartered the Bank in 1791 over the same objection, and *McCulloch* records both that the bill "did not steal upon an unsuspecting legislature, and pass unobserved" and, on the leaf before, that "a bold and daring usurpation might be resisted, after an acquiescence still longer and more complete than this."[^58]

#### 3. The Federal Power by Implication: *McCulloch* Stated and Not Relied On, and the Premise It Supplies

In *McCulloch v. Maryland* the Court held that Congress could incorporate a bank, and it did so by denying that incorporation was a power at all. "The power of creating a corporation, though appertaining to sovereignty, is not, like the power of making war, or levying taxes, or of regulating commerce, a great substantive and independent power, which cannot be implied as incidental to other powers, or used as a means of executing them. It is never the end for which other powers are exercised, but a means by which other objects are accomplished."[^59] The corporation was a means, and the Necessary and Proper Clause supplied the means to the ends enumerated. The chapter states this honestly and does not rest on the contrary. If *McCulloch*'s implication is rejected, every federal corporation from the Bank to the present rests on nothing the people granted; if it is accepted, the federal corporation rests on an implication the Convention's own members said was not there. The article's thesis does not turn on which. It turns on what any corporation, however created, holds.

But *McCulloch* is cited here for a second reason, because in the same opinion Marshall stated the premise on which the whole of this article rests. The Constitution's authority, he wrote, derives from the conventions of the people: "The government proceeds directly from the people; is 'ordained and established,' in the name of the people; and is declared to be ordained, 'in order to form a more perfect union, establish justice, insure domestic tranquillity, and secure the blessings of liberty to themselves and to their posterity.'"[^60] The people are sovereign through the Constitution they ratified, the instrument is theirs, and the instrument's purpose is the list in the Preamble, ending in the blessings of liberty secured to the people and their posterity. Justice Wilson had said it in 1793: "To the Constitution of the United States the term SOVEREIGN, is totally unknown," because the people who ordained it, "serenely conscious of the fact," had no need to announce themselves as what they were.[^61] Chief Justice Jay in the same case: "Here we see the people acting as sovereigns of the whole country; and in the language of sovereignty, establishing a Constitution by which it was their will, that the State Governments should be bound."[^62] And the Court said it of a corporate charter, in 1880, in words this article could not improve: "the power of governing is a trust committed by the people to the government, no part of which can be granted away." The people, "in their sovereign capacity, have established their agencies," which govern within their authority while in power, "but they cannot give away nor sell the discretion of those that are to come after them."[^63] The premise on which Part Two measures the Court's grants to the name was stated by the Court itself, in a case about what a legislature may promise a name, and Section IV.E returns to the page. A grant, in this order, is an act of the people, through the federal Constitution, a state constitution, or a legislature acting within the powers a constitution gave it. An act of a court is not an act of the people. That is the premise, and it is the Court's.

#### 4. The States' Grant in the People's Words: Existence Under General Laws, the Condition in the Same Section, the Definition by What Individuals Lack

The state corporation's grant is not a matter of inference. The people of the States wrote it down, and from the 1840s onward they wrote it into their constitutions in nearly uniform words, together with the condition.

Michigan, 1850, article XV, section 1: "Corporations may be formed under general laws, but shall not be created by special act except for municipal purposes. All laws passed pursuant to this section may be amended, altered or repealed."[^64] Alabama, 1867, article XIII, section 1, in nearly the same words, with "all general laws, and special acts passed pursuant to this section" for Michigan's "all laws."[^65] Illinois, 1848: "Corporations, not possessing banking powers or privileges, may be formed under general laws, but shall not be created by special acts, except for municipal purposes, and in cases where, in the judgment of the general assembly, the objects of the corporation cannot be attained under general laws"; Wisconsin, the same year, in the same frame, with the legislature as the judge of the exception.[^66] Illinois, 1870: "No corporation shall be created by special laws, or its charter extended, changed or amended, except those for charitable, educational, penal or reformatory purposes, which are to be and remain under the patronage and control of the State, but the general assembly shall provide, by general laws, for the organization of all corporations hereafter to be created."[^67] West Virginia, 1872: "The Legislature shall provide for the organization of all corporations hereafter to be created, by general laws, uniform as to the class to which they relate, but no corporation shall be created by special law."[^68] And Virginia, 1902, defining the thing granted: "the term 'corporation' or 'company' shall include all trusts, associations and joint stock companies having any powers or privileges not possessed by individuals or unlimited partnerships, and exclude all municipal corporations and public institutions owned or controlled by the State."[^69]

The six are specimens. The census of Thorpe's seven volumes, which the article carries as Appendix A, counts seventy-three constitutions and amendments of forty-two States, from Delaware's of 1831 to Oklahoma's of 1907, that speak to how a corporation comes to exist.[^70] They fall into three forms, in sequence. The first kept the special charter and fenced it with a two-thirds vote: Delaware in 1831, Michigan in 1835, Florida in 1838, Texas in 1845. Delaware's people wrote the condition into the same sentence, "with a reserved power of revocation by the legislature," and gave every charter twenty years. The second is the form the six specimens show, and Louisiana wrote it first, in 1845, a year before New York: corporations "shall not be created in this State by special laws, except for political or municipal purposes, but the legislature shall provide, by general laws, for the organization of all other corporations, except corporations with banking or discounting privileges, the creation of which is prohibited." Thirty-three constitutions took that form between 1845 and 1894. The third, from Iowa's of 1857 and Illinois's of 1870 onward and in twenty-eight constitutions, forbids the special charter outright and commands the legislature to provide by general laws for "the organization of all corporations hereafter to be created"; Tennessee's people gave the section its title, "corporations only to be provided for by general laws." Sixty-one of the seventy-three are in the second or third form. Fifty-two reserve, in terms, a power over the laws or the charters themselves, counted by the rule Appendix A's head note states. And twenty, beginning with New York's of 1846 and ending with Oklahoma's of 1907, define the thing granted, in words that vary by a syllable: "all associations and joint-stock companies having any of the powers or privileges of corporations not possessed by individuals or partnerships." Mississippi's people in 1890 put it in fewer words: companies "for pecuniary gain, having privileges not possessed by individuals or partnerships." Not one of the seventy-three grants the name anything against the State.

The people's grant has a shape, and the shape is the rule this chapter has traced from the Digest. The inference should be stated exactly, because an adversary will say that a state legislature's power is plenary and needs no clause, and that clauses of this kind regulate the mode and condition of a power already held. Both are true, and neither reaches the point. The plenary power is itself the people's grant, made once: when the colonies became States the Crown's power passed to them, and the people of each State then gave themselves a constitution, in convention or by ratification, or kept a charter as one, as this section began by saying, vesting legislative power in a legislature. The vesting is the grant, and the seventy-three clauses are the people's statement of the mode and the condition of a power they had vested, not its sole source; what they add to the vesting is the people's own description of the thing their legislature may permit. The grant is of *existence under general laws*: the people permit the legislature to permit, and withdraw the special charter that had been the lesser commonwealth's instrument. The grant carries its *condition in the same section*: the laws "may be altered, amended, or repealed," and fifty-two of the seventy-three reserve the power in terms. The grant is defined by *what individuals lack*: Virginia's people called a corporation whatever has "powers or privileges not possessed by individuals or unlimited partnerships," which is to say the incidents, and nothing else; the definition is the people of a State conceding the whole of this Part, that everything the name has beyond a natural person is the grant's.[^71] And the grant confers *no right against the grantor*: not one constitution in the census grants the name anything as against the State, several forbid the legislature to extend a charter once given, and every clause that speaks runs in one direction, the people over the name. The Court had read the grant the same way in 1869, before it stopped reading, in the sentence Section IV.C quotes whole: a grant of corporate existence runs "to the corporators," confers "special privileges," and gives the single name and the exemption from individual liability.[^72] That is the rule, in the Court's words and the people's, and it is the whole of what the sovereign gave.

#### 5. The Two Sentences of 1809 and 1819: Marshall States the Rule, Then Clothes It and Hedges the Clothing

Part One has traced one rule through its settings, and it is the same rule in each. The Digest permitted a body to act under a name and attached two incidents to the permission, the separation of the body's obligations from the members' and the body's identity through every change of them, and it stated both as rules of the permission and not as properties of a being. The canonists' *persona ficta* was a rule of treatment, and the common law bounded every fiction of law by three conditions, that it be of a possible matter, for a necessity, and work no injury to any man. The English books made the king's consent absolutely necessary and the franchise a thing held of its grantor, and the king who granted could answer for the grant and be bound in his own person. The colonies came to independence as corporations; the Convention refused a federal power to incorporate on 18 August and again on 14 September 1787; and the people of the States granted corporate existence under general laws, reserved in the same section the power to alter, amend or repeal, and defined the thing granted by what individuals lack. At every step the corporation is a sovereign's permission to act under a name, and the permission is a rule the sovereign made. Nothing in the history gives the name a right against its grantor, and the sovereign of the United States, the Constitution the people ratified, can be bound against the people only by its own amendment.

The chapter closes where Part Two begins. Marshall stated the rule in 1809, in *Deveaux*, and clothed it in 1819, in *Dartmouth College*, hedging the clothing with "if the expression may be allowed"; Sections III.B and III.C read the two sentences, and the hedge is Part Two's first case.

---

# Part Two. The Rights: What the Rule Can Confer, and the Test for a Holding That Granted More

Part Two asks what the rule of Part One can confer, and measures what the Court conferred. It opens with the census, the record of every term on which a corporate constitutional right rests, read in the order of its entry (Section III); sorts everything a corporation might claim into two sets and finds no third (Section IV); takes the derivative theory to the edge where no member's consent can be found (Section V); runs *Citizens United* through the two sets (Section VI); derives the test, that a holding resting on a coined term alone supersedes Article V, and refines it (Section VII); runs the test on a judgment the author would keep (Section VIII); and states the scope of the test and the remedy, which is measurement (Section IX). Its first case is the hedge of 1819, "if the expression may be allowed," and the citations that dropped it.

## III. The Coinage Census: Every Term on Which a Corporate Constitutional Right Rests Entered the Reports as a Figure of Speech or a Transplant

This section is the evidence. It reads every term on which a corporate constitutional right has been made to rest, in the order the term entered the Reports, and asks of each how it entered, whether it was argued, and what was afterwards hung on it. The answers fill the table in Section III.J, and the table is what the rest of Part Two measures.

### A. Coinage and Transplant: The Two Things a Term Can Be, and the Five Questions Put to Each

A term of art is a word the law has agreed to use in a fixed sense. A coinage is a word someone made up. The difference between them is not in the word but in what stands behind it: a term of art has a holding, a statute, or a settled usage that fixes its meaning, and a coinage has an author. The claim of this Section is that every term on which a corporate constitutional right rests entered the constitutional Reports as one of two things, neither of which is a term of art: a coinage, a figure of speech offered by a judge or a reporter to describe a result, or a transplant, a word settled in another field and carried into this one with nothing to fix it here; and that each was afterwards cited as though it were a term of art fixed by a holding. The claim is documentary. It is proved or disproved by reading the pages in order.

The second of the two, the transplant, is the one the objection to this census will press, and the entry of the table that carries the modern law belongs to it. A transplant is a term settled in one field of the law and carried into another without a holding, a statute, or an argument to carry it.[^73] The word "person," applied to a corporation, was not a coinage in 1886. It was settled usage older than the Republic.[^74] The English books divide persons into natural and artificial, and the artificial person is the corporation;[^75] the Reports' own vocabulary from 1809 is "artificial being," and *Paul v. Virginia* in 1869 wrote "artificial persons created by the legislature" to say what a citizen is not. But what the usage had settled was a treatment and not a holder. Part One is the record of it: a body of natural persons may hold, sue, and succeed under one name, for the purposes the grant allows, and the name holds nothing against its grantor. What the transplant carried was the king's body politic of Section II.E, and what it left behind was every check the English law kept on it. Blackstone's heir is, at the instant of the demise, "king to all intents and purposes," and the prerogative runs "unless where the constitution hath expressly, or by evident consequence, laid down some exception or boundary"; *Letson* deemed the corporation a person "to all intents and purposes," and *Dartmouth* gave it its properties "either expressly, or as incidental to its very existence." The same king was "the visitor of all civil corporations," his grants were construed by their words, and he could be petitioned and could answer; none of the three came over with the formula. *Deveaux*'s sentence in Section III.B states the treatment in terms: the name "is considered as an inhabitant, when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it."

A term of art carries its meaning with it. When "person" was carried from the law of corporations, where it fixed how a name is treated, into the Fourteenth Amendment, where it fixes who holds rights against the States, nothing came with it but the word. No holding carried it; Section III.E shows there is none. No statute carried it. The one statute the objection can cite, Congress's Dictionary Act, governs Acts of Congress and not the people's instrument, its first form is three years younger than the Amendment, and it is the people's own rule of treatment for the name, which Section V.A reads from the Act of 1871 as the Court quotes it.[^76] The Court that relies on it says the word has two senses, not one: "The term 'person' sometimes encompasses artificial persons (as the Dictionary Act instructs), and it sometimes is limited to natural persons."[^77] Which sense the Amendment carries is the question a transplant leaves unasked. No argument carried it: the Court "does not wish to hear argument on the question." A coinage is a word made up. A transplant is a meaning made up for a word that already existed, and it is the harder of the two to see, because the word arrives looking settled. The census treats the two alike, and the test of Section VII runs on both.

The method is the one the author's book, *The Subject's Unanswered Plea: A Restorative and Comparative History of Immunity* (book manuscript, 2026), applied to citations and applies here to vocabulary.[^78] For each term, five questions. Where did it enter the Reports, and from what source outside them? Who introduced it, and was the introduction argued, assumed, or hedged? Did the hedge survive the next citation? What was afterwards hung on the term? And, the question the rest of Part Two answers, does the holding survive the term's removal? The first four are answered below, term by term, in the order of their appearance. The result is a table, and the table is the argument.

### B. 1809: "Artificial Being," "Mere Legal Entity," and the Road Not Taken in *Deveaux*

The vocabulary enters the Reports in a single sentence of Chief Justice Marshall's, in *Bank of the United States v. Deveaux*, ten years before *Dartmouth College*. One of its words is older, and its two earlier appearances are both limits. Justice Iredell, dissenting in *Chisholm v. Georgia* in 1793, listed the differences between a State and a corporation and began: "A corporation is a mere creature of the king, or of parliament; very rarely, of the latter; most usually, of the former only." It owes "its existence, its name, and its laws … to the authority which create it," where a State "derives its authority from the same pure and sacred source as itself: the voluntary and deliberate choice of the people."[^79] Marshall used the word first for the Court in 1804, in *Head & Amory v. Providence Insurance Co.*, of an insurance company "which, in its corporate capacity, is the mere creature of the act to which it owes its existence," and drew from the figure a limit and not a nature: the company "may correctly be said to be precisely what the incorporating act has made it, to derive all its powers from that act, and to be capable of exerting its faculties only in the manner which that act authorizes."[^80] Two pages on he said the same thing without the figure: "with these bodies which have only a legal existence, it is otherwise: the act of incorporation is to them an enabling act; it gives them all the power they possess; it enables them to contract, and when it prescribes to them a mode of contracting, they must observe that mode, or the instrument no more creates a contract than if the body had never been incorporated."[^81] A body that is what its act made it, and no more, is the rule of this article's Part One. The vocabulary that would afterwards be used to give the body more begins in 1809. The question was whether a corporation could sue in the federal courts under the Diversity Clause, which speaks of "citizens." Marshall answered:

> That invisible, intangible, and artificial being, that mere legal entity, a corporation aggregate, is certainly not a citizen; and consequently, cannot sue or be sued in the courts of the United States, unless the rights of the members, in this respect, can be exercised in their corporate name.[^82]

Three terms arrive together: "artificial being," "legal entity," and the adjective that governs them both, "mere." The sentence does not argue for the being; it describes one. And the sentence's second half states, in its first form, the theory that will later be called derivative: the corporation is not a citizen, and the suit proceeds if at all because "the rights of the members … can be exercised in their corporate name." The right is the members'. The name is the means.

The next sentence is the road the law did not take. "If the corporation be considered as a mere faculty, and not as a company of individuals, who, in transacting their joint concerns, may use a legal name, they must be excluded from the courts of the Union."[^83] Here Marshall offers the alternative to the being: the corporation "considered as a mere faculty," a capacity, or as "a company of individuals" who "may use a legal name." On the next leaf he says where the being came from. "As our ideas of a corporation, its privileges and its disabilities, are derived entirely from the English books, we resort to them for aid, in ascertaining its character. It is defined as a mere creature of the law, invisible, intangible and incorporeal."[^84] The "creature" is an import, and Marshall names the shipment. The bill of lading survives. In *The Case of Sutton's Hospital* in 1612, Coke wrote that "a corporation aggregate of many is invisible, immortal, and rests only in intendment and consideration of the law," and in the next breath that such bodies "cannot commit treason, nor be outlawed, nor excommunicate, for they have no souls, neither can they appear in person, but by attorney": invisibility and immortality were stated as a rule of how the law treats a body of men, with the disabilities the rule carries, and not as a being with properties.[^85] What 1809 took from the English books was Coke's rule of treatment; what 1819 and 1889 hung on it was their own. Then, applying Coke's gloss on a statute of Henry VIII that taxed "inhabitants," he states the mechanism by which the law treats a name as a person: the tax "is imposed on the corporation itself, and consequently, this ideal existence is considered as an inhabitant, when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it."[^86]

Count the coinages on two leaves: artificial being, legal entity, creature of the law, faculty, legal name, ideal existence. Six words for one thing, and the Chief Justice using them as a writer tries phrases, not as a court fixes a term. The one that would carry everything afterward, "entity," is used here to deny the corporation a status, not to confer one. And the one sentence that states a rule rather than a picture is the last: the name is "considered as" a person "when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it." That is not a being. It is a rule of treatment, and it is the position this article takes.

The next citations fixed the figures in the order the sentence had offered them. In 1823, in *Society for the Propagation of the Gospel v. New Haven*, Justice Washington cited *Deveaux* for what "was stated by the Court, that a corporation, considered as a mere legal entity, is not a citizen," and applied the statement to alienage.[^87] "Entity" entered its second opinion as something the Court had stated, its denial of status intact. The year after, Marshall reused the figure himself, in *Osborn v. Bank of the United States*, to hold that every suit by the Bank arose under federal law: "This being can acquire no right, make no contract, bring no suit, which is not authorized by a law of the United States. It is not only itself the mere creature of a law, but all its actions and all its rights are dependant on the same law."[^88] The figure now did work, and did it without a citation; counsel in the same case had already quoted *Dartmouth*'s definition back to the Court as the Court's own.[^89] The road not taken was cited once, in 1844, and cited to close it. In *Louisville Railroad v. Letson* Justice Wayne took *Deveaux*'s sentence that "this ideal existence is considered as an inhabitant, when the general spirit and purposes of the law requires it," asked "If it be so for the purposes of taxation, why is it not so for the purposes of a suit in the Circuit Court of the United States, when the plaintiff has the proper residence?" and confessed the Court's "inability to reconcile these qualities of a corporation—residence, habitancy, and individuality, with the doctrine that a corporation aggregate cannot be a citizen for the purposes of a suit in the courts of the United States, unless in consequence of a residence of all the corporators being of the state in which the suit is brought."[^90] No opinion between 1809 and 1844 had cited the faculty, the legal name, or the ideal existence at all;[^91] the one that did made the rule of treatment a quality of the thing treated, and made the corporation a citizen. The confession is the proof. In the system of positive law Blackstone described, in which, as Section II.E quoted, not even a wrong may be defined without its redress, every incident of the body is the sovereign's positive grant, and a corporation's citizenship could come only from a rule that reconciled its qualities with the clause's word; the reconciliation was the grant's work, not the Court's. The Court said it could not perform it, and held the citizenship anyway: a holding by default, supplying what only positive law could supply. The default has a constitutional name. It is law made by its negative, a space filled because no rule forbade the filling, and the space is the one the Ninth Amendment keeps for the people, whose unenumerated rights the enumeration "shall not be construed to deny or disparage." A court that fills it for a name has made the construction the clause forbids, and the violation is concrete, measured by what the name received and the people lost. Section VIII.F returns to the clause with the test in hand.

The English books Marshall named say what he said they say. Blackstone's eighteenth chapter opens with the sentences Section II.F quoted, the law constituting "artificial persons" for a perpetual succession, and gives the reason: because "all personal rights die with the person," and "the necessary forms of investing a series of individuals, one after another, with the same identical rights, would be very inconvenient, if not impracticable."[^92] The rights are "granted"; the purpose is continuity; the body is a body politic; and the king's consent is, as Section II.F quoted, "absolutely necessary."[^93] The "artificial person" of the English books is a rule for succession under a grant, and nothing in Blackstone's chapter attributes to it any right against the grantor; it is a rule made for a king's grant, and incompatible with the instrumental governance of American constitutional democracy.
