### C. 1819: *Dartmouth College*'s Hedge, "If the Expression May Be Allowed," and the Citations That Dropped It

*Trustees of Dartmouth College v. Woodward* gave the vocabulary its canonical form, and the canonical form carries a hedge that its citations dropped. At page 636:

> A corporation is an artificial being, invisible, intangible, and existing only in contemplation of law. Being the mere creature of law, it possesses only those properties which the charter of its creation confers upon it, either expressly, or as incidental to its very existence. These are such as are supposed best calculated to effect the object for which it was created. Among the most important are immortality, and, if the expression may be allowed, individuality; properties, by which a perpetual succession of many persons are considered as the same, and may act as a single individual.[^94]

The first sentence is the concession theory. The second sentence contains the clause that lets the being back in: the corporation possesses what the charter confers "either expressly, or as incidental to its very existence." Properties that are "incidental to its very existence" are properties the charter did not name and that follow from the nature of the thing created. That is a thing with a nature, which is what the real-entity theory asserts and the concession theory denies. The sentence does its work by its scope. "Only" promises a limit, and the two branches that follow remove it: what the charter confers "expressly" is everything named, and what is "incidental to its very existence" is everything unnamed that attends the thing's being, so that the two together are the whole field, the positive grant and its negative, and nothing is left outside the word "only." A grant of that scope is the sovereign's own scope, a king's, who held every right in his person and could part with any of them; it is the scope the second presupposition requires (Section II.E), and it is why "only" limits nothing. "Supposed best calculated" then concedes that the properties so conferred are presumed, not shown. The unnamed half of the field is the unenumerated space of Section III.B's default: a definition named it, no grant filled it, and the Court filled it, until in 1978 Justice Rehnquist, dissenting, made the clause itself the measure of constitutional rights. In the same breath Marshall names the two incidents, "immortality" and "individuality," and hedges the second: "if the expression may be allowed." The pair spans the same field a second time. Immortality is the sovereign's attribute before it is anyone's: Blackstone's third attribute of the king's majesty is "his perpetuity," by which "the law ascribes to him, in his political capacity, an absolute immortality. The king never dies." Individuality is the attribute of the natural subject, whose "personal rights die with the person," the condition Section III.B quoted as Blackstone's reason for incorporating at all. The charter confers both on a body of men, the sovereign's attribute and the subject's, and the two together reach from one end of the field to the other, as "expressly" and "incidental" do. The hedge marks which end needs leave. Marshall states the sovereign's attribute without it, because perpetuity is what the grant is for, and asks leave for the subject's, because many are one person only by the law's allowance. In the system of positive law his definition describes, leave of that kind is the grantor's to give, and the hedge asks of the reader the one thing the being holds of its sovereign: allowance. Two sentences later he writes that "a perpetual succession of individuals are capable of acting for the promotion of the particular object, like one immortal being," and immediately qualifies: "But this being does not share in the civil government of the country, unless that be the purpose for which it was created."[^95]

The hedge is the finding. Marshall knew "individuality" was a figure and said so. He knew "immortal being" was a simile and wrote "like." The next hundred and fifty years of citation kept the being and lost the "if the expression may be allowed."[^96] When Justice Rehnquist quoted the passage in 1978 as the description of "the status of a corporation in the eyes of federal law," he quoted it one sentence past "incidental to its very existence" and stopped before the hedge, and then made that clause the test: "our inquiry must seek to determine which constitutional protections are 'incidental to its very existence.'"[^97] The clause Marshall wrote to describe charter powers became, in the hands of the Justice most sympathetic to the concession theory, the measure of constitutional rights. The hedge did not survive even its friends. Nor did it survive the lexicon. Webster's of 1828, nine years after *Dartmouth*, made the corporation its example under *immortal*, "A corporation is called an immortal being," and under *immortality* cited "J. Marshall" for "existence not limited": the simile became the definition, and what the citations carried forward was a being held in view, Johnson's "something appearing only to imagination," with the one incident Marshall asked leave for attached to it as a property.

It did not survive its author either. In 1830, in *Providence Bank v. Billings*, Marshall restated the hedged word as a plain property, with no citation and no qualification: "The great object of an incorporation is, to bestow the character and properties of individuality on a collective and changing body of men. This capacity is always given to such a body."[^98] The sentence that follows is the concession theory flat: "Any privileges which may exempt it from the burdens common to individuals, do not flow necessarily from the charter, but must be expressed in it, or they do not exist."[^99] The Court would later quote the first sentence beside its "person" holding and leave the sentence that follows where it was.[^100] The first opinion of the Court to cite page 636 for the definition came in 1839. Chief Justice Taney, in *Bank of Augusta v. Earle*: "In the case of *Dartmouth College v. Woodward*, 4 Wheat. 636, the same principle was again decided by the court," followed by the definition through "incidental to its very existence," and nothing after it.[^101] Decided. The passage Marshall wrote as a description, with a hedge two sentences on, is a decision twenty years later, quoted to the clause that lets the being in and stopped before the clause that lets it out. Rehnquist in 1978 ran one sentence further; neither reached the hedge.

### D. 1869: *Paul v. Virginia*, the Court Reading Its Own Text, as the Census's Control

One case in the sequence is not a coinage but its opposite, and it belongs in the census as the control. In *Paul v. Virginia* the question was whether a corporation was a "citizen" within the Privileges and Immunities Clause of Article IV. Justice Field for the Court:

> The term citizens as there used applies only to natural persons, members of the body politic, owing allegiance to the State, not to artificial persons created by the legislature, and possessing only the attributes which the legislature has prescribed.[^102]

This is the text read as written. "Citizens" means natural persons. Artificial persons have "only the attributes which the legislature has prescribed." No figure of speech is doing any work; the word "artificial" appears only to mark what the clause excludes. *Paul* is cited here because it shows the Court was able to read "citizen" without a coinage, in 1869, and because *Pembina* restated it in 1888 in the numbered paragraph before the one in which it reasoned the corporation into "person."[^103] The Court knew how to do this. It did it for one word and not for the other.

*Paul*'s words travelled as the others did, by restatement. The first opinion to carry them is Justice Field's own dissent in the *Slaughter-House Cases*, four years later, reciting what "the court answered" in *Paul*, "that the term citizens as there used applied only to natural persons, members of the body politic owing allegiance to the State, not to artificial persons created by the legislature and possessing only the attributes which the legislature had prescribed," and running on through the rest of the holding.[^104] The first opinion of the Court with the words is *Pembina*: "This was expressly held in *Paul v. Virginia*."[^105] The two opinions that cited *Paul* in between took its result without its sentence.[^106]

### E. 1886 to 1889: "Person," from a Remark Before Argument to a Holding by Citation in Three Years

The term that carries the modern law is "person," as used in the Fourteenth Amendment, and its entry into the Amendment's construction is the cleanest instance of the transplant Section III.A named: a word older than the Republic as a rule of treatment, carried into a grant of rights against the States by a remark.

*Santa Clara County v. Southern Pacific Railroad* was a tax case. The railroad's brief argued, among other points, that "Corporations are persons within the meaning of the Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution of the United States." The reporter's statement of the case records what happened before argument:

> Before argument MR. CHIEF JUSTICE WAITE said: The court does not wish to hear argument on the question whether the provision in the Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution, which forbids a State to deny to any person within its jurisdiction the equal protection of the laws, applies to these corporations. We are all of opinion that it does.[^107]

The syllabus repeated the proposition as the first headnote.[^108] The opinion of the Court, by Justice Harlan, did not. Of the constitutional questions it said: "These questions belong to a class which this court should not decide, unless their determination is essential to the disposal of the case in which they arise."[^109] It then decided the case on a ground of California law, that the state board had assessed fences it had no jurisdiction to assess, and concluded: "As the judgment can be sustained upon this ground it is not necessary to consider any other questions raised by the pleadings and the facts found by the court."[^110] Nothing was held. A remark was made from the bench, a reporter recorded it, and a headnote stated it. What the remark did is measured by what it carried and by how. The Equal Protection Clause is a positive grant in the people's instrument, written for persons against the States, and the remark carried the name into it. It did so outside the process by which Article III's judicial power acts, which is the decision of a case upon argument: the Court declined the argument, decided the case on another ground, and left the proposition standing in a reporter's note. From that note the citations that follow extended the clause's protection to the name, and the later sections of this Part follow the rest of what natural persons hold (Section IV.A) as it was extended in turn, until the name had the scope Section III.C read in the king's grant, the enumerated protection and the unenumerated remainder behind it. The people's own words refute both halves. The Ninth Amendment reserves the remainder to "the people," who are natural persons (Section VIII.F); and the principle Justice Iredell stated in 1793 (Section III.B), that a State draws its authority from "the voluntary and deliberate choice of the people" while a corporation is "a mere creature" of the power that made it, is the spirit of Independence in the Reports' own words, and it leaves no room for what a grant made to hold against its makers what they reserved to themselves. The extension was made outside the Article III process and against the Ninth Amendment, and it has never had more behind it than the remark.

Two years later, in *Pembina Consolidated Silver Mining Co. v. Pennsylvania*, the Court gave the proposition a reason for the first and only time:

> Under the designation of person there is no doubt that a private corporation is included. Such corporations are merely associations of individuals united for a special purpose, and permitted to do business under a particular name, and have a succession of members without dissolution. As said by Chief Justice Marshall, "The great object of a corporation is to bestow the character and properties of individuality on a collective and changing body of men."[^111]

The reason is the aggregate theory: the corporation is "merely associations of individuals." But the same sentence names the sovereign's permission, "permitted to do business under a particular name," and the incident the rule confers, "a succession of members without dissolution." The only argument ever made for the corporate "person" reaches, by its own terms, the members' rights carried through a permitted name, and nothing of the name's own. Sections IV and V take up what follows from that.

Two steps between the remark and the holding complete the record. The remark's first citation anywhere in the Reports is a dissent, in the term after it was made. In *Philadelphia Fire Association v. New York*, in the October Term of 1886, Justice Harlan quoted what "the Chief Justice observed" before argument in *Santa Clara* and extended it: a corporation of one State doing business in another "is to be deemed, at least in respect to that business, a 'person' within the jurisdiction of the latter State, in the meaning of the Fourteenth Amendment."[^112] *Pembina*'s own page does not cite *Santa Clara*; it says "there is no doubt" and cites Marshall's sentence of 1830.[^113] In the same term, in *Missouri Pacific Railway v. Mackey*, the proposition had become something the parties agreed on: "It is conceded that corporations are persons within the meaning of the amendment," with *Santa Clara* and *Pembina* cited and nothing said.[^114]

The year after, in *Minneapolis & St. Louis Railway v. Beckwith*, the proposition became a holding by citation:

> It is contended by counsel as the basis of his argument, and we admit the soundness of his position, that corporations are persons within the meaning of the clause in question. It was so held in *Santa Clara County v. Southern Pacific Railroad Co.*, 118 U.S. 394, 396, and the doctrine was reasserted in *Pembina Mining Company v. Pennsylvania*, 125 U.S. 181, 189.[^115]

"It was so held," citing page 396. Page 396 is the reporter's note of what the Chief Justice said before argument, in a case whose opinion declined the question. From 1889 the proposition has been "so held," and every later case cites the chain.[^116] Justice Rehnquist described the sequence accurately ninety years later: "This Court decided at an early date, with neither argument nor discussion, that a business corporation is a 'person' entitled to the protection of the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment."[^117] Neither argument nor discussion. A remark, a headnote, a dissent that quotes the remark, a reason that proves less than it is cited for, a concession, and a citation that calls the remark a holding. Not one page in the chain argues the proposition.

The census was run from the bench twice before this article ran it, by the two Justices who would have overruled the line, and both ran it on the text. Justice Black, dissenting alone in *Connecticut General Life Insurance Co. v. Johnson* in 1938, began with the word: "I do not believe the word 'person' in the Fourteenth Amendment includes corporations," and "Neither the history nor the language of the Fourteenth Amendment justifies the belief that corporations are included within its protection."[^118] He read § 1 clause by clause. Its first sentence makes "all persons born or naturalized" citizens, and "Certainly a corporation cannot be naturalized and 'persons' here is not broad enough to include 'corporations.'"[^119] Its second sentence names "citizens" in its first clause, and "efforts have been made to persuade this Court to allow corporations to claim the protection of this clause," without success.[^120] Of the next clause: "It has not been decided that this clause prohibits a state from depriving a corporation of 'life,'" the Court "has expressly held"[^121] that the liberty it guarantees "is the liberty of natural, not artificial persons," and so "the words 'life' and 'liberty' do not apply to corporations, and of course they could not have been so intended to apply"; the clause as the majority's cases construe it reads, in Black's rewriting of it, "Nor shall any State deprive any human being of life, liberty or property without due process of law; nor shall any State deprive any corporation of property without due process of law."[^122] The Amendment's second section counts "the whole number of persons in each State," its third bars any "person" who "engaged in insurrection" from Congress, and "No citizen could gather the impression here that while the word 'persons' in the second section applied to human beings, the word 'persons' in the first section in some instances applied to corporations."[^123]

The history he found where this section found it: the *Slaughter-House* Court, five years after ratification, "had apparently discovered no such purpose," and "The records of the time can be searched in vain for evidence that this Amendment was adopted for the benefit of corporations."[^124] The one record ever offered was offered in 1882, when "an argument was made in this Court that a journal of the joint Congressional Committee which framed the Amendment, secret and undisclosed up to that date, indicated the Committee's desire to protect corporations by the use of the word 'person,'" and Black's answer makes notice the measure: "A secret purpose on the part of the members of the Committee, even if such be the fact, however, would not be sufficient to justify any such construction," because "No section of the Amendment gave notice to the people that, if adopted, it would subject every state law and municipal ordinance, affecting corporations, (and all administrative actions under them) to censorship of the United States courts."[^125] Even he called the remark a decision, *Santa Clara* having "decided for the first time that the word 'person' in the Amendment did in some instances include corporations"; the dissenter who would undo the chain took its first link as the chain described it.[^126] His remedy is Section VII's: "If the people of this nation wish to deprive the States of their sovereign rights to determine what is a fair and just tax upon corporations doing a purely local business within their own state boundaries, there is a way provided by the Constitution to accomplish this purpose. That way does not lie along the course of judicial amendment to that fundamental charter."[^127]

Eleven years later Justice Douglas, joined by Justice Black, ran the census again in *Wheeling Steel Corp. v. Glander*, and began where this section began: "It has been implicit in all of our decisions since 1886 that a corporation is a 'person' within the meaning of the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. *Santa Clara County v. Southern Pac. R. Co.*, 118 U. S. 394, 396, so held. The Court was cryptic in its decision. It was so sure of its ground that it wrote no opinion on the point," and then the Chief Justice's words from the bench, set out entire, and the verdict on them: "There was no history, logic, or reason given to support that view. Nor was the result so obvious that exposition was unnecessary."[^128] He added a reader this section had not reached, the one judge to construe the word before the remark was made. In 1871, three years after ratification, Justice Woods on circuit "held that 'person' as there used did not include a corporation," with "the history of the submission by congress, and the adoption by the states of the 14th amendment, so fresh in all minds as to need no rehearsal."[^129]

Then he set out § 1 with its four nouns in italics and read them in order. "'Persons' in the first sentence plainly includes only human beings, for corporations are not 'born or naturalized.'" Corporations "are not 'citizens'" under the second sentence's first clause. "It has never been held that they are persons whom a State may not deprive of 'life.'" "'Liberty' in that clause is 'the liberty of natural, not artificial, persons.'" "But 'property' as used in that clause has been held to include that of a corporation since 1889 when *Minneapolis & St. L. R. Co. v. Beckwith*, 129 U. S. 26, was decided."[^130] 1889 and *Beckwith*: the year and the case at which this section's chain arrived at "It was so held." What followed is the sentence this census's third category restates: "It requires distortion to read 'person' as meaning one thing, then another within the same clause and from clause to clause. It means, in my opinion, a substantial revision of the Fourteenth Amendment."[^131] The construction he preferred was Woods's, and Woods's sentence meets the objection that the Amendment's framers chose "person" beside "citizens" for its breadth, because it says what the wider word reaches: "the persons to whom the equal protection of the law is secured are persons born or naturalized or endowed with life and liberty, and consequently natural and not artificial persons."[^132] The breadth is real, and it runs in one direction. "Person" reaches past "citizens" to the natural person who was not born or naturalized here, who is endowed with life and liberty and can be deprived of both; it does not reach a name, which is neither born nor naturalized, has no life to lose and no liberty, as the Court had "expressly held" in the line that the note to Black's dissent sets beside its contrary, the Reports holding both lines and once, in *Hague*, both in one opinion, and holds property only as the rule of Part One lets a name hold it. "History has gone the other way," Douglas conceded, and "now that the question is squarely presented I can only conclude that the *Santa Clara* case was wrong and should be overruled."[^133] His last paragraph is Section VII's premise in seven sentences: "We are dealing with a question of vital concern to the people of the nation. It may be most desirable to give corporations this protection from the operation of the legislative process. But that question is not for us. It is for the people. If they want corporations to be treated as humans are treated, if they want to grant corporations this large degree of emancipation from state regulation, they should say so. The Constitution provides a method by which they may do so. We should not do it for them through the guise of interpretation."[^134]

The Court's answer came in the same case, and it was not a reading of the word. Justice Jackson, who wrote for the Court in *Wheeling Steel*, added a separate opinion "to complete the record by pointing out why, in writing by assignment for the Court, he assumed without discussion that the protections of the Fourteenth Amendment are available to a corporation."[^135] The reasons were three. The State had not questioned it. "It has consistently been held by this Court that the Fourteenth Amendment assures corporations equal protection of the laws, at least since 1886, *Santa Clara County v. Southern Pacific R. Co.*, 118 U. S. 394, 396, and that it entitles them to due process of law, at least since 1889, *Minneapolis & St. L. R. Co. v. Beckwith*, 129 U. S. 26, 28." The two pages he cited are the two this section began and ended with: 396, the reporter's note of what the Chief Justice said before argument, and 28, "It was so held." And the dissenters had themselves written for the Court in cases where corporations claimed the Amendment's protection, and "it was not intimated therein that there was even doubt whether the corporations had standing to raise the questions or were entitled to protection of the Amendment."[^136] Assumed without discussion in 1949, by the Court's own author, because it had been so held since 1886 and 1889: the chain this section traced, cited as its own justification, and the two Justices who asked for the argument answered with the term's age and their own votes. The transplant has never been argued for by the Court. It was assumed in 1886 and assumed again in 1949, and on both occasions the absence of discussion is the Court's own description. What the removal test does with a sense that was never argued for, and why the question whether the Amendment's own structure bears that sense is the ordinary question of interpretation and not the test's, is Section VII.C's business; this section's work was to show that the Court asked neither question.

### F. 1907 and 1923: "Creature of the State," Kept for the Municipal Class Alone

The concession vocabulary did not disappear after *Santa Clara*. It was confined to a class. For municipal corporations the Court continued to say what Marshall and Blackstone had said of all corporations. In *Hunter v. Pittsburgh*: "Municipal corporations are political subdivisions of the State, created as convenient agencies for exercising such of the governmental powers of the State as may be entrusted to them," and their inhabitants "have no right by contract or otherwise in the unaltered or continued existence of the corporation or its powers, and there is nothing in the Federal Constitution which protects them from these injurious consequences. The power is in the State."[^137] In *Trenton v. New Jersey*: "A municipality is merely a department of the State, and the State may withhold, grant or withdraw powers and privileges as it sees fit. However great or small its sphere of action, it remains the creature of the State exercising and holding powers and privileges subject to the sovereign will," and the city "cannot invoke these provisions of the Federal Constitution against" the State.[^138]

The census records these not because the article adopts "creature," which it does not, but because the Court applied one vocabulary to the body politic below the State and another to the private corporation, and the only stated reason for the difference is *Pembina*'s: the private corporation has members with property. That reason is the derivative theory, and it reaches the members' property and nothing else.

The word itself travelled slowly.[^139] Justice Cardozo restated the rule in 1933 with the correlative noun: a municipal corporation "has no privileges or immunities under the federal constitution which it may invoke in opposition to the will of its creator," and cited six cases for it.[^140] Chief Justice Hughes in 1939 put the word and the citation together for the first time in an opinion of the Court, and used them for standing: "Being but creatures of the State, municipal corporations have no standing to invoke the contract clause or the provisions of the Fourteenth Amendment of the Constitution in opposition to the will of their creator."[^141] Sixteen years from the word to its first citation with the word, and the use was to close the courthouse to the body politic below the State while the private corporation stood inside it; the word's later life outside the municipal class is a figure for any created body and for nothing in particular.[^142]

### G. 1958: "Medium," the One Word That Supports No Corporate Right

One word in the sequence supports no corporate right at all, and it was the word the Court chose when it was looking carefully at what an association is. In *NAACP v. Alabama* the State argued that the Association lacked standing to assert its members' rights. The Court rejected the argument:

> Petitioner is the appropriate party to assert these rights, because it and its members are in every practical sense identical. The Association, which provides in its constitution that "[a]ny person who is in accordance with [its] principles and policies …" may become a member, is but the medium through which its individual members seek to make more effective the expression of their own views.[^143]

The Association was itself a nonprofit membership corporation,[^144] and the Court located the right in the members' own liberty under the Fourteenth Amendment[^145] and let the name assert it only because requiring the members to come forward would nullify the right at the moment of its assertion;[^146] Section IV.B tells the case. A medium is a conduit. It holds nothing. The one time the Court described a corporation with care, it described a rule of treatment and not a being.

The sentence was next cited five years later, in *NAACP v. Button*, as what the Court had said: "compare *NAACP v. Alabama ex rel. Patterson*, 357 U.S. 449, 459, where we said," and then the sentence entire, to deny any conflict between the Association's interests and its members'.[^147] Justice Douglas, concurring, quoted it the same way.[^148] The word was assumed in its next citation as every other word in this census was. The difference is in what was assumed. A conduit was assumed, and a conduit can be given no right of its own.

### H. 1978: "Corporate Democracy," a Presumption in Modal Verbs, Applied in 2010 as a Fact

The last term before *Citizens United* is the one that answers the dissenting shareholder, and it entered in a sentence whose every verb is modal. Justice Powell for the Court in *Bellotti*: "Ultimately shareholders may decide, through the procedures of corporate democracy, whether their corporation should engage in debate on public issues."[^149] The next page presumed the shareholders competent and gave them access to a derivative suit, in the same mood;[^150] the term describes a presumption, and the opinion calls it one. Its next appearance, thirty-two years later, is its elevation: the majority in *Citizens United* rejected the shareholder-protection interest because abuses "can be corrected by shareholders 'through the procedures of corporate democracy,'" with the quotation marks and the citation to *Bellotti* and nothing further,[^151] and no opinion between the two uses the phrase.[^152] The only opinion ever to ask what the words mean is the dissent in the case that elevated them, which answered from the literature that the shareholders' rights "are so limited as to be almost nonexistent."[^153] A term whose meaning is first asked after thirty-two years, by a dissenter, and answered from a law review, is a coinage and not a term of art. Section VI.D measures what the presumption presumes.

### I. 2010: "Associations of Citizens," Invoked for the Treasury and Denied for the PAC in One Opinion

The newest coinage is the oldest theory under a new name. The majority in *Citizens United* wrote that § 441b "permits the Government to ban the political speech of millions of associations of citizens," and that "certain disfavored associations of citizens—those that have taken on the corporate form—are penalized for engaging in the same political speech."[^154] This is *Pembina*'s "merely associations of individuals," the aggregate theory, invoked to make the corporation's speech the citizens' speech. Seventeen pages earlier the same opinion had held that the citizens' own association, the PAC, could not carry the corporation's speech, because "[a] PAC is a separate association from the corporation."[^155] The corporation is an association of citizens when the question is whether it may speak, and a separate thing from the association of its citizens when the question is whether their speaking suffices. The two sentences cannot both describe one object. They describe a word used two ways in one opinion. Section V.D supplies the measure the word lacks: speech through a sanctioned body is its members' own act only where the charter or the terms of membership contain that speech, and the business treasury's charter contains none.

The phrase first appears five pages earlier than the two the article has quoted, in the sentence that frames the holding: "If the First Amendment has any force, it prohibits Congress from fining or jailing citizens, or associations of citizens, for simply engaging in political speech."[^156] The next sentence names the thing the phrase stands for, "an association that has taken on the corporate form."[^157] That is the term's whole life in the opinions of the Court. No opinion of the Court through the last volume on the shelf uses the phrase again. Its one citation is Justice Breyer's, dissenting in *Holder v. Humanitarian Law Project* five months later, in a string of parentheticals for the protection of political speech.[^158] The newest term in the census has been cited once, in dissent, as settled.

### J. The Table: The Census in One View, and the Five Things It Shows

The two columns a grid cannot hold, the next citation of each term and what was afterwards hung on it, follow entry by entry in the order of the table.

**"artificial being," "mere legal entity," "mere creature of the law."** *Next citation:* *Society v. New Haven*, 8 Wheat. at 491 (1823), "it was stated by the Court," assumed; Marshall's own reuse, *Osborn*, 9 Wheat. at 823 (1824), argued, no cite; the "ideal existence" turned, *Letson*, 2 How. at 559 (1844), argued against; *Dartmouth* (1819) within the lineage. *What was hung on it:* The whole vocabulary; the road not taken is this article's position.

**"artificial being … incidental to its very existence … if the expression may be allowed, individuality … like one immortal being."** *Next citation:* The author's restatement without the hedge, *Providence Bank*, 4 Pet. at 562 (1830), assumed; first cited as "decided," *Bank of Augusta*, 13 Pet. at 587 (1839), stopped before the hedge; Rehnquist, *Bellotti*, 435 U.S. at 823–824 (1978), makes "incidental to its very existence" the test for constitutional rights. *What was hung on it:* the incidents; the Contract Clause holding rests on the charter as the trustees' contract (*Fletcher* \*137), not on the being.

**"natural persons … not artificial persons created by the legislature."** *Next citation:* Field dissenting, *Slaughter-House Cases*, 16 Wall. at 99 (1873), assumed; first opinion of the Court, *Pembina*, 125 U.S. at 187–188 (1888), "expressly held." *What was hung on it:* "Citizen" confined to natural persons.

**"citizen" (Article III; Article IV, § 2; the Fourteenth Amendment, § 1): the clause the Court kept closed.** *Next citation:* *Sullivan*, 19 U.S. at 451–452 (1821); *Breithaupt*, 26 U.S. at 240 (1828); *Slocomb*, 39 U.S. at 64 (1840), "the artificial being, a corporation aggregate, was not a citizen, as such"; the one exception, *Letson*, 43 U.S. at 558 (1844), "capable of being treated as a citizen of that state, as much as a natural person"; the presumption put in its place, *Marshall v. Baltimore & Ohio*, 57 U.S. at 327–328 (1854); *Wheeler*, 66 U.S. at 295–296 (1861); *Paul*, 75 U.S. at 177 (1869); *Blake v. McClung*, 172 U.S. at 259 (1898), "we now hold"; *Western Turf*, 204 U.S. at 363 (1907); *Hague*, 307 U.S. at 514 (1939) (Roberts, J., joined by Black, J.); *Asbury Hospital*, 326 U.S. at 210–211 (1945); 28 U.S.C. § 1332(c)(1) (1958), the statutory word supplied by Congress; *Carden*, 494 U.S. at 197 (1990), the rest left to Congress.[^159] *What was hung on it:* Nothing that lasted. The name was spoken of as "capable of being treated as a citizen" once, in *Letson*, and from 1854 the result rested on a presumption about the persons, the word where still spoken naming the presumption's effect "for such purposes" (*Blake* 259); for the rest of one hundred and eighty years the name was kept out of the one word that is the settled bond's own (Section VI.H), by reading the word and by looking through the name to the persons, and when the word was wanted in terms Congress wrote it.

**"person" (Fourteenth Amendment).** *Next citation:* Harlan dissenting, *Philadelphia Fire Ass'n*, 119 U.S. at 120 (Nov. 1886), the remark quoted and extended; *Pembina*, 125 U.S. at 189 (1888), "there is no doubt," the aggregate reason, *Santa Clara* not cited; *Mackey*, 127 U.S. at 209–210 (1888), "It is conceded"; *Beckwith*, 129 U.S. at 28 (1889), "It was so held"; argued against, in dissent only, Black, J., *Connecticut General*, 303 U.S. at 85–90 (1938), and Douglas, J., *Wheeling Steel*, 337 U.S. at 576–581 (1949), answered by Jackson, J., at 574–575 with the term's age. *What was hung on it:* The Fourteenth Amendment line entire, and within it the liberty line, told once in Section IV.B and printed here whole: liberty withheld from the name, *Riggs*, 203 U.S. at 255 (1906), and *Western Turf*, 204 U.S. at 363 (1907); the rule applied against persons through the name, *Berea College*, 211 U.S. at 54 (1908), where it was used to segregate a college because the Court held only half of it; the two sets stated, *Pierce*, 268 U.S. at 535 (1925); the transplant's step into the First Amendment, *Grosjean*, 297 U.S. at 244 (1936), by citation of two rate cases; the line restated, *Hague*, 307 U.S. at 527 (1939) (Stone, J., joined by Reed, J.); the persons' rights through the medium, *NAACP v. Alabama*, 357 U.S. at 459 (1958); the description, *Button*, 371 U.S. at 428 (1963); the description cited against the line, *Bellotti*, 435 U.S. at 780 n. 16 (1978); and for the treasury, *Citizens United*, 558 U.S. at 342 (2010).[^160]

**"creature of the State" (municipal).** *Next citation:* None with the word for ten years; *Williams v. Mayor*, 289 U.S. at 40 (1933), "the will of its creator," assumed; *Coleman v. Miller*, 307 U.S. at 441 & n. 6 (1939), "Being but creatures of the State," for standing. *What was hung on it:* No federal right against the creator.

**"the same protection of equal laws that natural persons … have a right to demand" (taxation of the privilege).** *Next citation:* *Lehnhausen*, 410 U.S. at 359–360, 365 (1973) (Douglas, J., for a unanimous Court), the clause applied with "great leeway" between corporations and individuals and *Quaker City Cab*'s equality "only a relic of a bygone era"; *Metropolitan Life*, 470 U.S. at 882 (1985), the clause applied between two names with no person on either side.[^161] *What was hung on it:* The name's equality with natural persons against a heavier charge on the privilege, asserted in 1928 by citation of the "person" entry; abandoned in 1973 for "great leeway," the clause applied and the claim failing; the clause applied between names in 1985: the measure of a name's protection moving with the Court as no person's does (Section VI.I).

**"medium."** *Next citation:* *NAACP v. Button*, 371 U.S. at 443 (1963), "where we said," assumed; Douglas, J., concurring, at 446. *What was hung on it:* No corporate right; the members' rights carried through.

**"corporate democracy."** *Next citation:* None, 1978–2009; *Citizens United*, 558 U.S. at 362, 370 (2010), as *Bellotti*'s holding; Stevens, J., dissenting, at 477, the only opinion to ask what it means. *What was hung on it:* The rejection of the shareholder-protection interest.

**"associations of citizens."** *Next citation:* None by the Court through 592 U.S.; Breyer dissenting, *Holder*, 561 U.S. at 43 (2010), quoting 349. *What was hung on it:* Treasury speech as the citizens' speech.

Five things the table shows that the prose has stated piecemeal. First, the hedges are all at the beginning. Marshall assumed in 1809 and hedged in 1819; nobody hedged afterward, because the next citation always dropped the qualification and kept the noun. Second, the next citation is the step. In every entry the term's second appearance is a restatement, "it was stated by the Court," "the same principle was again decided," "This was expressly held," "there is no doubt," "It is conceded," "where we said," and a restatement carries the noun and not the qualification. The only citations that argue a term are the two that turned it to a new use, *Letson* in 1844 and Justice Stevens in 2010; the two that argued against one, Black in 1938 and Douglas in 1949, were dissents, and were answered with the term's age; and two of the terms were first carried forward by dissents, "person" by Harlan in 1886 and "associations of citizens" by Breyer in 2010, the last with no later citation by the Court at all. Third, the only entries that were argued from the text, *Paul*, *NAACP* and the citizenship entry that *Paul* belongs to, are the ones that confer nothing on the name. Where the Court read its words it found natural persons and a medium. Where it found a holder, it found one by a figure of speech, or by a word carried in from the law of corporations with its meaning left behind, and the figure, or the word, was afterwards cited as the holding. Fourth, the two entries added to the census here test the rest from the other side: the clause the Court kept closed, it kept closed by reading the word and looking through the name to the persons, as Section III.K records, and on the tax side the name's protection moved with the Court as no person's does, as the entry records and Section VI.I measures. What was carried into one clause and kept out of the next, and asserted for a third and abandoned, was carried each time by a citation and not by the Amendment's words, and the test of Section VII asks, clause by clause, what stands under the citation. Fifth, the limiting word is the grant's cover. In the sentence that confers the right on the name, the entries find in the same breath a word that denies the name, "mere," "only," "although," "artificial," "for such purposes," and Hale's sentence in Section VII.C adds "but an association" and "assumed"; the denial is what let the grant pass: it reads as a limit, and the grant is the whole. Where the limiting word was held to its meaning, as in *Deveaux*, *Paul* and *Blake*, the holding reached the persons or the grant's incidents; where it covered the grant, as in *Letson* and *Hale*, the holding is the name's own. What is left when the figure is removed is the question of Section VII.

### K. The Control the Table Found: "Citizen," the Clause the Court Kept Closed to the Name for One Hundred and Eighty Years by Reading the Word and Looking Through to the Persons

The table has one entry the census did not set out to find, and it is the control the other entries wanted. The Court was asked from the beginning whether a corporation is a citizen, under Article III's "controversies between citizens of different States," under Article IV's "citizens of each State," and under the Fourteenth Amendment's "citizens of the United States," and for one hundred and eighty years it answered no, of every clause but one, and of that one for ten years. The first answer is *Deveaux*'s, "certainly not a citizen," and the reporter's note of *Hope Insurance Co. v. Boardman*, the companion case argued with it (the pin in note 159), records the Court as holding "that a body corporate as such cannot be a citizen, within the meaning of the constitution." The exception is *Letson* in 1844, which Section III.B has read: the Court there held a corporation "to be deemed to all intents and purposes as a person, although an artificial person, an inhabitant of the same state, for the purposes of its incorporation, capable of being treated as a citizen of that state, as much as a natural person," and "substantially, within the meaning of the law, a citizen of the state which created it," for the purposes of suing and being sued.[^162] Ten years later the Court rested the same jurisdiction on the persons instead. In 1854, when it settled the diversity question by presumption, Grier, J., for the Court, allowed the premise and its conclusion, and refused to let the conclusion decide the case: the corporation "is an artificial person, a mere legal entity, invisible and intangible," and "This is no doubt metaphysically true in a certain sense. The inference, also, that such an artificial entity 'cannot be a citizen' is a logical conclusion from the premises which cannot be denied"; but the citizen who sued it "did not deal with a mere metaphysical abstraction, but with natural persons," and "State laws, by combining large masses of men under a corporate name, cannot repeal the Constitution."[^163] From 1854 the jurisdiction rested on a presumption about the persons, that the members of a corporation are citizens of the chartering State, which *Blake* in 1898 would state in its settled form, the members "are to be conclusively presumed to be citizens of the State creating such corporation," adding that "therefore it has been said that a corporation is to be deemed, for such purposes, a citizen": where the word was still spoken, it named the presumption's effect, for those purposes, and nothing more. Seven years later Taney, C.J., for the Court, restated both halves: a corporation "exists only in contemplation of law, and by force of law; and where that law ceases to operate, the corporation can have no existence. It must dwell in the place of its creation," and, restating *Deveaux*, "a corporation is not a citizen, within the meaning of the Constitution of the United States."[^164] *Paul* in 1869 is the entry's Article IV statement, quoted in Section III.D; *Blake v. McClung* in 1898 made it a holding in terms, "we now hold, that a corporation is not a citizen within the meaning of the constitutional provision," in the same paragraph that called the corporation a "person" under the Fourteenth Amendment on the authority of *Santa Clara*'s page 396; *Western Turf* in 1907 and *Hague* in 1939 said it of the Fourteenth Amendment's own clause, the privileges and immunities of "citizens of the United States," Roberts, J., in *Hague* writing, with Black, J., that "Natural persons, and they alone, are entitled to the privileges and immunities which § 1 of the Fourteenth Amendment secures for 'citizens of the United States'"; *Asbury Hospital* in 1945 restated it for both clauses together.[^165]

The entry closes with the statutory word, and it was the legislature that spoke it. In 1958 Congress wrote that a corporation "shall be deemed a citizen of any State by which it has been incorporated and of the State where it has its principal place of business," the diversity statute's own rule of treatment, enacted by the people's legislature for the people's courts as Section V.A says the Dictionary Act was enacted for the people's statutes.[^166] In 1990, asked whether a limited partnership takes its citizenship from all its partners, Scalia, J., for the Court, answered that the question was "more readily resolved by legislative prescription than by legal reasoning," that such accommodation "is not only performed more legitimately by Congress than by courts, but it is performed more intelligently by legislation than by interpretation of the statutory word 'citizen'," and that "having established special treatment for corporations, we will leave the rest to Congress."[^167]

Two things about the entry bear on the rest of the table. The first is how the Court kept the clause closed. It read the word, as *Paul* and *Blake* did, and found natural persons; and where a result for the persons was wanted, as in the diversity cases, it looked through the name to the persons and presumed their citizenship, as *Deveaux*, *Marshall* and *Wheeler* did, which is the rule of Part One and of Section IV.B and not a coinage; *Letson*'s decade is the exception, and the Court itself ended it. The second is what the word is. Citizenship is the settled bond's own word, as Section VI.H shows: the Fourteenth Amendment's first sentence makes citizens of "all persons born or naturalized," and the four voting amendments each say "citizens." A name is born of a grant and naturalized by nothing, and for one hundred and eighty years, *Letson*'s decade excepted, the Court said so whenever the word was put to it, in the same years in which it moved the name into "person" by a remark and into "liberty" by a citation. The census shows the transplant stopping at one word, and the word it stopped at is the one that names the people.

### L. The Transplant in One View: The Reports' Phrase Beside the King's, and the Checks That Stayed Behind

The census table reads the terms in the order of their entry. The table below reads the same terms against their source. Each phrase on which the Reports built the being has an English original, and the original was said of the king's body politic, the second of his two bodies (Section II.E), or of the corporation as the king's grant (Section II.F). The left column is the American phrase as the Court wrote it, with its page; the right is the English phrase as Plowden, Coke and Blackstone wrote it, with its leaf; every quotation stands in the section named, where its page is read.

| The Reports' phrase, with its page | The English books' phrase, said of the king's body politic or the king's grant, with its leaf | Read at Section |
|---|---|---|
| "invisible, intangible" (*Deveaux*, 9 U.S. at 86, 88 (1809); *Dartmouth*, 17 U.S. at 636 (1819)) | "a body that cannot be seen or handled" (*Duchy of Lancaster*, 1 Plowd. 213, 75 Eng. Rep. 326 (1562)); "invisible" (*Sutton's Hospital*, 10 Co. Rep. 32b, 77 Eng. Rep. 973 (1612)) | II.E, III.B |
| "immortality"; "like one immortal being" (*Dartmouth*, 17 U.S. at 636) | "never dies" (*Wroth's Case*, 2 Plowd. 457, 75 Eng. Rep. 685 (1573)); "immortal" (10 Co. Rep. 32b); "an absolute immortality. The king never dies" (1 Bl. Comm. 242 (1765)) | II.E, III.C |
| "existing only in contemplation of law" (*Dartmouth*, 17 U.S. at 636) | "consisting of policy and government" (1 Plowd. 213); "rests only in intendment and consideration of the law" (10 Co. Rep. 32b) | II.E, III.B |
| "artificial being"; "artificial persons created by the legislature" (*Deveaux*, 9 U.S. at 86; *Dartmouth*, 17 U.S. at 636; *Paul v. Virginia*, 75 U.S. at 177 (1869)) | "artificial persons, who may maintain a perpetual succession, and enjoy a kind of legal immortality" (1 Bl. Comm. 455); "natural persons, or artificial" (1 Bl. Comm. 119) | II.F, III.A, III.D |
| "mere creature of the law" (*Deveaux*, 9 U.S. at 88, "derived entirely from the English books") | artificial persons "created and devised by human laws" (1 Bl. Comm. 119); the king's act, "*creamus, erigimus, fundamus, incorporamus*" (1 Bl. Comm. 460–461) | II.F, III.B |
| "either expressly, or as incidental to its very existence" (*Dartmouth*, 17 U.S. at 636) | the prerogative runs "unless where the constitution hath expressly, or by evident consequence, laid down some exception or boundary" (1 Bl. Comm. 243) | III.A, III.C |
| "to be deemed to all intents and purposes as a person" (*Letson*, 2 How. at 558 (1844)) | the heir, at the demise, "king to all intents and purposes" (1 Bl. Comm. 242) | III.A |

Read across, the rows are a recitation. The Court described the corporation in the words the English books had used for the king's political body and for the king's grant, and the description came without argument, as Sections III.B and III.C record, because the words arrived looking settled. Read down the right column, the rows are also an inventory of what did not come over. The same books that gave the body politic its perpetuity and its scope kept four checks on the king's corporation, and none of them has a row on the left: the king was "the visitor of all civil corporations" (1 Bl. Comm. 469); his grant was construed by its words, a dignity by patent enuring "only to the grantee for life" without "words to direct the inheritance" (388–389); he could be petitioned and could answer (Section II.E); and the body he made was a "system subordinate," bound to "acting up to the end or design, whatever it be, for which they were created by their founder," and regranted by the Crown when it could no longer perform its functions (467; *Rex v. Pasmore*, 3 T.R. 199, 242–243 (K.B. 1789); Section II.F). The transplant carried the sovereign's attributes and left the sovereign's remedies behind, which is what Section III.A named and the test of Section VII measures.

## IV. The Two Sets: What Natural Persons Already Hold, What Incorporation Alone Confers, and Why There Is No Third

The census has shown what the terms are. This section supplies the measure the rest of Part Two applies: two sets, the rights the members already hold and carry through the name, and the incidents the grant alone confers; and it shows why there is no third. Whatever a court has called a corporation's own right must be found in one set or the other, or in nothing.

### A. The Division: The Members' Own Rights Carried Through the Name, and the Incidents of the Grant

Everything a corporation might claim as a constitutional right falls into one of two sets, and the sets do not overlap.

The first set is what natural persons hold in full before any charter exists. Assembly, which the First Amendment secures to "the people," and association, which the Court derived from it. Speech. Property. The "liberty" the Fourteenth Amendment protects against the States. The unenumerated rights the Ninth Amendment reserves. Every member of every corporation holds all of these as a natural person, and held them the day before the charter issued. Incorporation cannot add to this set. There is nothing to add. What incorporation can do with these rights is carry them: the members may assert them together, under one name, and the name is what the Court called the NAACP, "but the medium through which its individual members seek to make more effective the expression of their own views."[^168] A medium is a conduit. It holds nothing of its own, and it carries nothing that was not put into it.

The second set is what incorporation alone confers. Limited liability. Perpetual succession. The capacity to hold, to contract, to sue and be sued under a common name. These are the incidents of the rule, and none of them is a constitutional right. Each is a sovereign's grant, each binds strangers to the agreement that sought it, and each remains the grantor's to condition or withdraw.

There is no third set. A right that is neither a member's own right carried through the name nor an incident of the rule has no source. "Corporate constitutional rights," as a phrase, therefore names one of two things: a natural person's retained right, which incorporation did not give, or an incident of the rule, which the Constitution does not protect as a right of the name. Neither is a right the corporation holds as itself.

### B. The First Set, from the Court's Own Case: *NAACP v. Alabama* and the Incorporated Body Called "But the Medium"

The division is not a construction imposed on the cases from outside. It is what the Court found when it looked, in the one case in which it looked carefully.

The NAACP in *NAACP v. Alabama* was "a nonprofit membership corporation organized under the laws of New York."[^169] Alabama had ordered it to produce its membership lists, and it refused on the ground that disclosure would expose its members to reprisal. The State argued that the Association had no standing to assert rights that belonged to the members, who were not parties. The Court agreed that the rights were the members' and allowed the Association to assert them anyway, because "it and its members are in every practical sense identical," because the Association "is but the medium" for the members' expression, and because requiring the members to come forward themselves "would result in nullification of the right at the very moment of its assertion."[^170] The right itself was located in the members' own liberty: "freedom to engage in association for the advancement of beliefs and ideas is an inseparable aspect of the 'liberty' assured by the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, which embraces freedom of speech."[^171]

Three things follow. The right was a natural person's liberty, held in full without any charter. The charter added nothing to it and could add nothing. And the Court called an *incorporated* body a medium, so the incorporation did not turn the body into a holder. That is the first set, described by the Court in the case most often cited for corporate rights of association, and the description supports no right of the name's own.

The division has a ground, and the ground should be stated before more cases are, because it is what the cases track. Constitutional rights divide by their object and by the direction in which their interest runs. Rights of security have an object that can be held: a house, papers and effects, property, a claim, a defense. The Fourth Amendment secures the people "in their persons, houses, papers, and effects"; the Fifth forbids a taking without just compensation and a deprivation without due process; the Seventh preserves the jury over a claim at law; the Sixth guarantees the accused. Their interest lies in the person whose holding is at stake, and they run from that person outward, against the sovereign, as limits on what it may do to what is theirs. Because the invasion is of the object, whoever lawfully holds the object may invoke the limit. The head of a household invokes the Fourth Amendment against a search of the home because the home is theirs, as their own interest and not as anyone's representative; the Court's rule that Fourth Amendment rights "are personal rights which, like some other constitutional rights, may not be vicariously asserted," so that a person aggrieved only through a search of "a third person's premises or property has not had any of his Fourth Amendment rights infringed," is a rule about the household's members, each of whom has the same interest in the home.[^172] The right is in the holder because the object is the holder's.

When the people grant a name that may hold property, these limits travel with the holding, not as rights of the name's own but as the incident of holding: the members' plant, papers and effects are no less theirs for being held under the name. The Court's standing rule, applied to a name, resolves the question of whose security a search of the plant invades by naming the name, and denies the shareholder a right in premises that are "a third person's." That is a description the article measures and a result it keeps. Strike the name as the holder and the plant is still the members' property, invaded; and the question who may complain of it is the one this section's *NAACP* paragraph has already answered, the name asserting the holding for the persons behind it because requiring them to come forward severally "would result in nullification of the right at the very moment of its assertion," which is the medium, the one vicarious assertion the Court allows and has named. That is what the Court saw in *Hale v. Henkel* as well, an association of individuals under an assumed name whose property cannot be taken without compensation (Section VII.C), and it is why the Court was right in result to find the Fourth Amendment at a corporation's premises and right to say "some" of the rights it finds there; the holder named as the name is the description the article measures. *Dow Chemical* is the case the adversary names, and the article names it back as a security case rightly decided. The question was whether aerial photography of a two-thousand-acre plant was a search, and the Court held it was not, because the open areas of an industrial complex are not the curtilage of a home: "The intimate activities associated with family privacy and the home and its curtilage simply do not reach the outdoor areas or spaces between structures and buildings of a manufacturing plant."[^173] The plant is the members' holding, and the Court measured the holding by what a plant is, not by what a person is; *Morton Salt*'s sentence that corporations "can claim no equality with individuals in the enjoyment of a right to privacy" and *Oklahoma Press*'s "the Fourth, if applicable" (Section VII.C) are the Court's own statements that the security travels as far as the object and no further.

Rights of participation have no object but the person's own act: to speak, to publish, to assemble, to petition, to worship, to vote. They exist because the people govern themselves, and each member's voice is the sovereign's own deliberation distributed to each as a member. Their interest runs the other way, from the sovereign to each person as a member. And because their object is the act of the self, they cannot be held or exercised by one person for another without that person's consent to the act. An agent speaks the principal's words by the principal's consent, and the act remains the principal's; without that consent no one may speak another's speech, cast another's vote or hold another's belief, and a head of household who tried to exercise the household's speech, or to prevent it, would find no constitutional provision behind them. Where consent to the act is recorded, and where it is not, is Section V.D's question. These rights therefore cannot pass to a name. What comes out of a name is two things at once: the words, which are identified persons' acts, the writer's article, the officer's statement, protected as theirs; and the money, which is the pool's, the people's grant and the people's to condition. The statute reached the second and left the first whole (Section VI.A). There is no third thing for the name to hold. The Fifth Amendment privilege proves the line, because it is a shield that the Court denies to the name while granting the Fourth. The privilege protects a person's own testimony, an act of the self that no custodian can hold, which is why the Court calls it "purely personal" and says that a custodian's production of the corporation's records "is not deemed a personal act, but rather an act of the corporation."[^174] From *Hale* in 1906 to *Braswell* in 1988 the Court's practice tracks the object of the right exactly: holdable, through the name; an act of the self, never. The Court has drawn the article's line, and in 1989 Justice O'Connor named it, in the criterion Section VII.C quotes: what a corporation "can do as an entity."

The adversary's objection to the two sets has a standard form, and it was given in writing to the Senate's committee in the weeks after *Citizens United*. Without constitutional rights for corporations, the witness answered, "the government could seize corporate property without due process or just compensation; corporations would not have rights against warrantless, unreasonable searches of their properties," and the same would follow for partnerships and unions, which "have rights because they are made up of people."[^175] Every item in the parade is a right of security, and every one travels through the name as the incident of the members' holding; the article grants them all, and grants them for the witness's own reason, that the body is made up of people. The witness's next sentence concedes the rest of the article's premise: "The law allows for the legal fiction of a corporation because it benefits the citizenry and the members of the corporation."[^176] The parade names one claim of participation, and the article meets it in the witness's terms. "The true judicial radicals," he wrote, "are those who would deprive Americans of their rights on the grounds that they have joined in the corporate form."[^177] Granted: the persons who join the form keep every right of participation they had, and Section VI.A shows that the statute left all of them open, the officer's own speech, the members' pooled speech through the committee, every member's vote. What they cannot do is hold the pool's speech as their own act without their consent to it (Section V.D), and that is not a right they had before joining, so it cannot be one they were deprived of by joining. There is no household head for speech.

Procedural due process and the civil jury are not rights of security at all. They are rules of the proceeding, which the people's permission to sue and be sued carries with it, binding the court whoever the party is, and the Court's own Seventh Amendment case says so. In *Ross v. Bernhard* the Court held that a derivative suit presenting a legal claim carries the jury right, and it reasoned from the suit and not from the plaintiff: "a corporation, although an artificial being, was commonly entitled to sue and be sued in the usual forms of action," and "whether the corporation was viewed as an entity separate from its stockholders or as a device permitting its stockholders to carry on their business and to sue and be sued, a corporation's suit to enforce a legal right was an action at common law carrying the right to jury trial at the time the Seventh Amendment was adopted."[^178] The jury came because the suit was at law, not because the plaintiff was a person; the Court declined the theory question, as this article does; and when it said that the claim "is not his own but the corporation's," that the corporation is "the real party in interest, the stockholder being at best the nominal plaintiff," and that "the heart of the action is the corporate claim," it was speaking the entity vocabulary the article measures.[^179] The article's sorting puts the claim where *Ross*'s result puts it. A corporate cause of action is an asset of the pool, the second set's capacity to sue under a common name (Section IV.A), sued on under the name by the sovereign's own procedure, and the members' interest in it is the residual claim that lets a stockholder bring the suit. One phrase slips, "one entitling the corporation to a jury trial under the Seventh Amendment," where the entitlement is the suit's, tried at law by the sovereign's procedure whoever the party is. Right in result, right in its reasoning from the suit, and no third set.

The Court had faced the question of looking through the name fifty years before *NAACP v. Alabama* and had declined to, and the case belongs here because the article's rule is the one it misapplied. Kentucky's Day Law of 1904 made it unlawful for "any person, corporation or association of persons" to maintain a school where white and Black pupils were both received; its title, as Harlan gives it, was "An act to prohibit white and colored persons from attending the same school"; Berea College's charter, in his recital, was for an institution of learning "in order to promote the cause of Christ."[^180] The Kentucky court had added, "as if merely incidental to or a make-weight," in Harlan's words, that the corporation "has no natural right to teach at all. Its right to teach is such as the State sees fit to give to it."[^181] The Court affirmed on that ground alone. "In creating a corporation a State may withhold powers which may be exercised by and cannot be denied to an individual. It is under no obligation to treat both alike"; it was "unnecessary for us to consider anything more than the question of its validity as applied to corporations"; and the Court would "concern ourselves only with the inquiry whether the first section can be upheld as coming within the power of a State over its own corporate creatures."[^182] The sentence about the name is true, and the holding is the error. A name has no natural right to any act; that is this article's first premise. The error is the next step, that because the name has none the State may forbid the teaching. Teaching is a person's act, a right of participation in its plainest form, and the corporation taught only as its teachers taught. The grant gave the name the power to maintain a school, to hold the buildings and the endowment and to contract in one name, and the State might condition or withhold that power; what the grant never gave or withheld was the act of teaching and learning together, which was the teachers' and the students', and which the same persons might do without the form. The Court let the condition on the power reach the act, and that is the error.

Harlan, dissenting, saw it. In so ruling, he wrote, "it must necessarily have been assumed by this court that the legislature may have regarded the teaching of white and colored pupils at the same time and in the same school or institution, when maintained by private individuals and associations, as wholly different in its results from such teaching when conducted by the same individuals acting under the authority of or representing a corporation"; the statute was "an arbitrary invasion of the rights of liberty and property guaranteed by the Fourteenth Amendment against hostile state action"; and the right to impart instruction is "beyond question, part of one's liberty as guaranteed against hostile state action by the Constitution of the United States."[^183] If the State could do this, he added, it could "forbid the assembling of white and colored children in the same Sabbath-school," though the teaching be "under the authority of the church to which the school is attached as well as with the consent of the parents of the children."[^184] Every case in Section III's census runs one way, the persons' rights moved into the name. *Berea* runs the other way, the State reaching the persons' acts through the name by addressing the name. Both rest on one false premise, that the name holds the act. The article takes *Berea*'s rule and names its use in each place it cites it: the rule was used to segregate a college because the Court held only half of it.

The Court supplied the missing half seventeen years later, in two sentences that this section's division could have written. "Appellees are corporations and therefore, it is said, they cannot claim for themselves the liberty which the Fourteenth Amendment guarantees. Accepted in the proper sense, this is true," the Court said in *Pierce v. Society of Sisters*, citing *Riggs* and *Western Turf*, the two cases that had kept liberty from the name in 1906 and 1907 and whose rule *Berea* applied without citing them; the sentences that follow, on the business and property the schools held and the child who "is not the mere creature of the State," Section V.A quotes.[^185] The Court even described *Berea* for what it was, a statute read "as though merely intended to amend the charters of certain private corporations." *Pierce* is *Berea* with both sets held: the name's property in the second, the persons' liberty in the first, and nothing of the name's own between them. Harlan's dissent of 1908 is the first half of *Pierce*'s reasoning of 1925.

Then came *NAACP v. Alabama*, where the State's device was Kentucky's. Alabama sued under its foreign-corporation statute "to enjoin the Association from conducting further activities within, and to oust it from, the State," and demanded the members' names;[^186] the Court looked through the name to the persons, as the paragraphs above record. Five years later, in *NAACP v. Button*, the Court added a description. The Association "may assert this right on its own behalf, because, though a corporation, it is directly engaged in those activities, claimed to be constitutionally protected, which the statute would curtail. Cf. *Grosjean v. American Press Co.*"; and in the next sentence, "We also think petitioner has standing to assert the corresponding rights of its members."[^187] The second sentence is the first set. The first is right in result and wrong in description, a name "directly engaged" in acts that only its lawyers and members performed, with *Grosjean* as the authority, which Section IX.E measures. The business treasury inherited the description. *Bellotti*, rejecting the reasoning of the Massachusetts court, quoted the argument that the Fourteenth Amendment's liberty "is the liberty of NATURAL not of artificial persons" and answered it in a footnote with "But see *NAACP v. Alabama ex rel. Patterson* … ; *NAACP v. Button*," the two civil-rights cases cited as if they had held the name's own liberty, against the very line *Berea* had applied;[^188] and *Citizens United*, at page 342, cited *Button* and *Grosjean* for the proposition that political speech does not lose protection "simply because its source is a corporation" (Section III.J, the census's "person" entry). The Court withheld liberty from the name when the persons behind it were Black students and their teachers; looked through the name to the persons when they were the Association's members; described the name as acting on its own behalf in the Association's next case; and cited that description for the business treasury. The persons' victories were real and were theirs, by the Court's own words of 1958. What the business corporation inherited in 1978 and 2010 was the description, not the persons.

### C. The Second Set, from the Sovereigns Who Granted It: Ulpian, Blackstone, *Paul*, the State Constitutions, *Flint*, and Rehnquist

The second set is older than the first set's case law by two millennia, and every sovereign who granted it said what it was.

Ulpian, in the Digest's title on suits in the name of a body, in the fragment Section II.B translated: "*Si quid universitati debetur, singulis non debetur: nec quod debet universitas singuli debent.*"[^189] And in the next fragment: whether all, some, or none of the body's members remain, the body is the same.[^190] The separation of the body's debts from the members' and the body's survival through every change of them: the two incidents, stated as rules of the sovereign's law in the second century, in a title whose first fragment says that such a body is not conceded to everyone but "in very few cases."[^191]

Blackstone, opening his chapter on corporations, in the sentences Section II.F quoted: the law constitutes artificial persons for a perpetual succession, to preserve rights which, granted only to the individuals composing the body, "would upon their death be utterly lost and extinct."[^192] The incident is succession; the rights are "granted"; and the king's consent is "absolutely necessary."[^193]

And closing the chapter, at the dissolution: "The debts of a corporation, either to or from it, are totally extinguished by it's dissolution; so that the members thereof cannot recover, or be charged with them, in their natural capacities," a rule Blackstone grounds in the same maxim of Ulpian's that opens this section.[^194] Limited liability, in the chapter's own words, is the grant's incident, and it lasts as long as the grant.

The Supreme Court, in *Paul v. Virginia*: "Now a grant of corporate existence is a grant of special privileges to the corporators, enabling them to act for certain designated purposes as a single individual, and exempting them (unless otherwise specially provided) from individual liability."[^195] Note to whom the grant runs. Not to a corporation; to "the corporators," the natural persons, who receive two privileges, the single name and the exemption from liability. The corporation itself, the same opinion says, is "the mere creation of local law" and "can have no legal existence beyond the limits of the sovereignty where created."[^196]

The people of the States, in their constitutions. Virginia's, in 1902, defined the thing: "the term 'corporation' or 'company' shall include all trusts, associations and joint stock companies having any powers or privileges not possessed by individuals or unlimited partnerships," excluding municipal corporations and the public institutions of the State.[^197] A corporation is, by the people's own definition, whatever has powers individuals and partnerships lack. That is the second set written into a constitution. And Alabama's people in 1867, in the words Michigan's had used in 1850[^198] and Section II.G.4 quotes, reserved the condition in the section that granted the power.[^199] The grantor conditions the grant in the act of making it.

And Justice Rehnquist, dissenting in *Bellotti*, named the incidents as the grant in the modern Reports: "A State grants to a business corporation the blessings of potentially perpetual life and limited liability to enhance its efficiency as an economic entity," and "the mere creation of a corporation does not invest it with all the liberties enjoyed by natural persons."[^200]

The Court said the same of the privilege when it taxed it and when it regulated it. The federal corporation tax, *Flint v. Stone Tracy Co.* held in 1911, is "an excise upon the particular privilege of doing business in a corporate capacity," laid "upon the privileges which exist in conducting business with the advantages which inhere in the corporate capacity of those taxed, and which are not enjoyed by private firms or individuals"; the advantages the Court listed are this section's second set, "the continuity of the business, without interruption by death or dissolution, the transfer of property interests by the disposition of shares of stock, the advantages of business controlled and managed by corporate directors, the general absence of individual liability"; and "it is this distinctive privilege which is the subject of taxation."[^201] In 1987 the Court called corporations "entities whose very existence and attributes are a product of state law," and said that "no principle of corporation law and practice is more firmly established than a State's authority to regulate domestic corporations, including the authority to define the voting rights of shareholders."[^202] Brandeis, dissenting in *Liggett*, recorded the grant's history as a grant: incorporation for business "was commonly denied long after it had been freely granted for religious, educational and charitable purposes," and only "after 1875 extension of the privilege to every lawful business became" common.[^203] Section VI.I measures what the Court later did with the privilege's charge. Here it is enough that the Court, taxing the privilege and regulating it, described it as this section does.

The second set, then, is not a theory. It is what the Digest, Blackstone, the Court, the people of the States, and the one Justice who tried to limit corporate rights all said the corporation was: a grant of succession and exemption to natural persons, conditionable by the grantor.

### D. Why the Two Sets Exhaust the Field: The Line Is Drawn by What Private Agreement Cannot Create

The division is exhaustive because the second set is exactly the part of a corporation that private agreement cannot produce, and the first set is exactly the part it can.

Natural persons may agree among themselves to act together, to pool property, to speak with one voice, to appoint agents, to share profits and losses. That is a partnership, and every right a partnership can assert is a right its partners hold already. What natural persons cannot do by agreement among themselves is bind people who were not party to it. Limited liability tells a creditor or a tort victim, who agreed to nothing, that the members' assets are beyond reach. Perpetual succession tells the law of inheritance that this property does not pass at death, and tells the members' successors that the body continues without them. Each alters the rights of strangers to the contract. Binding strangers is what a sovereign's rule does and what private agreement cannot do. So the line between the sets is not drawn by a theory; it is drawn by who can create what. Everything the members could create for themselves is theirs already and needs no grant. Everything that needs a grant binds strangers and is not a right.

Carter saw this in 1919, listing the "distinguishing characteristics of a corporation" as "limited liability of individual members for the debts of the corporation, continuing existence irrespective of change of membership or stock-ownership, and centralized control of property and interests," and adding "the necessary condition of corporate existence: origin and definition of legal powers in an enabling act of the State."
