He noted that the right to sue and hold property in a group name "is not exclusively a quality of the incorporated group," since a statute may give it to unincorporated associations.[^204] Strip away what a legislature may give to anyone and what remains of the corporation is the two incidents and the enabling act: the second set, and the rule that confers it.

The hinge should be stated at its narrowest, because an adversary will narrow it. Much of what the two incidents do can be had by agreement: a lender may agree to look only to the firm's assets, a continuing body was had before any general act by the deed of settlement and the trust, and management is agency. The part no agreement reaches is the part that binds persons who did not agree. The tort victim contracted for nothing. The member's own creditor is told by the sovereign's rule that the pool is beyond reach, and that shielding of the body's assets from its owners' creditors is the one thing organizational law provides and contract cannot.[^205] The law of succession is told that this property does not pass. Those three are the whole of the criterion, and they are enough, because each binds a stranger, and binding strangers is what the article means by the second set. The private forms themselves lived on the sovereign's sufferance: the unchartered joint stock was suppressed by statute in 1720, and registration and limited liability were granted back by statute in 1844 and 1855, the "system subordinate" permitted and withdrawn by the power that permits it.[^206]

### E. The Consequence: A Right of the Name's Own Is a Right of Something That Does Not Exist

If there is no third set, then every holding that attributes a constitutional right to a corporation as such is a holding about something that does not exist. Where the right in question is one a member could assert in their own name, the holding is correct in result and wrong in description: the right is the member's, the name is the medium, and the holding should say so. Where the right in question is one no member could assert, the holding has conferred on a rule the protection the text reserves to persons, and Section V shows where that line falls.

*Dartmouth College* is the oldest case of the first kind, and the one its own figure of speech has most obscured. The plaintiffs' title, Marshall wrote, "originates in a charter dated the 13th day of December, in the year 1769, incorporating twelve persons therein mentioned, by the name of 'The Trustees of Dartmouth College,' granting to them and their successors the usual corporate privileges and powers"; the act of 1816 "increases the number of trustees to twenty-one, gives the appointment of the additional members to the executive of the state, and creates a board of overseers, with power to inspect and control the most important acts of the trustees"; and "The majority of the trustees of the college have refused to accept this amended charter."[^207] The Court held the acts void under the Contract Clause, and the holding needs no "artificial being."

Nine years earlier, in *Fletcher v. Peck*, the Court had held that a grant is a contract: "A grant, in its own nature, amounts to an extinguishment of the right of the grantor, and implies a contract not to re-assert that right. A party is, therefore, always estopped by his own grant. Since, then, in fact, a grant is a contract executed, the obligation of which still continues …"[^208] Marshall put *Dartmouth* on that rule in sentences that name persons and acts and not a being: "It can require no argument to prove, that the circumstances of this case constitute a contract. An application is made to the crown for a charter to incorporate a religious and literary institution. In the application, it is stated, that large contributions have been made for the object, which will be conferred on the corporation, as soon as it shall be created. The charter is granted, and on its faith the property is conveyed. Surely, in this transaction every ingredient of a complete and legitimate contract is to be found."[^209] The applicants, the contributors, and the twelve were natural persons; the charter was their contract; the acts impaired it. The right was theirs, a first-set right carried through the name, and the holding was right in result. It was wrong in description, and only there: nine pages on, the opinion located what the clause protected in a being whose properties were "incidental to its very existence," and described the being with a hedge. Strike the being and the holding stands where *Fletcher* and the charter put it. The people of the States answered *Dartmouth* in the sections of their constitutions that make the grant, in the sentence that follows the grant in Michigan's and Alabama's: "All general laws, and special acts passed pursuant to this section, may be altered, amended, or repealed."[^210] The reservation takes no right from any person. It states the grantor's rule in advance, so that no later charter can be read as a promise not to alter it, and it is the remedy of Section IX.C in action: a sovereign conditioning the grant the name holds, by the people's own instrument.

The Court itself practiced the measurement in 1880, in the case the adversary offers to show that the test is unnecessary. Mississippi had chartered a lottery company for twenty-five years and then, in its constitution of 1868, forbade lotteries; the company pleaded its charter as a contract. "No legislature can bargain away the public health or the public morals," the Court answered. "The people themselves cannot do it, much less their servants."[^211] Then the second set, in the Court's own words, after the trust sentence Section II.G quoted: the people's agencies "may create corporations, and give them, so to speak, a limited citizenship; but as citizens, limited in their privileges, or otherwise, these creatures of the government creation are subject to such rules and regulations as may from time to time be ordained and established for the preservation of health and morality."[^212] Two years earlier the Court had said of a chartered fertilizer works ordered out of a growing village that "the charter was a sufficient license until revoked; but we cannot regard it as a contract guaranteeing, in the locality originally selected, exemption for fifty years from the exercise of the police power of the State."[^213] And in *Charles River Bridge* Taney had put the grantor's standing terms in one sentence: "While the rights of private property are sacredly guarded, we must not forget that the community also have rights, and that the happiness and well being of every citizen depends on their faithful preservation."[^214] *Stone* is the grantor conditioning the grant, which is what the people's reservation clauses do in advance; the two are one proposition, and "limited citizenship" is this section's second set named by the Court. "Creatures" is the Court's word, quoted here and never the article's own.

What the reserved-power doctrine never asked is the question this article puts. The doctrine limited what a charter may promise. It never asked whether the name holds a right against the grantor, because in 1880 that question had not been invented; it was invented when the question moved from the grant to the right, and the police power could not follow it there. *Citizens United* at page 351 is the gap: a condition on the grant treated as a penalty on a speaker, in the sentence Section VI.H reads whole, that the State cannot exact the forfeiture of First Amendment rights as the price of the form's special advantages. *Stone* answers a charter's promise. It has no answer to a right the name is said to hold, and the test does. The test detects what the doctrine lacks, not what it duplicates.

Blackstone supplies the baseline the doctrine stood on and the adversary leaves out. The founder of a corporation is the king alone, as Section II.E quoted; "the law doth annex a condition to every such grant, that if the corporation be dissolved, the grantor shall have the lands again, because the cause of the grant faileth. The grant is indeed only during the life of the corporation"; and a corporation may be dissolved "by act of parliament, which is boundless in it's operations," by the death of all its members, by surrender of its franchises, or "by forfeiture of it's charter, through negligence or abuse of it's franchises; in which case the law judges that the body politic has broken the condition upon which it was incorporated, and thereupon the incorporation is void."[^215] Lord Kenyon stated the same law from the bench in 1789: "Corporations are the creatures of the Crown: and on their dissolution their franchises revert to the Crown. But if the King choose that all their rights shall be revived, it is competent to him to do so, either with the old or new corporators; and thereby no person is injured, nor is any rule of law infringed."[^216] In England the sovereign of one will could end any corporation, and when the Crown seized the charter of the City of London the proceeding "gave great and just offence" and was answered by a statute that the charter "shall never more be forfeited for any cause whatsoever."[^217] The American innovation was the Contract Clause as *Fletcher* and *Dartmouth* read it, which withdrew that boundless power from the legislatures of the States for every charter on the faith of which property had been conveyed. The sentence this section needs is that the withdrawal was a rule the people wrote about their own servants, not a right the corporation holds of its own. The people of the United States bound the States not to impair the obligation of contracts; a charter on the faith of which property was conveyed was, as this section has shown, the corporators' contract; and the people of the States then wrote Blackstone's condition into their constitutions so that no later charter could be read as a promise not to alter it. *Dartmouth* stays where *Fletcher* and the charter put it, the trustees' contract, right in result. The reservation clauses are the condition the common law annexed to every grant, written down by the grantor. And page 351 is the one place in the Reports where that condition was read as a price exacted from a holder of rights.

## V. The Derivative Theory: True for Every Claim a Member Could Bring, and Ended Where No Member's Consent Can Be Found

The adversary's strongest answer to the two sets is the derivative theory: that a corporation's right is only its members' rights asserted together, so that nothing need have been conferred. This section grants the theory everything it can claim, which is the whole of the first set, and then follows it to its edge: the first claim no member could bring, where consent runs out and only the name is left.

### A. The Theory: The Corporation's Rights as Its Members' Rights Asserted Collectively, from *Deveaux* to *Citizens United*

The derivative theory holds that a corporation has no rights of its own, and that when a court protects "the corporation's" property or speech it is protecting the natural persons behind the name, whose rights are asserted collectively because that is the convenient way to assert them. The theory is the oldest account of corporate rights in the Reports and the one most compatible with the two sets, because it needs no grant at all. On its own terms it is simply the first set, stated as a theory.[^218]

Its founding instance is Marshall's sentence in *Deveaux*, read in Section III.B: the corporation "is certainly not a citizen," and the suit proceeds only where "the rights of the members, in this respect, can be exercised in their corporate name."[^219] The corporation is not the holder; the members are. Its strongest instance is *NAACP v. Alabama*, where the Court protected the members' associational liberty through the medium of the Association because the members could not protect it themselves.[^220] Its invocation for the treasury is the majority's in *Citizens United*, which called corporations "associations of citizens" and held that the government may not ban "the political speech of millions of associations of citizens."[^221] The theory's fullest statement in the modern Reports is in *Burwell v. Hobby Lobby Stores, Inc.*, and the statement should be read with the word the Court was reading. The question was whether closely held corporations are "persons" under the Religious Freedom Restoration Act, and the Court answered it from the Dictionary Act, which it "must consult" in determining the meaning of any Act of Congress "unless the context indicates otherwise," and which provides that "the words 'person' and 'whoever' include corporations, companies, associations, firms, partnerships, societies, and joint stock companies, as well as individuals."[^222] That statute is the people's legislature saying when a name is to be treated as a person for the purposes of the people's statutes, with the bound written into the sentence that says it: unless the context indicates otherwise. It is the rule of treatment of Section II.C enacted, a purpose-bound "as if" made by the sovereign's own organ, and what the legislature made by statute it may unmake by statute.

The rule is older than its present text, and its date is the article's exhibit and not the adversary's. On February 25, 1871, three years after the Fourteenth Amendment's ratification and fifteen before *Santa Clara*, Congress provided, in the words the Court quotes, that "in all acts hereafter passed . . . the word 'person' may extend and be applied to bodies politic and corporate . . . unless the context shows that such words were intended to be used in a more limited sense."[^223] Every word of it is the article's. The rule is for "acts hereafter passed," the people's statutes and not their Constitution; it is permissive, "may extend"; it carries its bound in its own sentence; and it was needed, which is the legislature's own admission that "person" did not reach the name by its own force. The people's legislature wrote the rule for its statutes and wrote none for the Amendment, which only Article V could carry; the adversary who offers the ratifying generation's statute as proof that "person" reached corporations offers the proof that it took a statute to make it do so.

The Court read the present rule as such a rule is read, by its purpose: "Congress provided protection for people like the Hahns and Greens by employing a familiar legal fiction: It included corporations within RFRA's definition of 'persons.' But it is important to keep in mind that the purpose of this fiction is to provide protection for human beings. A corporation is simply a form of organization used by human beings to achieve desired ends."[^224] Then, on the next page, the Court stated the derivative sorting in its own voice: "When rights, whether constitutional or statutory, are extended to corporations, the purpose is to protect the rights of these people. For example, extending Fourth Amendment protection to corporations protects the privacy interests of employees and others associated with the company. Protecting corporations from government seizure of their property without just compensation protects all those who have a stake in the corporations' financial well-being. And protecting the free-exercise rights of corporations like Hobby Lobby, Conestoga, and Mardel protects the religious liberty of the humans who own and control those companies."[^225] And it stated the rule of treatment outright. The court below had written that general business corporations do not exercise religion "separate and apart from" their owners and employees, and the Court answered: "All of this is true—but quite beside the point. Corporations, 'separate and apart from' the human beings who own, run, and are employed by them, cannot do anything at all."[^226] Every sentence is the derivative theory, and every sentence is this article's. What they are not is a holding that the name holds a constitutional right. The case was decided under the statute; the "person" the Court extended to the companies was the Dictionary Act's, the people's own rule for the name; the rights protected were "the religious liberty of the humans who own and control those companies," which is the first set in the Court's words; and the capacity in which the companies stood in court was the second set in action, the people's grant of a name that may sue and may be a "person" under the people's statutes on the people's terms. Whether the statutory extension reaches a company of one owner or of a thousand is a question about the statute's context, and the Court's answer to it is the Court's; the article's sorting never turns on the count (Section VI.A). Run on the companies' own instruments, the criterion of Section V.D gives the Court's result without the count. Conestoga's board-adopted statements and Hobby Lobby's statement of purpose, to which every member of the owning family "has signed a pledge," committed the companies to "operating the company in a manner consistent with Biblical principles," and the Court found that the States' laws "permit for-profit corporations to pursue 'any lawful purpose' or 'act,' including the pursuit of profit in conformity with the owners' religious principles."[^227] The instrument contained the commitment, so the owners' exercise through the name was consented matter, which is the first set, and the count never enters.

*Hobby Lobby* is therefore not the article's difficulty but its cleanest modern exhibit: the sovereign's own organ extending a word to a name, for a stated purpose, by an instrument the sovereign can amend; the Court reading the extension by its purpose; and the opinion nowhere supposing that the Fourteenth Amendment had done the same.

The theory's principal authority, though, is not the case that states it most fully but the one that applies it with both halves held, and that is *Pierce v. Society of Sisters*. Oregon's compulsory education act would have ended the appellees' schools; two corporations that maintained them sued to enjoin it before its effective date; and the State answered that corporations cannot claim the Fourteenth Amendment's liberty. The Court agreed with the State's premise and decided for the corporations anyway, and the two steps are the derivative theory entire. "Appellees are corporations and therefore, it is said, they cannot claim for themselves the liberty which the Fourteenth Amendment guarantees. Accepted in the proper sense, this is true," the Court said, citing *Riggs* and *Western Turf*, which had kept liberty from the name in 1906 and 1907; "But they have business and property for which they claim protection."[^228] The liberty the Court vindicated was the persons': "The child is not the mere creature of the State; those who nurture him and direct his destiny have the right, coupled with the high duty, to recognize and prepare him for additional obligations."[^229] And the corporations' standing to vindicate it rested on what they held by grant and on the persons who dealt with them: "the injunctions here sought are not against the exercise of any proper power. Plaintiffs asked protection against arbitrary, unreasonable and unlawful interference with their patrons and the consequent destruction of their business and property. Their interest is clear and immediate," within the rule of the cases "where injunctions have issued to protect business enterprises against interference with the freedom of patrons or customers."[^230] The name held no liberty. The name held business and property, the grant's incidents and the members' holding (Section IV); the parents and the teachers held the liberty; and the suit in the name carried the persons' right because the name's holding was the thing the statute would have destroyed. The Court was unanimous, and it wrote in 1925 what the theory's fullest modern statement wrote in 2014: the name as the form through which persons' rights are protected, with nothing of the name's own between. Section IV.B placed *Pierce* beside *Berea* as the Court supplying the half it had withheld in 1908. Here it is the derivative theory's model case, because it is the one in which the Court said in terms that the name has no liberty of its own and protected the persons through it in the same opinion.

The theory's appeal to a reading grounded in popular sovereignty is plain. The Constitution restrains government. If government takes property without due process, natural persons are injured whether the title was in their own names or in a name they share. Nothing is conferred on the corporation. The people's own rights are exercised through it. On that view there is no corporate right for Article V to have omitted, and the article's question dissolves.

### B. Where the Theory Is True: The First Set, Stated as a Theory

The article accepts the theory within its proper class, and states the class precisely. The derivative theory is true for every claim a member could bring in their own name. The members' property, held under the corporate name, is the members' property, and a taking of it without due process injures them. The members' association, carried on under the corporate name, is the members' association, and a State that punishes membership abridges their liberty. For claims of this kind the corporation needs no grant, the name is a medium, and the Court's protection of "the corporation" is correct in result. This is the first set, and the theory describes it accurately.

What is theirs should be stated exactly, because Section VI depends on it. The members' property in the pool is the residual claim: the right to distributions, and to the surplus on dissolution, which the Court protects, under the name,[^231] and which a taking of the pool injures. The direction of the pool while it is pooled, what it may be spent on and by whom, is not theirs; it is the rule's, fixed by the charter's purposes, the doctrine of ultra vires, and the power the people reserved in the section that made the grant.[^232] *Citizens United* protected the direction. The claim was never touched.

### C. Where the Theory Ends: A Right No Member Holds, from Either End of the Chain

The theory's own logic fixes its limit. A right that derives from the members cannot exceed what the members hold. A medium carries what passes through it. Two forms of one claim exceed the members' rights, and for each the theory has no answer.

The first is a right no member holds. The treasury speech in *Citizens United* is the instance. A shareholder has no right to spend another shareholder's money on political speech the second shareholder opposes. Yet that is what general-treasury expenditure is: "it is the shareholders, as the residual claimants, who are effectively footing the bill," as Justice Stevens wrote in the passage Section VI.C sets out.[^233] Speech funded by persons who oppose it derives from none of them. The derivation stops. The right the majority protected has no member as its source, and on the theory's own terms it is therefore not derivative; it is the name's.

The second is the same claim seen from the other end of the chain. In the modern public corporation the "members" from whom rights would derive are, for the most part, other corporations, funds, and nominees, and the derivation regresses from name to name before it reaches a natural person. It does reach one: every chain of ownership ends in persons, and the Court's reason in *Hobby Lobby* for reading a statute's "person" to a company, that "the purpose of this fiction is to provide protection for human beings," is the reason the regress is not by itself an answer to the theory.[^234] What the regress never reaches is those persons' consent to the particular speech. The beneficiary at the end of a chain of funds was never asked whether the treasury at its head should speak, and a right that derives from members derives from no one who did not choose it. That is the first form again, with the dissenting shareholder replaced by a person who was never consulted, and the theory's answer to it is the same: none.

The derivative theory has a rival that reaches the same edge from the other side, and the rival should be answered here, once, because it is the ground on which the treasury's speech is most often defended in the literature. The realist, or association, theory holds that the corporation is neither an unreal thing nor a sum of its members but a real group, with a life and a will that the law recognizes rather than creates. Its American statement is Machen's, in 1911: "A corporation is an entity—not imaginary or fictitious, but real, not artificial but natural. Its existence is as real as that of an army or of the Church."[^235] Its question is Laski's, in 1916: "The state knows certain persons who are not men. What is the nature of their personality? Are they merely fictitious abstractions, collective names that hide from us the mass of individuals beneath? … Or is that personality real?"[^236] Its modern voices, Ripken, Tucker and Greenfield among them, draw from the group's reality a conclusion the theory's first exponents did not draw: that a body real enough to act is real enough to hold the rights of those who act, so that a corporation's speech is its own because the corporation is a real association, and citizens do not lose their rights by associating.[^237] The article does not dispute the reality. An army is real, a church is real, and a company is real as a going concern; the law's recognition of a body that acts is what Section II.A called a name that functions in a sovereign's rule, and a rule is as real as anything in law. What the article disputes is the step from reality to right, and the theory's own American exponent refused that step on the page where he asserted the reality. Having "established that a corporation is a real and natural entity," Machen asked whether the entity is a person and answered that in the word's ordinary sense "the corporate entity is not, in truth and reality, a person. For the corporate entity is not a human being; it is not even a rational creature capable of feeling and willing."[^238] Laski, for his part, records the English lawyers' answer in a sentence the article quotes as his and not its own, "The corporation is the creature of the state. Its will is a delegated will; its purpose exists only because it has secured recognition," and records in the note to it that the English lawyers hold the concession theory, the body existing by the sovereign's grant, and that Sir Frederick Pollock had denied in 1911 that they had received the "fiction theory" at all; so that on the realists' own account the English lawyers' theory was the article's, the grant, and the fiction theory, which the common law is often said to have taken, is the one Pollock denied it ever took.[^239] What Section III found in the Reports is the American half of the same finding: the Court adopted no theory and used a word.

The step from reality to right fails by two measures this article has already stated, and the failure is the same whichever way the group is described. The first is the measure of consent, which Section V.D makes checkable. A real group acts only through persons, and what it does as a group is what its members consented to do together; the record of that consent is the charter or the terms of membership. The association theory supposes that a body which may act in one name may speak in one name. But a body speaks only as its members' consent reaches the utterance, and the ordinary business charter, "any lawful business" in the words the general incorporation laws have used since Brandeis counted them (Section IV.C), records consent to business and to no utterance. The reality of the group does not supply the consent of its members: a real army does not thereby cast its soldiers' votes, and a real company does not thereby hold its shareholders' speech. The second is the measure the common law set on its own fictions and rules of treatment, the possible matter (Section II.D): the law may proceed "as if" only of what could be true, for the law imitates nature. That a group may hold land, sue in one name and succeed to itself is possible, and the grant makes it actual. That a group has a conscience, a belief or a voice of its own, distinct from the consent of the persons who compose it, is not a possible matter, and Machen said as much in the sentence just quoted. The realists are right that the corporation is not an unreal thing, and Part One never said it was: it said the corporation is a name that functions in a sovereign's rule, with nothing maintained for convenience (Section II.A). What is a fiction of the impossible, and by the common law's own test an unlawful one, is the person the Court made of the name against the people's instrument. So the two theories end at one place. The derivative theory ends where no member's consent is found. The association theory ends there too, because the reality it rightly asserts is the reality of persons acting together, and when the persons' consent is subtracted there is nothing of the group's own left over to hold a right. The article names the edge once here and measures it, case by case, in the section that follows.

### D. The Association Cases: The Charter-Consent Criterion and the Usurpation Harm

A church is a different body from a business corporation, and the difference is not where the adversary looks for it. It is not in the grant. An incorporated church and a trading company are both formed under the people's general laws, both hold property in one name, both sue and are sued under it, and the incorporation of each is the second set and nothing else. Madison saw that in 1811, when as President he vetoed a bill incorporating the Protestant Episcopal Church in Alexandria, and the Court recounts the veto in the case that protects a church's choice of its ministers: the Religion Clauses "ensured that the new Federal Government—unlike the English Crown—would have no role in filling ecclesiastical offices," and Madison's ground was that the bill "exceeds the rightful authority to which Governments are limited, by the essential distinction between civil and religious functions," because it "enacts into, and establishes by law, sundry rules and proceedings relative purely to the organization and polity of the church incorporated, and comprehending even the election and removal of the Minister of the same."[^240] The civil power may grant the name; it may not write the religion into the grant. That is the two sets stated about a church's charter by the man the Court calls "the leading architect of the religion clauses of the First Amendment." The difference is in what the charter contains. A church's charter contains speech. Its creed is a declaration that every member consents to by joining, and when the body declares it the declaration is the members' own act performed together: a right of participation in Section IV.B's sense, exercised by the persons who hold it, through the body as their medium, in the one way the Court allows a right of that kind to be asserted through a name, where the consent to the act is recorded. A business corporation's ordinary charter contains no speech. Its purpose clause is "any lawful business," and no shareholder consents to any utterance by buying a share; what a shareholder consents to is business, and the shareholder's rights of participation stay where they were before the purchase, in the shareholder (Section IV.B). The criterion follows. Speech through a sanctioned body is the members' own act, in the first set, only where the charter or the terms of membership contain that speech, because the charter is where consent at formation is recorded. That is the rule Section VI.A states for the pool, that consent enters at the formation and never by headcount, made checkable: one reads the instrument the members joined under.

The criterion limits as much as it permits, and the limit is the part the adversary will resist. "Christ is King" is in a creed. "Candidate X should be President because X holds the creed" is in no creed. The step from the first sentence to the second is an inference the body's officers draw, and an inference is not consent. A member who joined under the creed consented to the declaration and to nothing the officers derive from it; the declaration the members made together does not carry the endorsement the officers make in its name; and the creed's speech is not transferable to advocacy the charter does not contain. The body remains free to declare what its charter declares. Its officers and members remain free to endorse whom they please as the persons they are. What the body cannot do is lend the weight of its members' consent to a position its members never consented to. Where the advocacy is paid for from the body's treasury a second bar falls independently of this one, since the pool is the people's grant and the people may condition its political use whatever the charter says (Section VI.I); the two measures are independent, and a body may pass one and fail the other. For the business corporation the criterion leaves exactly what Section IV.B left: nothing passes through the name as the persons' own except an identified officer's or employee's speech as themselves.

Consent is the point, and it should be said why. The weight of a body's speech is its members' consent, and nothing else; the name has no weight of its own, because a name has nothing of its own. When a church declares its creed, the declaration carries every member who joined under it, and those who hear it are told the truth about whose voice it is. When officers endorse a candidate in the body's name and the charter contains no such endorsement, the speech carries no member's consent, and those who hear it are told something false: that the members stand behind it. The weight the speech carries is borrowed from the members' consent, and where the consent is absent it is usurped. The harm runs two ways. It runs against the members, whose consent is taken and spent on a position they never sanctioned, so that each is made to appear to hold a view they may reject. That is the dissenting shareholder's injury of this Part's third section and the union member's injury of Section VI.E, and the Court has named the injury in its own words. "Compelling individuals to mouth support for views they find objectionable violates that cardinal constitutional command," the Court wrote in *Janus*; when speech is compelled "individuals are coerced into betraying their convictions. Forcing free and independent individuals to endorse ideas they find objectionable is always demeaning"; and "[c]ompelling a person to subsidize the speech of other private speakers raises similar First Amendment concerns," with Jefferson's sentence that to compel a man to furnish contributions for the propagation of opinions he disbelieves "is sinful and tyrannical."[^241] The Court was describing a compulsion by the State, and the article takes its description of the injury and not its claim. A body that speaks in its members' name compels no one; it attributes. The First Amendment runs against no private body, and the article asserts no claim under it against one. What the Court's sentences supply is the name of the thing taken: the attribution to a person of a position they reject, which is what a member suffers when a body speaks in the members' name without the members' consent.

And the harm runs against the sovereign. The people's deliberation, the process by which they govern themselves and in which each member's voice is the sovereign's own distributed to each (Section IV.B), receives a weight that does not exist: a candidate is characterized by a consent no member gave, and the people who must decide are told that a body of their fellow citizens stands where it does not. That is why the people may condition the use of their grant in their own deliberation, under Section VI.I and Section IV.B together. Not because the speech is corporate. Not because it is wealthy, which was *Austin*'s ground, which *Citizens United* rejected and which this article does not revive (Section VI.I). But because speech in a sanctioned body's name claims a consent it does not have, and the sovereign is the party deceived. The Court has met the members' half of this injury once and rejected it, and the rejection should be named where the harm is stated. In *Citizens United* the Government argued that corporate expenditures could be limited because of "its interest in protecting dissenting shareholders from being compelled to fund corporate political speech"; the majority answered that the interest "would allow the Government to ban the political speech even of media corporations" and that abuses could be corrected "through the procedures of corporate democracy," the coinage Section III measured, in the two sentences Section VI.C meets in the treasury's own case.[^242] What this section does with the injury is not what the Government asked. The dissenting member's injury is not offered as a ground for restricting the name's speech, which is what the Court rejected; it is the first half of the reason the people may condition their own grant, and the second half, the sovereign deceived, the majority never addressed. The media reductio is met by the criterion itself: a newspaper's instrument contains publication, so what it publishes is what its owners consented to at formation, and Section IX.E measures the media corporation on that ground. And the majority's own sentence left the door open: "As to other corporations, the remedy is not to restrict speech but to consider and explore other regulatory mechanisms."[^243] A condition on the people's grant is such a mechanism, and it is the people's to impose.

The Court has said each half of this, in the two opinions that bound the treasury's question. In *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* the Court let an advocacy corporation spend from its treasury and said why in the grammar of consent: "The resources in the treasury of a business corporation, however, are not an indication of popular support for the corporation's political ideas. They reflect instead the economically motivated decisions of investors and customers," whereas the statute's committee is funded so that "[t]he resources available to this fund, as opposed to the corporate treasury, in fact reflect popular support for the political positions of the committee."[^244] The three features of the exempt corporation that Section VI.A quotes, formation "for the express purpose of promoting political ideas," no shareholders with a claim on its assets, and no business corporation behind it, are the criterion in the Court's own hands: the charter contained the speech, so the treasury's political resources reflected political support. In *Citizens United* the majority, upholding disclosure, said the other half: "This transparency enables the electorate to make informed decisions and give proper weight to different speakers and messages."[^245] Weight, in the Court's sentence, depends on who stands behind the speech, and the electorate is entitled to know it; that is the hearer's half of the usurpation harm, stated by the majority in the opinion that let the business treasury speak. The article does not say that the Court has held the criterion. It says that the Court has stated both of its premises, the one in 1986 and the other in 2010, and that the criterion is what the two premises require when they are held together.

### E. The Criterion Run on the Cases: The Four Religion Cases Sorted, *Dale* Measured by Its Method on the Question the Court Declined to Ask, and the Court's Own Grammar Found to Be the Criterion's

The criterion should then be tested against the Court's religion cases, which the adversary offers as proof that bodies hold rights of their own, and it sorts each of the four. *Hosanna-Tabor*: the Court held that the Religion Clauses bar the government from interfering with "the freedom of religious groups to select their own" ministers, and distinguished *Smith* because "a church's selection of its ministers is unlike an individual's ingestion of peyote": the case "concerns government interference with an internal church decision that affects the faith and mission of the church itself."[^246] The selection of ministers is within the faith the members joined under; a congregation that confesses a creed has consented to a ministry that teaches it; and the Court's own phrases, "religious groups" and "the faith and mission of the church itself," name the consented matter. The congregation's free exercise carries the holding through the body, and the criterion is the reason the holding is right. *Trinity Lutheran*: the State's policy "expressly discriminates against otherwise eligible recipients by disqualifying them from a public benefit solely because of their religious character," and the Court said the church was "free to continue operating as a church" but at "the cost of automatic and absolute exclusion from the benefits of a public program for which the Center is otherwise fully qualified."[^247] The religious character is the congregation's; the benefit was open to all; the exclusion fell on the persons' exercise through the body's name, and the name was the medium of a first-set right. *Lukumi*: the Court's own first sentence sorts the case. "Petitioner Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. (Church), is a not-for-profit corporation organized under Florida law in 1973. The Church and its congregants practice the Santeria religion."[^248] The corporation is the grant, organized under the State's law; the practice is the Church's and its congregants', and the ordinances reached the congregants' own act. *Fulton*: Justice Barrett, concurring, asked the question this section answers: "Should entities like Catholic Social Services—which is an arm of the Catholic Church—be treated differently than individuals? Cf. *Hosanna-Tabor*."[^249] The answer is that the entity is treated differently from the individuals exactly to the extent of the grant and no further. What it holds by grant, its property, its contracts in one name, its capacity to be the City's contractor, the people may condition as they condition every grant; what it does as its members' consented faith, which the placement of children according to the Church's teaching is, is the persons' free exercise through the body, and the body's incorporation adds nothing to it and takes nothing from it. In each of the four the body is a church or an arm of one, the charter contains the faith, and the right vindicated is the members'. Where a church is incorporated, the incorporation is the people's grant for holding property and acting in one name; the members' worship is theirs; and nothing in the Free Exercise Clause attaches to the charter, which is what Madison's veto said in 1811 and *Hosanna-Tabor* repeated in 2012.

*Dale* is the case the criterion would have decided on the question the Court declined to ask, and the article measures its method and says nothing of its result. The Boy Scouts is not a church, and its instrument did not contain the message; the Court said so. The values the Scouts seek to instill are "based on" those listed in the Scout Oath and Law, and "the Scout Oath and Law do not expressly mention sexuality or sexual orientation," while the terms "morally straight" and "clean" "are by no means self-defining. Different people would attribute to those terms very different meanings."[^250] The message came from the officers. The Scouts "asserts" its teaching in its briefs; "We accept the Boy Scouts' assertion. We need not inquire further to determine the nature of the Boy Scouts' expression with respect to homosexuality"; and the written evidence the Court then consulted, "if only on the question of the sincerity of the professed beliefs," was a 1978 position statement "signed by Downing B. Jenks, the President of the Boy Scouts, and Harvey L. Price, the Chief Scout Executive."[^251] That is the step from the creed to the candidate, taken by the officers and deferred to by the Court. The deference sentence of 653, "we must also give deference to an association's view of what would impair its expression," with its own bound in the next sentence, "That is not to say that an expressive association can erect a shield against antidiscrimination laws simply by asserting that mere acceptance of a member from a particular group would impair its message," is the Court stating that the deference has a limit without saying where.[^252] The criterion says where: at the instrument the members joined under. It would have asked what the Oath and Law, as the members took them, contained, which is the question the Court declined to ask. "An association's view," taken from the officers for the members, is the aggregate word of Section III.I arriving in the law of associations, and the criterion is its correction in the Court's own vocabulary, since the Court had said in *Jaycees* that a forced-membership rule "may impair the ability of the original members to express only those views that brought them together."[^253] The original members, and the views that brought them together: that is the formation measure, and it asks after the instrument and not the officers. Whether the Scouts' members had in fact joined under the view the officers asserted is a question of their record, and the article does not answer it; it answers only that the Court did not ask.

The Court's own grammar in these cases is the criterion's. *Roberts v. United States Jaycees* opens, in Justice Brennan's words for the Court, with "the constitutional freedom of association asserted by members of a private organization"; the Jaycees are described as "a nonprofit membership corporation" whose objective is "set out in its bylaws," which the Court then reads; and the court below is described as having found a violation of "the First and Fourteenth Amendment rights of the organization's members."[^254] The right is the members'; the body's instrument is read for what the members joined under; and the group right is derived from the individual's in terms: "An individual's freedom to speak, to worship, and to petition the government for the redress of grievances could not be vigorously protected from interference by the State unless a correlative freedom to engage in group effort toward those ends were not also guaranteed," so that the Court has "long understood as implicit in the right to engage in activities protected by the First Amendment a corresponding right to associate with others in pursuit of a wide variety of political, social, economic, educational, religious, and cultural ends."[^255] On the next page the Court gave the formation measure in terms: a regulation that forces a group to accept members it does not desire "may impair the ability of the original members to express only those views that brought them together," and the statute's impact is measured, on the same page, on "the male members' associational freedoms." *Americans for Prosperity Foundation v. Bonta* holds that the State's demand "imposes a widespread burden on donors' associational rights," and says what triggers the protection: "not only … actual restrictions on an individual's ability to join with others to further shared goals."[^256] The donors', the individual's. *303 Creative* locates the right in the designer and treats her company as the form: "Ms. Smith offers her speech for pay and does so through 303 Creative LLC, a company in which she is 'the sole member-owner.' … But none of that makes a difference. … Nor, this Court has held, do speakers shed their First Amendment protections by employing the corporate form to disseminate their speech."[^257] *Masterpiece Cakeshop* is Phillips's case from its first page to its last, the Ltd. the petitioner in the caption and the person in every sentence: "Phillips was entitled to the neutral and respectful consideration of his claims"; the Commission's treatment showed "a clear and impermissible hostility toward the sincere religious beliefs that motivated his objection"; and "the Commission's treatment of Phillips' case violated the State's duty under the First Amendment."[^258] In each the Court located the right in persons, members, donors, a sole owner, a baker, and treated the body as the medium through which it was asserted. That is the derivative method in the modern Court, and it is the criterion's grammar: the right is the persons', the body carries it, and the charter or the terms of membership are read to find whose consent the body's act records.

The same opinions also write the body's name as the holder, and the article names those sentences rather than leaving them to the adversary. *Hobby Lobby* lists *Hosanna-Tabor* and *Lukumi* as "free-exercise claims brought by nonprofit corporations"; *Trinity Lutheran* writes of the institution, "free to continue operating as a church," and of its "religious character"; *Jaycees* speaks of "the group's freedom of expressive association" on the page that derives the group's freedom from the individual's; the sentence before the one quoted from *Citizens United* says that disclosure "permits citizens and shareholders to react to the speech of corporate entities in a proper way"; and *303 Creative* adds that the same fact "underlies our cases involving everything from movie producers to book publishers to newspapers."[^259] The article measures those sentences as Section IV.B measured the Fourth Amendment standing rule, as descriptions that name the name where the right is the persons', and takes the members' sentences on the same pages as the ground the Court itself gives; the media cases are Section IX.E's. The business treasury's cases alone speak otherwise throughout, and Section VI measures them.

### F. The Sovereign's Different Terms: The Exempt Class's Condition as *Regan* Reasons It, Four Replies Met in Advance, and the Two Cases That Bound the Treasury's Question Sorted by Both Bars

The bodies sorted above receive different terms from the sovereign, and the difference confirms the criterion rather than complicating it. The exempt class is defined by the people's statute, which lists the purposes an exempt body may pursue, forbids the inurement of its earnings to any private shareholder, limits its lobbying, and provides that the body is one "which does not participate in, or intervene in (including the publishing or distributing of statements), any political campaign on behalf of (or in opposition to) any candidate for public office."[^260] That is Section VI.I's exception in the form the exempt class takes. The exemption is the people's grant on the people's terms, and the bar on campaign intervention is a condition on the exempt pool's use, accepted at formation by every body that takes the exemption. The Court upheld the same structure for the same paragraph's lobbying condition in *Regan v. Taxation With Representation*, and said what such a condition is. "The Code does not deny TWR the right to receive deductible contributions to support its non-lobbying activity, nor does it deny TWR any independent benefit on account of its intention to lobby. Congress has merely refused to pay for the lobbying out of public moneys"; "Congress has not infringed any First Amendment rights or regulated any First Amendment activity. Congress has simply chosen not to pay for TWR's lobbying," and the Court rejected "the 'notion that First Amendment rights are somehow not fully realized unless they are subsidized by the State'"; "a legislature's decision not to subsidize the exercise of a fundamental right does not infringe the right, and thus is not subject to strict scrutiny," exemptions and deductions being "a matter of grace."[^261] The members' own speech stays open, as persons and through an affiliate that takes no exemption; the Court noted that the Service "apparently requires only that the two groups be separately incorporated and keep records adequate to show that tax-deductible contributions are not used to pay for lobbying," and found this "not unduly burdensome."[^262]

Justice Blackmun, concurring, made that the condition of the result: in his view the outcome "depends entirely upon the Court's necessary assumption—which I share—about the manner in which the Internal Revenue Service administers § 501," because the exempt body "is free to make known its views on legislation through its § 501(c)(4) affiliate without losing tax benefits for its nonlobbying activities," and "[a]ny significant restriction on this channel of communication, however, would negate the saving effect of § 501(c)(4)."[^263] That condition is the article's own point, that the persons' speech stays open through a body that takes no exemption, and the article's placement carries it. The line between a condition on the exempt pool and a penalty on the persons' exercise is the Court's own, drawn in *Regan* against *Speiser*: the government "may not deny a benefit to a person because he exercises a constitutional right," but a refusal to pay for the exercise out of public moneys denies no benefit on that account.[^264] *Trinity Lutheran* stands on the penalty side of that line, a benefit open to all withheld for religious character. The campaign-intervention condition, on which no opinion of the Court has passed, stands on *Regan*'s side by *Regan*'s reasoning, a term of the exempt grant accepted by the body that took it, with the affiliate open; the placement is the article's, by the Court's reasoning and under Blackmun's condition, and not a holding the article attributes to the Court. *Bonta* itself marks the difference when it says that "conferral of tax-exempt status may raise issues not presented by California's disclosure requirement," citing *Regan*.[^265] So the sovereign's different terms run exactly as Section VI.I says the exception runs. The measure is one-directional: a body that takes no exemption bears no condition from this ground, and the measure of consent, which is Section V.D's, is independent of it. An exempt church whose officers endorse a candidate in its name fails both measures at once, the charter's and the grant's; an unexempt association whose charter contains the endorsement fails neither; and a business corporation with the ordinary charter whose treasury speaks fails the first whatever its tax position, because its charter contains no speech, and fails the second to the extent of its exception (Section VI.I).

Four replies are met in advance. First, a body may amend its charter to adopt a political position. Then the consenting members' declaration of it is theirs through the body, and the criterion is permissive; but the treasury's spending remains the pool's, the dissenting members' money cannot be spent on it (Section VI.E's rule, which *Janus* states for the union), and if the body is exempt the people's terms still condition the pool's electoral use. Second, a doctrinal position may also be a political one. Where the creed contains it, its declaration is the members' act and the criterion protects it, however political the question; what the criterion refuses is the officers' inference from the creed to a candidate; and the members and the ministers remain free to endorse as the persons they are. Third, the business corporation whose shareholders unanimously endorse. The treasury is still the pool, by the rule that made it not theirs (Section VI.A's answer to the sole owner); the shareholders may distribute it and speak with their own money as their own at any scale; and consent by headcount never enters, because the criterion reads the instrument and not the room. Fourth, that the criterion gives churches more than companies. It gives persons exactly what they consented to and no more. A church whose officers endorse candidates in its name outside its creed usurps its members as a company's officers do, and the people's terms on the exempt pool reach it as they reach no unexempt body; a company whose shareholders adopt a declaration in the charter has, to that extent, a charter that contains speech, and the criterion treats it as it treats a church.

*Citizens United* fails on both bars, and *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* passes both, which is the whole of the difference between the two cases that the Court itself did not state. The exempt corporation of 1986 had a charter formed for the express purpose of promoting political ideas and a treasury that reflected popular support for them, in the three features Section VI.A quotes; its speech was its members' consented act, carried by a pool whose formation was the consent. The business treasury of 2010 had a charter that bound no contributor to any candidate and a pool that the people had granted on their standing terms; its speech was no member's act, and its weight was borrowed. The derivative theory, which the majority invoked by calling corporations "associations of citizens," had its answer in its own premise: an association speaks for its members only as its members consented to the speech, and the record of the consent is the charter. The Court has the criterion's two premises in its own opinions and the criterion's grammar in its association cases. What it has not done is hold them together, and holding them together is what this Part has done.

### G. The Theory's Own Witness: *Pembina*'s Reason, and *Citizens United*'s Two Uses of "Association"

The limit is not imposed on the theory from outside; the Court's own uses of the theory mark it. In *Pembina*, the one case that gave a reason for calling the corporation a "person," the reason was the aggregate theory, and the sentence that stated it said what the aggregate receives: corporations "are merely associations of individuals united for a special purpose, and permitted to do business under a particular name, and have a succession of members without dissolution."[^266] Associations of individuals, permitted, under a name, with succession. The theory that was to extend "person" to the corporation reaches, by its own words, the individuals and the permission and the incident, and nothing of the name's own.

In *Citizens United* the theory was invoked and denied in one opinion, in the two uses of "association" Section III.I set out: the corporation was its members' association when the question was whether it might speak,[^267] and a separate association from the citizens' own PAC when the question was whether their speaking sufficed.[^268] A derivative theory cannot be run in that direction. If the corporation's speech is the citizens' speech, then the citizens' speech through the PAC was the corporation's speech, and the statute left it open. Only a name can be both, and only because a name is whatever the sentence using it requires.

### H. What Is Left: Beyond the Edge a Right Is the Name's, and a Name Holds Rights Only by a Rule the People Never Made

The derivative theory survives as a true account of the first set and fails at its edge, and the association theory, which rightly asserts the group's reality, ends at the same edge, since the reality it asserts is that of persons acting together (Section V.C). For every claim beyond that edge, the right is held by the name, and a right held by a name exists only by a rule conferring it. The rule is the sovereign's. In the American order the sovereign is the Constitution the people ratified, the people sovereign through it and through the constitutions of their States, and the people have enacted no rule conferring constitutional rights on names. They enacted the opposite. They defined the corporation as the holder of powers individuals lack, they granted its existence under general laws, and they reserved the power to alter, amend, or repeal. What the Court attached to the name beyond the members' own rights it attached by a figure of speech or a transplanted word, and Section III has shown which. Section VII asks what remains when the figure is removed.

## VI. The Treasury Is Not a Speaker: *Citizens United* Run Through the Two Sets

Section IV sorted everything a corporation might claim as a constitutional right into two sets, what natural persons already hold in full before any charter and what incorporation alone confers, and found no third (Section IV.A); the sets are the measure here. The test Section VII states, which strikes the coined term and looks for the clause, comes after this case because the case supplies the term it strikes.

*Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission* is the case in which the Court conferred one, and it is the right case to examine because the line between the two sets ran through the middle of the statute. Congress had drawn it with care. On one side stood every natural person's speech and association, which the statute left untouched. On the other stood the general treasury, which the statute reached. The Court held that reaching the treasury abridged the freedom of speech. The question this section asks is whose.

### A. What the Statute Reached, and What It Left Alone: Every Natural Person's Speech Open, the General Treasury Alone Restricted

Section 441b of Title 2, as amended by the Bipartisan Campaign Reform Act of 2002, prohibited corporations and unions "from using their general treasury funds" to make independent expenditures for speech expressly advocating a candidate's election or defeat, or for electioneering communications within thirty or sixty days of an election.[^269] That was the whole of the prohibition. It did not forbid any officer, shareholder, or employee of a corporation from speaking. It did not forbid them from speaking together. It did not forbid them from pooling money to speak.

The statute said so in terms. Subsection (b)(2)(C) exempted from the ban "separate segregated funds established by a corporation for political purposes," the political action committee, which a corporation could create and administer, and which could be financed by voluntary contributions from the corporation's "stockholders and their families and its executive or administrative personnel."[^270] The Court had already held, in what it called "this Court's unanimous view," that "the ability to form and administer separate segregated funds … has provided corporations and unions with a constitutionally sufficient opportunity to engage in express advocacy."[^271] A second route stood open beside the first. A nonprofit advocacy corporation of the kind described in *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* could spend without limit, so that "if ideologically aligned individuals wish to make unlimited expenditures through the corporate form, they may utilize an MCFL organization."[^272]

Set these two routes against the two sets. The PAC is identified natural persons putting in their own money, knowingly, for political speech they chose. The persons speaking are the persons paying. That is assembly, speech, and the members' own property, which is the first set entire, and nothing in it depends on limited liability or perpetual succession. The MCFL organization is the same thing under a corporate name that the Court had already recognized as a medium. Both routes were open. Every right in the first set was available to every person connected with every corporation in the country on the day the case was argued.

What the statute left on the far side of the line was the general treasury alone. The general treasury is the pool of assets accumulated under the corporate form. It is shielded from the members' creditors by limited liability. It passes to no one at any member's death because of perpetual succession. It accumulates under the favorable treatment of accumulated assets that *Austin* named among the form's special advantages, in the sentence Section VI.H reads whole.[^273] Every one of those features is the sovereign's rule. The treasury exists in the form it has only because the rule exists. It is the second set, and it is nothing but the second set.

The objection to that sentence is the sole owner's, and it should be met where it is strongest. A corporation with one shareholder, or a family's, or a company whose every member directs the expenditure, has a treasury that is, in every economic sense, its members' money: they bear its gains and its losses, and Justice Stevens's own word for them is "the residual claimants."[^274] Why is such a treasury not the members' medium, carrying their own speech in the first set, so that § 441b reached first-set speech in their case and the ban was unconstitutional at least as applied to them? The answer is that the pool is not theirs by the very rule that made it. The shield exists only because the law says the pool is *not* the members': their creditors cannot reach it, their tort victims cannot, their deaths do not pass it, and no one of them can be sued for its debts.[^275] A member cannot have the pool be not theirs when they owe and theirs when they speak. That is the same word used two ways that Section V.G found in *Citizens United* itself, an association of citizens when the question was whether the corporation might speak and a separate association when the question was whether the citizens' PAC sufficed;[^276] only a name can be both, and the sole owner who claims the treasury's speech as their own while keeping the shield is asking the name to be both for them. The route the law leaves them is real: they may distribute the pool to themselves under the ordinary rule and speak with it as their own, at any scale, since a natural person's independent expenditure in their own name has been unlimited since *Buckley*.[^277] What they may not do is speak from behind the shield. The distribution's cost, the tax on the dividend and the loss of the shield over what is withdrawn, is not a burden laid on speech because it is speech, which is the class the Court condemns;[^278] it falls on withdrawal for any use, a vessel as much as a broadcast, and is therefore a condition on the privilege and not an abridgment of anyone's freedom. The same answer meets the majority's complaint that PACs are "burdensome alternatives," "expensive to administer and subject to extensive regulations":[^279] a burden on the convenience of a privilege is not an abridgment of a freedom that was never touched.

The Court has drawn this line itself, for the pool's papers, in a line of cases from 1906 to 1988 whose rule is unbroken and whose last case was decided five to four, and the line is the article's. In *Hale v. Henkel* the Court distinguished the individual, who "may stand upon his constitutional rights as a citizen" and "owes no duty to the State or to his neighbors to divulge his business," from the corporation, which "is a creature of the State," "presumed to be incorporated for the benefit of the public," receiving "certain special privileges and franchises" and holding them "subject to the laws of the State and the limitations of its charter"; and *Wilson v. United States* in 1911 quoted the passage whole and drew the consequence for the officer who holds the books. "The corporate form of business activity, with its chartered privileges, raises a distinction when the authority of government demands the examination of books"; "the corporation has no privilege to refuse. It cannot resist production upon the ground of self-crimination"; and its officers "may decline to utter upon the witness stand a single self-criminating word," but "the visitatorial power which exists with respect to the corporation of necessity reaches the corporate books without regard to the conduct of the custodian," because to hold otherwise "would not be a recognition, but an unjustifiable extension, of the personal rights they enjoy."[^280] That is the object-and-direction principle of Section IV.B in the Court's hands: the privilege is the person's own act and stays with the person; the books are the pool's and travel with the grant.

The Court then kept the line when the ground under it changed. In *White* it extended the rule to an unincorporated union, and *Braswell* says what that did: "the Court jettisoned reliance on the visitatorial powers of the State over corporations owing their existence to the State—one of the bases for earlier decisions."[^281] What survived the jettisoning is the rule the article stands on, which *Bellis* states from *White*: "individuals, when acting as representatives of a collective group, cannot be said to be exercising their personal rights and duties nor to be entitled to their purely personal privileges. Rather they assume the rights, duties and privileges of the artificial entity or association of which they are agents or officers"; the privilege "is a purely personal one"; and "an artificial entity can only act to produce its records through its individual officers or agents."[^282] *Bellis* then refused the count in terms, "It is well settled that no privilege can be claimed by the custodian of corporate records, regardless of how small the corporation may be," and *Braswell* restated the rule against the claim that the doctrine "does not apply when a corporation is so small that it constitutes nothing more than the individual's alter ego," adding that "it is well established that such artificial entities are not protected by the Fifth Amendment."[^283] The Court has reserved one case at the rule's edge, and the reservation is the article's principle and not an exception to it. *Braswell* left open "whether the agency rationale supports compelling a custodian to produce corporate records when the custodian is able to establish, by showing for example that he is the sole employee and officer of the corporation, that the jury would inevitably conclude that he produced the records."[^284] The reserved question concerns the person's act of production, which in a one-person corporation collapses into the person's own act, and never the pool's papers, which remain the body's: that is the object-and-direction line at its narrowest point, the act the person's and the thing the body's. And *Braswell* was five to four. Justice Kennedy wrote for the four that the privilege "is an explicit right of a natural person, protecting the realm of human thought and expression," and that the Court had denied "an individual his Fifth Amendment privilege against self-incrimination in order to vindicate the rule that a collective entity which employs him has no such privilege itself"; the rule was contested on the article's side, for the person, and never against the rule that the body has none.[^285] The article never needed the visitatorial power. What it needs is the rule that outlived it, that a person acting for a body is not exercising a personal right, which is Section IV.B's consent to the act stated from the other side; and the Court's two hedges on the pool's privacy, *Morton Salt*'s and *Oklahoma Press*'s, belong beside it (Sections IV.B and VII.C).[^286]

What the article takes from the line should be stated exactly, and so should what it does not. It takes the Court's sorting by the act and the Court's refusal of headcount for the pool's papers. It does not take the privilege's result and carry it into another clause, because the Court confined the collective-entity rule to the guarantees it called "purely personal" and put speech outside them in terms, in *Bellotti*'s footnote and Justice O'Connor's inventory, which Section VII.C quotes; her criterion is that paying a penalty, unlike remaining silent, "is something that a corporation can do as an entity."[^287] That is the Court's line. Section VI.G's fourth step contests it for the negative claim, where O'Connor's own words place the ability to remain silent among the things a corporation cannot do as an entity. For the sole owner the Court's line and the article's agree. The custodian of a one-person corporation's books has no privilege "regardless of how small the corporation may be," because the books are the body's; the treasury is the body's by the same rule, the shield that makes the pool not the owner's for the owner's debts; and when *Citizens United* counted "for-profit corporations with only single shareholders" among the reasons the statute was overinclusive, it counted heads where the rule counts the shield, since the single shareholder who keeps the shield keeps the pool as the body's and may distribute it and speak with it as a person at any scale.[^288] Headcount never enters the article's sorting, and where the Court has counted, the count was of the wrong thing.

The sorting the article needs is therefore not by the number of members but by what the law reached. A law that compels a person's act through the name reaches the person, and the name is the medium: Alabama demanded the members' names, and the compulsion reached the members' own association through the Association's name;[^289] the contraceptive mandate compelled the companies, as employers, to arrange coverage under penalty, and through them the owners, and the Court found the burden on "the humans who own and control those companies."[^290] *NAACP* is a first-set case, and the article claims it. *Hobby Lobby* the article claims differently and more exactly (Section V.A): the Court reached the companies through a statute's "person," the people's own rule for the name, and the burden it found was on the persons, whose exercise through the name their own instruments recorded (Section V.D); the sorting there is by the source of the claim, as it is here, and never by the count. A law that conditions the use of the rule's own product, compelling no one, reaches the rule: § 441b compelled no person to speak or to be silent in their own name, and reached the pool's spending and nothing else. That is a second-set case, and the condition was the grantor's to impose. The Court itself once sorted a pool by its formation rather than by the identity of its holder. The advocacy corporation in *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* could spend from its treasury because of "three features essential to our holding": it "was formed for the express purpose of promoting political ideas, and cannot engage in business activities," which "ensures that political resources reflect political support"; it "has no shareholders or other persons affiliated so as to have a claim on its assets or earnings"; and it "was not established by a business corporation or a labor union."[^291] "Voluntary political associations," the Court said on the page before, "do not suddenly present the specter of corruption merely by assuming the corporate form,"[^292] which is to say that such a treasury is persons' pooling under a corporate name and not the shield's product. The PAC is the same thing: identified persons' money, put in for this speech, an agreement among persons, which is the first set. The general treasury is what no agreement among persons can make. Consent enters the analysis at the pool's formation and nowhere else; it never enters by headcount.

### B. What the Court Held, in Its Own Words: "A PAC Is a Separate Association from the Corporation," and the Forfeiture Sentence at Page 351

The majority did not deny that the members could speak. It held that their speaking was not enough, because the corporation could not. The sentence is at page 337:

> A PAC is a separate association from the corporation. So the PAC exemption from § 441b's expenditure ban, § 441b(b)(2), does not allow corporations to speak. Even if a PAC could somehow allow a corporation to speak—and it does not—the option to form PACs does not alleviate the First Amendment problems with § 441b.[^293]

The first of those sentences is true, and it is beside the point unless a corporation is something that speaks, a premise Section VI.G returns to. Read the passage against the first set. It concedes that the association of citizens behind the corporation was free to speak through the PAC. It holds that this concession is beside the point, because "the corporation" is a speaker distinct from that association, and the statute silenced it. The speaker whose right the Court vindicated is therefore not the natural persons, whose speech was never restricted. It is the name, holding the treasury.

The majority then addressed the second set directly. *Austin* had distinguished corporations from wealthy individuals on the ground that "[s]tate law grants corporations special advantages—such as limited liability, perpetual life, and favorable treatment of the accumulation and distribution of assets."[^294] The majority answered in one sentence: "This does not suffice, however, to allow laws prohibiting speech. 'It is rudimentary that the State cannot exact as the price of those special advantages the forfeiture of First Amendment rights.'"[^295]

That sentence presupposes the thing in dispute. For the special advantages to be the "price" of a "forfeiture," the corporation must already hold a First Amendment right that could be forfeited. The sentence does not establish that it does. It assumes it, and then treats a condition on the grant as a penalty on the right. But the only right the statute could have reached was one the name held as itself, because the members' rights were untouched. If the name holds no such right, there was nothing to forfeit. What the statute actually did was condition the sovereign's own grant: you may accumulate under limited liability and perpetual succession, and you may not spend the accumulation on candidate elections. Conditioning a grant is what a grantor does. The Court had said so for the municipal corporation, which "remains the creature of the State exercising and holding powers and privileges subject to the sovereign will,"[^296] and it had said so for the private corporation's claim to be a citizen, confined in *Paul* to "natural persons, members of the body politic" and denied to "artificial persons created by the legislature" (Section III.D).[^297] The majority at page 351 treated the condition as censorship of a rights-holder, and the rights-holder is a word.

The Court had answered page 351 a century before, in the case this article owns with its use named. "In creating a corporation a State may withhold powers which may be exercised by and cannot be denied to an individual," the Court said in *Berea College*, upholding the Day Law as applied to the corporation alone, in the sentences Section IV.B quotes whole and whose use it names.[^298] The sentence is the second set stated by the Court: what the name holds, the grantor may withhold or condition, and a condition on a grant is not a price exacted for a right the grantee holds, because the grantee holds none of its own. The article takes the rule and names its use in each place it cites it, as Section IV.B does at length: the rule was used to segregate a college because the Court held only half of it, and let a condition on the name's power reach the teachers' and students' own act. Page 351 is the other half held alone.
