The majority treated a condition on the name's grant as a penalty on a right, and so found a holder where *Berea* had rightly found none; *Berea* had denied the persons what page 351 would give the name. *Pierce* holds both halves in two sentences and is the measure of each, as Section V.A quotes them: the liberty the corporations could not claim for themselves, and the business and property for which they claimed protection, the liberty vindicated being the parents'.[^299] Read against page 351, *Pierce* says what the statute could and could not reach: the name's business and property, the second set, which the sovereign's condition may touch, and the persons' liberty, the first, which the statute left whole. The sentence the majority quoted at 351 is Justice Scalia's, dissenting in *Austin*, and it assumes the one thing *Pierce* and *Berea* agree in denying: a right of the name's own for the condition to forfeit.[^300]

One reply must be met here, because *Berea*'s sentence is the creating State's. Section 441b is Congress's, and Congress chartered none of the corporations it reached, so the adversary says that Congress conditioned a grant it never made. The answer is Section VI.I's. The federal condition rides on the people's federal grant, the exception in their own revenue law that excepts the treasury from the charge persons bear, and on the national instrument the treasury spends; and the people are one sovereign through both instruments, so that the States' grants, in the Court's words, "cannot repeal the Constitution," and by the same reasoning cannot enlarge it. A condition the people lay through Congress on the use of their own exception is a grantor's condition in the only sense this article uses (Sections VI.I and VII.C).[^301]

The sentence is the unconstitutional-conditions doctrine, and the article names it so that no reader can say it was missed. *Frost & Frost Trucking* in 1926 held that what a State may not do "by words of express divestment" it may not accomplish "under the guise of a surrender of a right in exchange for a valuable privilege which the state threatens otherwise to withhold"; *Western Union Telegraph* in 1910 had held that a foreign corporation admitted to local business could not be made to surrender its federal exemption as the price of staying, "any more than it would have been bound to surrender any other right secured by the National Constitution," and that "any such condition was unconstitutional and void"; and *Alliance for Open Society* in 2013 drew the line between "conditions that define the limits of the government spending program" and "conditions that seek to leverage funding to regulate speech outside the contours of the program itself."[^302] The doctrine forbids a government to condition a privilege on the surrender of a right the holder of the privilege has. It cannot create a holder. Where the condition left every natural person's own money and speech untouched, the PAC and the advocacy corporation's pool, there is no person whose right the condition exacts, and the only forfeiture is of a right the name would have to hold as itself, which is the question.

### C. The Shareholders: Speech Funded by Persons Who Oppose It Derives from No One

The point is not an abstraction, because the Court was told who would pay for the speech it protected. Justice Stevens, dissenting, put it at page 475:

> When corporations use general treasury funds to praise or attack a particular candidate for office, it is the shareholders, as the residual claimants, who are effectively footing the bill. Those shareholders who disagree with the corporation's electoral message may find their financial investments being used to undermine their political convictions. The PAC mechanism, by contrast, helps ensure that those who pay for an electioneering communication actually support its content and that managers do not use general treasuries to advance personal agendas.[^303]

This closes the one route by which the majority's holding might have been brought back inside the first set. If treasury speech were the members' own speech carried through a medium, every member would be a willing source of it, as every member of the NAACP was a willing source of the association's claim to keep its membership list private. Dissenting shareholders show that the treasury's speech derives from no one. It is officers spending second-set assets in the name's voice, funded by persons who oppose what is said. A right that derives from no member cannot come from the first set, and a second-set incident is not a constitutional right. The right the majority protected has no source in either. It is the residue the two-set analysis says cannot exist, and the majority created it.

The interest in protecting those shareholders was not new. The Court had recognized it in *National Right to Work Committee* as the interest in "protect[ing] the individuals who have paid money into a corporation or union for purposes other than the support of candidates from having that money used to support political candidates to whom they may be opposed,"[^304] and in *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* as the protection of "members or shareholders who may disagree with the object of the expenditures."[^305] The majority disposed of it in two sentences. At page 362: "There is, furthermore, little evidence of abuse that cannot be corrected by shareholders 'through the procedures of corporate democracy.' … Those reasons are sufficient to reject this shareholder-protection interest."[^306] At page 370: "Shareholder objections raised through the procedures of corporate democracy … can be more effective today because modern technology makes disclosures rapid and informative."[^307]

### D. "Corporate Democracy": A Presumption of 1978, Applied in 2010 Without a Record

The phrase in the majority's quotation marks is a term of art with a known origin. It entered the Reports in 1978, in the sentence of *First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti* that the census quotes (Section III.H).[^308] The next page explained what the procedures were: "Acting through their power to elect the board of directors or to insist upon protective provisions in the corporation's charter, shareholders normally are presumed competent to protect their own interests. In addition to intracorporate remedies, minority shareholders generally have access to the judicial remedy of a derivative suit."[^309] A footnote distinguished the compelled-subsidy cases on the ground that union members were "required … to pay dues," whereas investment in a corporation is "voluntary," and observed that on that record "[n]ot a single shareholder" had objected.[^310]

Consider the verbs. Shareholders *may* decide. They *normally are presumed* competent. They *generally have access* to a derivative suit. The procedures *can be* more effective today. Not one of these is a finding that the procedures exist in any given corporation or work in any. *Bellotti* stated a presumption and said so in the word "presumed." *Citizens United* applied the presumption as if it were conclusive, to reject a governmental interest that Congress had found on a legislative record, and it did so, as the dissent observed, with "no record" and "just the majority's own unsupported factfinding."[^311] A rebuttable presumption of remedial adequacy was used to deny a constitutional protection without the presumed fact ever being found.

What the presumption presumes is also worth stating. Justice Stevens read "corporate democracy" as "the rights of shareholders to vote and to bring derivative suits for breach of fiduciary duty," and reported what practitioners say of them: "these rights are so limited as to be almost nonexistent, given the internal authority wielded by boards and managers and the expansive protections afforded by the business judgment rule."[^312] The shareholder's remedy for having their money spent against their convictions is therefore a vote that management controls, a suit the business judgment rule defeats, or exit, which is selling the shares. Exit is an opt-out. The dissenting shareholder keeps their First Amendment protection only by leaving.

The presumption also answers the wrong question. "Corporate democracy" is offered as the dissenting shareholder's remedy, and the article has shown it to be a presumption and not a finding. But the harm the shareholder suffers is not only the loss of money to a cause they oppose; it is the attribution, which Section V.D stated: speech in the name claims a consent no member gave. A vote the board controls and a suit the business judgment rule defeats do not restore a consent that was never given. They are procedures for the money, not for the attribution, and exit by sale leaves the attribution standing for every share that remains. The majority rejected the shareholder-protection interest as a ground for restricting speech and left "other regulatory mechanisms" open (Section V.D; page 362), and the people's conditions on their own grant are such a mechanism. The usurpation is the ground the presumption never reaches, because "corporate democracy" presumes that the members' consent is there to be exercised, and the charter says it never was.

### E. The Court's Own Standard, Applied to the Other Collective: *Abood*, *Hudson*, *Knox* and *Janus* Against the Shareholder's Exit

The Court has a settled standard for exactly this situation, and it is not a presumption. When the collective is a union, and the question is whether a natural person may be made to fund political speech they oppose, the Court does the opposite of presuming the remedy adequate.

In *Abood*, the Court held that a union's expenditures for "the advancement of other ideological causes not germane to its duties as exclusive bargaining representative" must be "financed from charges, dues, or assessments paid by employees who do not object to advancing those ideas and who are not coerced into doing so against their will by the threat of loss of governmental employment."[^313] In *Chicago Teachers Union v. Hudson*, the Court did not presume that the union's procedures for objectors were adequate; it prescribed them. "We hold today that the constitutional requirements for the Union's collection of agency fees include an adequate explanation of the basis for the fee, a reasonably prompt opportunity to challenge the amount of the fee before an impartial decisionmaker, and an escrow for the amounts reasonably in dispute while such challenges are pending."[^314] The union's own voluntary escrow arrangement "does not cure all of the problems in the original procedure."[^315] In *Knox v. Service Employees*, the Court held that an opt-out structure was itself insufficient: a union "may not exact any funds from nonmembers without their affirmative consent," and requiring objecting nonmembers to opt out "as opposed to exempting them from making such payments unless they opt in" was "a remarkable boon for unions," since "[a]n opt-out system creates a risk that the fees paid by nonmembers will be used to further political and ideological ends with which they do not agree."[^316] In *Janus*, the Court completed the line: a waiver of the right not to subsidize "cannot be presumed," it "must be freely given and shown by 'clear and compelling' evidence," and "[u]nless employees clearly and affirmatively consent before any money is taken from them, this standard cannot be met."[^317]

Set the two standards side by side. For the union collective, the Court prescribed the dissenter's procedures itself, rejected the collective's own remedy as inadequate, refused opt-out, and forbade presumed consent. For the corporate collective, the Court presumed the procedures adequate, accepted that they "can be" effective, left the shareholder to exit or to a vote the dissent called almost nonexistent, and presumed consent from the "voluntary" character of investment, a premise that does not survive the observation that the second-set rule locks the capital in after entry, so that consent at purchase is not consent to each expenditure afterward, which is precisely the point on which *Knox* and *Janus* turned for union members. The two treatments cannot both be the First Amendment's measure of one interest, and the article is exact about what is being compared. It does not claim that the shareholder holds a First Amendment right against the board. Congress found a governmental interest in protecting the shareholder, the "shareholder-protection interest" the Court names and rejects at page 362, and the Court disposed of that interest by a presumption it refuses to apply to the identical interest when the collective is a union, where it prescribes the dissenter's procedures itself.[^318] The comparison is of the Court's treatment of two legislative interests in the same persons' money, not of two constitutional claims. And the State is in the room in both: the compulsion the union cases condemn is a statute's, and the lock-in that makes the shareholder's money the name's, no withdrawal, no direction, exit only by sale, is the sovereign's own rule, the second set, enacted by the general incorporation law the people's constitutions commanded. Between 1978 and 2018 nothing changed in the text. The only variable is the identity of the speaker. In the union cases the Court looked through the collective to the natural persons who paid, and protected them. In the corporate case it did not look through, because the speaker in its account was the corporation, and a corporation that is a speaker in its own right has no dissenting members, only assets.

### F. The Question the Court Did Not Ask: Whose Speech, and Under Which Clause

*Citizens United* is not, on this analysis, a case about how much speech money may buy, and the article does not join the argument the majority rejected at page 350, that the government may restrict some voices "in order to enhance the relative voice of others."[^319] That argument concedes what is in dispute. It treats the treasury's holder as a speaker with a right and asks only that the right be turned down. The article's claim is prior, and Sections VI.A, VI.B and VI.D have stated it.

The clause that protects the speaker in *Citizens United* does not exist. The holding rests on the word.

### G. The Clause Names No Holder: The Freedom Is Someone's, the Hearer's Interest Is First-Set, and the Coined Term Is "Speaker"

The strongest answer to the two sets is not *Pembina*'s but *Bellotti*'s, and the article meets it in the Court's own words. "The proper question therefore is not whether corporations 'have' First Amendment rights and, if so, whether they are coextensive with those of natural persons. Instead, the question must be whether § 8 abridges expression that the First Amendment was meant to protect."[^320] The Speech Clause restrains Congress; it names no person, no citizen, no people, and no holder. On this account the two sets answer a question the Court declined to ask, and the removal test, run honestly, would have nothing against the holding: strike every term in the census and what remains is a statute that silenced political speech the public would otherwise have heard, and a clause that forbids Congress to abridge the freedom of speech. *Citizens United* rests on exactly this line: political speech does not lose First Amendment protection "simply because its source is a corporation."[^321]

Four steps meet it, each in the text's own grammar.

First, the clause presupposes a holder though it names none. "The freedom of speech" is a freedom, with the definite article, and a freedom is someone's. The Court's own listener cases say whose. "Freedom of speech presupposes a willing speaker. But where a speaker exists, as is the case here, the protection afforded is to the communication, to its source and to its recipients both."[^322] The recipients were natural persons who wished to hear; the source was a pharmacist who would advertise; "if there is a right to advertise, there is a reciprocal right to receive the advertising, and it may be asserted by these appellees."[^323] A clause that forbids Congress to abridge *the freedom* forbids it to abridge a freedom natural persons have. It says nothing about a sovereign's grant to a name, because the name has no freedom to abridge.

Second, the hearer's interest is first-set, and the statute served it. Whatever a hearer may claim is a natural person's claim and sits in the first set. Every word the general treasury would have bought could reach the same hearer through the PAC the statute left open, from the same persons, in the same words, as Section VI.A showed. The Court has said that a hearer's right is not defeated because the message could be had by other means,[^324] but that principle protects a speaker's message from being rationed, and here no speaker's message was rationed: the persons who wished to speak could speak, and could pool their speech. The only speech § 441b kept from any hearer was speech that no natural person had chosen to fund. The listener theory therefore proves the article's point rather than the Court's. The hearer lost nothing a natural person had offered.

Third, the Court's sentence smuggles the holder back. "If the speakers here were not corporations, no one would suggest that the State could silence their proposed speech," *Bellotti* said, and the speech's worth "does not depend upon the identity of its source, whether corporation, association, union, or individual."[^325] The sentence assumes what the two sets deny: that a name is a speaker, a source with an identity. Strike the name as a speaker and the sentence reads: the speech comes from officers spending assets accumulated under a sovereign's rule, funded in part by persons who oppose it. That is not a source the First Amendment protects, because it is not a source. It is a medium, in the Court's own word of 1958, with no one behind the particular words. The coined term in *Citizens United* is therefore not "person," which the Fourteenth Amendment line supplied, but "speaker," the name used as the subject of the verb to speak; Section VII.B runs the test on that word. The Court has never said that every guarantee belongs to a corporation. In the footnote that rejected the appellee's "creatures of the State" argument as "an extreme position," *Bellotti* said that "purely personal" guarantees are unavailable to corporations, and that whether a guarantee is so limited "depends on the nature, history, and purpose of the particular constitutional provision"; Section VII.C quotes the footnote whole.[^326] The nature of the freedom of speech is that it is someone's; its history is a natural person's; its purpose, on the Court's own listener account, is a natural person's hearing. The Court's test, applied to the Court's clause, reaches the people who speak and hear, and stops.

Fourth, the test must be run on the negative claim, because the strongest form of the adversary's case is not a corporation's right to speak but its right not to. *Pacific Gas & Electric* is the case. California's commission ordered a regulated utility to carry a ratepayer group's messages in its billing envelopes, and a plurality of the Court held the order a violation of the utility's First Amendment rights. Justice Powell, announcing the judgment in an opinion joined by Chief Justice Burger and Justices Brennan and O'Connor, wrote that "[t]he identity of the speaker is not decisive in determining whether speech is protected," that "[f]or corporations as for individuals, the choice to speak includes within it the choice of what not to say," and that "[w]ere the government freely able to compel corporate speakers to propound political messages with which they disagree, this protection would be empty."[^327] A right not to speak is the purest right of participation, because its object is nothing but the self: a person's refusal to utter what they do not believe. By Section IV.B it cannot be held by a name. By Section V.D the utility's charter contains no speech, so there is no member whose consented declaration the envelope could carry or betray; the ratepayers joined under no creed, and the employees who filled the envelopes said nothing. Strike "speaker" as applied to the name and what remains is this. The envelopes were the utility's property, which the article does not dispute: "The envelopes themselves, the bills, and *Progress* all remain appellant's property." Five Justices in three opinions rejected by name the argument that the utility's regulated status lessened its right, the plurality because a regulated monopoly's position "does not decrease the informative value of its opinions on critical public matters," Justice Marshall because passing overhead to ratepayers "cannot affect the utility's ownership of its property, nor its right to use that property for expressive purposes," and the Chief Justice because the envelopes were "property like the mailing envelopes acquired by Pacific from its income and resources"; and the article does not rest on that argument.[^328] It rests on the thing the property does not supply, which is a conscience. The plurality's own reason for rejecting the regulated-status argument is the informative value of the utility's opinions to hearers, and a right not to carry another's message serves no hearer: the First Amendment, in the plurality's words, "protects the public's interest in receiving information," and a hearer's interest cannot ground a right to keep hearers from hearing.[^329]

Nor was any natural person made to say anything. The Commission required TURN "to state that its messages are not those of appellant," so the speech was TURN's, in TURN's name, disclaimed; what was attributed to the utility and its shareholders was the envelope and the return address, and Section V.D's harm, speech in a body's name claiming its members' consent, has no purchase on a disclaimed insert.[^330] What the plurality feared was that the utility would "feel compelled to respond," and the response it had in mind was the pool's newsletter, no person's own speech. And the conscience ground had four votes, not five. Justice Marshall concurred in the judgment on the ground that California "has taken from appellant the right to deny access to its property—its billing envelope," a redefinition of a property right in the extra space, so that the narrowest ground of the judgment is property and not conscience; and the plurality's own footnote states the article's question without answering it, that "the public's ownership of the 'extra space' does not nullify the First Amendment rights of the owner of the property from which that space derives," which assumes that the owner, being a name, has the right in question.[^331]

Justice Rehnquist's dissent, in the Part he wrote for himself alone, is the article's argument in the Reports. "Extension of the individual freedom of conscience decisions to business corporations strains the rationale of those cases beyond the breaking point. To ascribe to such artificial entities an 'intellect' or 'mind' for freedom of conscience purposes is to confuse metaphor with reality"; corporate speech rights, he wrote, "do not arise because corporations, like individuals, have any interest in self-expression," but were recognized "as an instrumental means of furthering the First Amendment purpose of fostering a broad forum of information to facilitate self-government"; and "the two constitutional liberties most closely analogous to the right to refrain from speaking—the Fifth Amendment right to remain silent and the constitutional right of privacy—have been denied to corporations based on their corporate status."[^332] That is the object-and-direction line drawn by a Justice, and it is older than the Republic. The common law said in 1614 what the dissent said in 1986. In *Tipling v. Pexall*, Coke, reciting Chief Baron Manwood's opinion, said of corporations "that they were invisible, immortall, and that they had no soule; and therefore no subpœna lieth against them, because they have no conscience nor soule; a corporation, is a body aggregate, none can create soules but God, but the King creates them, and therefore they have no soules; they cannot speak, nor appear in person, but by attorney."[^333] And in *Sutton's Hospital* two years before, in Coke's own report, a corporation aggregate "cannot commit treason, nor be outlawed, nor excommunicate, for they have no souls, neither can they appear in person, but by attorney."[^334] Two things are in those sentences. The first is the common law's rule of treatment, that a name acts only through natural persons, "by attorney," which Section II.D received and Section IV.B applied; and the report's own grammar answers the adversary who reads "by attorney" as mere agency, since Manwood's "therefore" runs from the incapacity to the procedure. A body appears by attorney because it has no person and no conscience of its own, and the procedure records the incapacity, as *Sutton's Hospital* says in the same breath: "for they have no souls, neither can they appear in person, but by attorney." The second is the possible-matter bound of Section II.D stated in 1614: the king can create the body and not the soul, so the body has no conscience to be compelled, and a law that feigned one would feign the impossible. "To confuse metaphor with reality" is "none can create soules but God" in a modern voice, and the Court's own criteria say the same thing a third time. *Bellotti*'s "purely personal" guarantees, O'Connor's "unlike the ability to remain silent," and Rehnquist's "metaphor with reality" are three statements of one rule, that a name has no conscience to be compelled, and the fourth step rests on that rule and on nothing else.

The corporate-speech cases after *Bellotti* sort by the same step, one sentence each. *National Institute of Family and Life Advocates* and *303 Creative* compel an act of identified persons, the clinics' staff who must post the State's notice and the designer who must write, and they sit in the first set under Section VI.A's source rule; the Court's own words place them there, the notice "targets speakers, not speech," and speakers do not "shed their First Amendment protections" by employing the corporate form, as Section V.E quotes the sentence, though the Court's "speakers" names the covered facilities and the article measures the description and takes the act, which was the staff's.[^335] *Consolidated Edison* and *Central Hudson* are the pool's political and commercial speech. The Court held that the bill-insert restriction "cannot be upheld on the ground that Consolidated Edison is not entitled to freedom of speech," a sentence that names the body as the holder and that the article measures as Section IV.B measured the standing rule; *Central Hudson* gives the ground the article takes, that "[t]he First Amendment's concern for commercial speech is based on the informational function of advertising," which is the hearers' first-set interest in the Court's own words, carrying whatever survives and nothing of the name's.[^336] *Sorrell* is the same with data as the speech: "the creation and dissemination of information are speech within the meaning of the First Amendment," the creators' and the hearers' interest, the pool the vehicle.[^337] *Pacific Gas & Electric* alone claims a conscience for the name, and it is the one that fails outright. Section IX.A's "measuring" is thereby argued and not asserted: run the test Section VII states, strike the name as a speaker from each of these, and the first-set interests stand where they stood, and the one holding that rested on the name's own mind has nothing under it.

### H. The Two Bonds: The Citizen's Standing Is Settled, the Name's Is a Grant, and the Sentence at Page 351 Made Them One

Section VI.A measured page 351 by what the statute reached, and Section VI.G by whose the freedom is. A third measure remains, and it is the oldest in the American vocabulary: the bond between the sovereign and those it taxes. The adversary's reply to that measure should be stated before the measure is, because it is the strongest reply the article has had to meet. The colonists' maxim, it runs, cuts the other way. A corporation is taxed; the Court has said so since 1911, describing the federal corporation tax as "an excise upon the particular privilege of doing business in a corporate capacity."[^338] It is not represented: it has no vote and no representative, and the article insists on both. If taxation without representation was the wrong that made the Republic, then a taxed name is owed a voice, and the treasury's political spending is the only voice it has. On this reply the two bonds are the article's own argument turned against it.

The answer is that the maxim never had a name for its object. Blackstone stated it of the subject, and the Declaration of "us," in the sentences Section II.E quoted; its object is persons, and the persons behind every corporate treasury are represented already, as the shareholders and employees they are, each a person in the settled bond and most of them citizens with the vote, and nothing a share costs them has reduced either the vote or the voice. What the name bears is not a tax on a person. It is, in the Court's own description of the tax the adversary relies on, a charge on a privilege, laid in *Flint*'s words "upon the privileges which exist in conducting business with the advantages which inhere in the corporate capacity of those taxed," the advantages being the four Section IV.C lists from the same page.[^339] The name pays that charge. The classical corporation pays the tax laid "on the taxable income of every corporation," and its shareholders pay again when the income reaches them as dividends; what this section calls an exception is the grantor's further term on that charge, and the word means exactly that throughout.[^340] A charge on a grant is a condition of the grant, and a condition of a grant is owed nothing by the grantor beyond the grant's own terms. The Court said that too, in 1908, of a State creating a corporation, in *Berea*'s sentence that the State "is under no obligation to treat both alike," the rule the Court used, in that case, to uphold a State's segregation of a college, as Section IV.B records; the article takes the rule and names the use.[^341] So the reply inverts the maxim's object. A name owed a voice for bearing a charge on its privilege would be a grantee owed a right against its grantor for bearing the grant's condition, which is the proposition Part One found nowhere in the law of the grant and Section II.E found impossible under a sovereign of many wills. And what the people granted was never the tax. It was the privilege itself, which the general incorporation laws extended until, in Brandeis's record of them, which Section IV.C quotes, the privilege had been extended to every lawful business,[^342] and, where the grantor chose, the exception from the charge upon it. The citizen is taxed on all lawful business as a person; the body formed for any lawful business is taxed on its privilege, at a rate and on a base the grantor sets, and excepted from either where the grantor chooses. That difference is the two bonds, and the rest of this section states them.

The citizen's standing in the bond is written, and it is closed. The citizen is taxable as a person, under the power to lay taxes on incomes "from whatever source derived."[^343] The citizen is represented in a House the people choose and, since 1913, in a Senate "elected by the people thereof."[^344] The citizen votes under four amendments, and each of the four says "citizens": the right "of citizens of the United States to vote" shall not be denied or abridged on account of race, on account of sex, "by reason of failure to pay any poll tax or other tax," or, for those eighteen years of age or older, on account of age.[^345] The citizen speaks under a clause that names no holder, and Section VI.G has said whose the freedom is. The citizen holds what is not enumerated, under an amendment that keeps the unenumerated rights "retained by the people."[^346] And the citizen's standing changes in one way only, by the amendment Article V describes. The rate and the exceptions are another matter. They are ordinary law, for persons and names alike, set and changed by the deliberation every year, and the article claims nothing else for them. Every term of the standing runs to a person. The bond's two oldest American statements, Blackstone's at page 135 and the Declaration's grievance, are in Section II.E, where they ground the thesis that the sovereign of the United States is the Constitution the people ratified, the people sovereign through it; what this section adds is that the bond they state is a bond of persons, with the person's standing in it fixed by the text and placed beyond ordinary means. The article calls it the settled bond. Its members are taxable, represented, voting and speaking; the deliberation that sets the rate is theirs; and an exception made to one of them is an exception within the bond, made by the deliberation to a member of it.

The corporation's terms are of another kind. They are a grant on the people's standing terms, as Section II.E stated and Section II.G showed in the people's own words; the grant is conditioned and may be resumed, under the reservations Appendix A collects and the dissolutions Blackstone lists (Section IV.E); and it is bound to the purpose for which it was made (Section II.F). The name is not a citizen, and the Court kept that word from it, in every clause that confers a right, for one hundred and eighty years; the diversity cases gave the name the word's effect for jurisdiction, by the presumption Section VII.C passed through the bounds, and nothing more. The name, Taney wrote for the Court in 1861, restating *Bank of Augusta v. Earle*, "can have no legal existence out of the bounds of the sovereignty by which it is created"; the rest of the passage, and its restatement of *Deveaux* that a corporation is not a citizen, Section III.K quotes.[^347] Seven years earlier Grier, for the Court, had granted the premise even as he reached past it: the inference that an artificial entity cannot be a citizen follows from premises he did not deny.[^348] The name has no vote and no representative. It is taxed on its privilege and not on its being, as *Flint* said, and it pays that charge; what the grantor excepts from the charge is the grantor's further term on its own product. The privilege carries the exceptions. Scalia named them himself, dissenting in *Austin*, in the passage the Court adopted at page 351: those who form a corporation "are, to be sure, given special advantages—notably, the immunization of their personal fortunes from liability for the actions of the association—that the State is under no obligation to confer. But so are other associations and private individuals given all sorts of special advantages that the State need not confer, ranging from tax breaks to contract awards to public employment to outright cash subsidies."[^349] Nothing in the text attaches a vote or a voice to any of it, and the dissent's own words concede the grant. The terms of this bond are the grantor's to set, and they are set in the general laws and the revenue acts, not in the instrument; and where the settled bond's member holds a standing the text fixes, the name holds an existence and incidents the people may withdraw. An exception to a person is made within the bond; an exception to a name is a further term of the grant. The article calls it the unsettled bond. Nothing in it is a fiction held as law: Appendix A is the sovereign disposing, in words, within its power, and the revenue acts are the same.

The Court made the two bonds one in a single paragraph, and the paragraph should be read whole. At page 351 the Court recites *Austin*'s ground, that "[s]tate law grants corporations special advantages—such as limited liability, perpetual life, and favorable treatment of the accumulation and distribution of assets," and answers, "This does not suffice, however, to allow laws prohibiting speech." It adopts Scalia's sentence: "It is rudimentary that the State cannot exact as the price of those special advantages the forfeiture of First Amendment rights." It declares it "irrelevant for purposes of the First Amendment that corporate funds may 'have little or no correlation to the public's support for the corporation's political ideas.'" And then: "All speakers, including individuals and the media, use money amassed from the economic marketplace to fund their speech. The First Amendment protects the resulting speech, even if it was enabled by economic transactions with persons or entities who disagree with the speaker's ideas," with Kennedy's *Austin* dissent in support: "Many persons can trace their funds to corporations, if not in the form of donations, then in the form of dividends, interest, or salary."[^350] That sentence is the two bonds made one. The individual's money was earned in the settled bond and taxed as a person's; the treasury's was amassed under the grant and taxed on the privilege at the grantor's rate, less whatever the grantor excepted; the word "speakers" placed them in one class, and the cited dissent shows how. The person who traces their funds to a corporation received them as a dividend, as interest or as salary, which is to say as their own, in the settled bond, taxed as income from whatever source derived. The treasury that holds the rest never crossed into that bond. The paragraph treats the crossing as made.

The "as if" had been written twice before, by a Justice writing separately each time, and Section II.D's terms sort the two. In 1837 Baldwin, concurring in *Charles River Bridge*, took Marshall's sentence of 1830 that incorporation bestows "the character and properties of individuality on a collective and changing body of men," which Section III.C quotes as Marshall's, and added its consequence: "by which their rights become as sacred as if they were held in severalty by natural person."[^351] In 1933 Brandeis, dissenting in *Liggett*, wrote that "the prevalence of the corporation in America has led men of this generation to act, at times, as if the privilege of doing business in corporate form were inherent in the citizen," and added that "throughout the greater part of our history a different view prevailed."[^352] "As if" is the grammar of a fiction. Baldwin's is a lawful fiction stated as such, the common law's rule of treatment: the members' rights, held through the name, are treated as if held in severalty, and it is the members' rights he names. Brandeis's is a fiction of the impossible named as an error: a privilege cannot be inherent in the citizen, because what is inherent is not granted, and he says the generation acted "as if" it were. At page 351 the "as if" is gone. The privilege is treated as inherent, the exception's product as the citizen's own, and no one is told that anything is being supposed.

### I. The Whole People's Promise: What an Exception to the Name Costs and Whom, the Tax Side's Own Census of the Two Bonds, and the Door the People Kept Open

What the treasury holds, the law has also described, and the description is the whole people's. In *Juilliard v. Greenman* the Court read the monetary clauses as a power "to provide a national currency for the whole people, in the form of coin, treasury notes, and national bank bills," and legal tender as "one of the powers belonging to sovereignty in other civilized nations, and not expressly withheld from Congress by the Constitution."[^353] Behind the currency stands the credit of the United States: the power "To borrow Money on the credit of the United States," and the Fourteenth Amendment's command that "the validity of the public debt of the United States, authorized by law, … shall not be questioned."[^354] The notes themselves are, by the statute that created them, "obligations of the United States," receivable "for all taxes, customs, and other public dues."[^355] An obligation of the United States is an obligation of the whole people, payable from the taxing power, and the taxing power, in the structure Section II.E quoted from *McCulloch*, "acts upon its constituents": the legislature that imposes the tax answers to those who pay it. So in law each note in any hand is a claim on the whole people's taxed labor, issued under a structure in which the taxed are the represented. The note carries the bond whoever holds it. A dollar in any hand is the same promise, made by the same people and backed by the same tax, and a person spends it on speech under the freedom Section VI.G found to be theirs, needing no grant to do so. What the dollar is does not decide who may spend it. It decides what an exception costs, and whom: an exception to a treasury is paid in the whole people's promise, by the persons who bear the rate the promise rests on.

The rate is set against the whole. The public charge is fixed by what the people have undertaken to pay for, and the base is everything all produce; an exception to one class does not reduce the charge, it moves the burden to the rest. James Otis stated the principle in 1764: "*Qui sentit commodum sentire debet et onus*, has been thought a maxim of equity. But that a man should bear a burthen for other people, as well as himself, without a return, never long found a place in any law-book or decrees, but those of the most despotic princes."[^356] None of this makes differential taxation of the corporate privilege unlawful. It is within the sovereign's power, as *Flint* held of Congress and as Holmes and Brandeis said of the States; the Court has since said that "when it comes to taxes on corporations and taxes on individuals, great leeway is permissible so far as equal protection is concerned," and the Constitution forbids only a classification that is arbitrary rather than "based on a real and substantial difference having a reasonable relation to the subject of the particular legislation."[^357] The leeway runs both ways, and that is the point. A heavier charge on the privilege and a lighter one are both within the power; the warrant for either can only be the legislature's judgment of the public welfare, which is the charter's own reason for being, since the people permitted incorporation for any lawful business because they judged the business useful to them. The measure of an exception is therefore the grant's own, and it has three parts: the exception is warranted by the Constitution, by the purpose of the charter, and by the public welfare the grant was made to serve. Where all three fail, the burden shifted to natural persons is Otis's burden without a return, and the note in the excepted treasury is a claim on labor taxed from persons who are represented, held by a name that is not. The article states the devaluation that follows as a matter of representation and not as a prediction: the same promise backed by fewer who pay, and the deliberation that fixed the rate now counting a will that bore none of it. Whether a dollar buys less in consequence is a question of fact the article does not reach; what the law says a dollar is, and whose, is a question of text, and the text answers it.

The measure runs one way, as the article's test does. A treasury taxed without exception has no claim under this section and no reproach from it. That is this section's measure only. Such a treasury still speaks, when it speaks, with money no member consented to spend on speech, and Sections V.D and VI.A measure that; the two measures are independent, and a general treasury that passes the first fails the second unless its charter contains the speech. The business corporation and the consenting corporation are different bodies by the Court's own criterion. The body whose treasury could speak in *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* had the three features Section VI.A quotes, formed to promote political ideas and not for business, without shareholders or other persons with a claim on its assets, and not established by a business corporation or a union, features the Court called "essential to our holding," and the Court allowed that the class so defined "will be small."[^358] A charter that says "any lawful business" is consent to business and to no utterance, and a body whose charter contained a political position would be an advocacy corporation and could not, by the Court's first feature, be a business corporation. The treasury that would pass both measures is therefore the advocacy corporation and no other, which is where Section VI.A placed it.

The treasury's political will is then four things at once, and the article has met each elsewhere. It is unconsented, by members whose charter contains no speech (Section V.D). Where it is excepted, it is paid for in part by the citizen taxpayers, in the whole people's own promise, which is this section's addition. It is beyond the grant's purpose, which the sovereign's oldest writ asks after (Section IX.C), and which the Court itself has recorded as the ban's further interest, the protection of those who paid money into the corporation for other purposes, in the sentence Section VI.C quotes.[^359] And it is the political will of one or of some re-entering by the grant: the sovereign of one will that Section II.E named as the presupposition under the census, now in the treasury's hand, which is the thing Independence repudiated.

The argument is not *Austin*'s. *Austin* rested on "the corrosive and distorting effects of immense aggregations of wealth that are accumulated with the help of the corporate form," and judged the speech by the wealth behind it;[^360] the Court overruled it at page 365, "*Austin* … should be and now is overruled," returning to "the principle established in *Buckley* and *Bellotti* that the Government may not suppress political speech on the basis of the speaker's corporate identity."[^361] The two bonds judge no speech. They sort by the source of the claim, as Section VI.A sorted, and the treasury's claim has its source in the grant. Nor is the argument that the franchise is a precondition of speech. The adversary will say that speech never required the vote, that aliens and minors speak, and that is true: they are persons, inside the settled bond by the word "person" in the Fourteenth Amendment's second sentence (Sections III.E and VII.C), taxable as persons on income from whatever source derived, and the vote they lack is the one term of the standing they do not hold. The name is in neither bond's text. The vote shows that the name has no place in the settled bond at all, and Section VI.G has already found the freedom to be someone's. The Court itself left the governing question open three pages before it overruled *Austin*: "We need not reach the question whether the Government has a compelling interest in preventing foreign individuals or associations from influencing our Nation's political process."[^362] The question it did not reach is this section's question put of a name: whether what stands outside the settled bond may be kept from the deliberation that sets the bond's terms. And the test stays one-directional, as Section VII.C states it. The article does not hold § 441b valid. It says that the holding at page 351 rests on the word "speakers," the term Section VI.G identified, and that when the word is struck the sentence "All speakers … use money amassed from the economic marketplace to fund their speech" has no subject that the clause names. What remains is the clause, the persons who speak and hear under it, and the two bonds standing in the text where the sentence had erased them. The test has nothing against the clause. It has the word.

The Court has run the two bonds on the tax side already, in both directions, and the cases are the article's census in a second doctrine. In 1928 *Quaker City Cab Co. v. Pennsylvania* struck a tax on the gross receipts of corporations operating taxicabs because it did not reach "like receipts of natural persons and partnerships": the corporation was "entitled in Pennsylvania to the same protection of equal laws that natural persons within its jurisdiction have a right to demand under like circumstances," and "the character of the owner is the sole fact on which the distinction and discrimination are made to depend. The tax is imposed merely because the owner is a corporation."[^363] That is the transplant on the tax side: the unsettled bond handed the settled bond's shield against a heavier charge on the privilege, the name claiming against its grantor the equality of persons. The object principle of Section IV.B sorts it. A tax laid, in the Court's own words, "merely because the owner is a corporation" is a charge on the form, the second set, a condition of the grant that the grantor sets, and it reaches the members' property only as the privilege's cost; a tax laid on the property as property would be the members' and would travel through the name. Holmes's dissent is that principle in a sentence: "if the State desired to discourage this form of activity in corporate form and expressed its desire by a special tax I think that there is nothing in the Fourteenth Amendment to prevent it." Brandeis dissented from the record: "It has been the consistent policy of Pennsylvania since 1840 to subject businesses conducted by corporations to heavier taxation than like businesses conducted by individuals," Pennsylvania having been the first State to adopt a general corporation tax.[^364] His *Liggett* dissent five years later, quoted above, is the same dissent's conclusion.

In 1973 a unanimous Court retreated. *Lehnhausen v. Lake Shore Auto Parts Co.* quoted Holmes, recorded that "each of these dissenters thought *Flint v. Stone Tracy Co.* … should govern *Quaker City Cab*," and quoted its own earlier observation that "the existence, unchallenged, of differences between the taxation of incomes of individuals and of corporations in every federal revenue act since the adoption of the Sixteenth Amendment" shows that there may be just ground for the difference; then: "*Quaker City Cab Co. v. Pennsylvania* is only a relic of a bygone era. We cannot follow it and stay within the narrow confines of judicial review."[^365] The two bonds were restored on the tax side, for the same note, without the name. Twelve years later the transplant returned. *Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Ward* held that the Equal Protection Clause "imposes limits upon a State's power to condition the right of a foreign corporation to do business within its borders," and that "promotion of domestic business by discriminating against nonresident competitors is not a legitimate state purpose": equal protection applied between names, foreign insurers against domestic ones, with no person on either side, which presupposes the name as a "person" and so records the transplant's persistence after *Lehnhausen*, and says nothing of persons against names.[^366] Section III.J carries the three as one entry. The Court has also said, of the treasury itself, what this section says of it. The resources in a business corporation's treasury, the Court wrote in *Massachusetts Citizens for Life* in the sentence Section V.D quotes, are not an indication of popular support for its political ideas but reflect the economically motivated decisions of investors and customers, whereas a separate fund's in fact reflect popular support.[^367] That is the Court's own statement that the treasury's dollars carry no political consent, repeated in *Austin* and *Beaumont*, before page 351 declared the correlation irrelevant.[^368] The article adds what the dollars do carry: the taxpayers' bond.

The bond has a second dimension, and the state constitutions supply it. Virginia's people defined a corporation as whatever has powers or privileges that individuals and partnerships lack (Section II.G.4), which concedes the whole of Part One: everything the name has beyond a natural person is the grant's. The Tenth Amendment is the source of a State's power to make that grant, and the article does not call the grant a violation of it. The bound lies elsewhere, where a State's grant stops operating on the State's own constituents and begins operating on the whole people's bond, and Marshall stated the bound in the case Section II.E quoted. "The people of all the states have created the general government, and have conferred upon it the general power of taxation. The people of all the states, and the states themselves, are represented in congress, and, by their representatives, exercise this power. When they tax the chartered institutions of the states, they tax their constituents; and these taxes must be uniform. But when a state taxes the operations of the government of the United States, it acts upon institutions created, not by their own constituents, but by people over whom they claim no control."[^369] A government may burden only those it represents. That is the principle Marshall stated of the Union's instruments, and the article extends it, in its own voice and by Marshall's own terms, to a State's exception to a name; the extension is the article's and not his holding. A State's exception inverts the principle twice. Inside the State the burden shifts to the State's own natural persons, who are at least represented there. Outside it, so far as the dollar is the whole people's promise, the exception is paid in that promise by persons the State does not represent. The Court has never put that question, and in *Cuno*, as this section will show, it declined to hear the person who would put it. The cost side is the same sentence read again. The sovereign's goods, the courts that enforce the charter, the credit the notes rest on, the existence the name has "only in contemplation of law, and by force of law," are bought by natural persons at full price and, where the grantor excepts the name, by the name at a discount, and the discount is what the treasury spends on political will. Representation for the untaxed becomes governance at a lesser cost.

Two things the name spends in a federal election, and three grants would be needed for the spending to be its own; the text contains two. The people granted Congress the national instrument: "To lay and collect Taxes, Duties, Imposts and Excises"; "To borrow Money on the credit of the United States"; "To coin Money, regulate the Value thereof"; and *Juilliard* read the grant as a currency "for the whole people."[^370] The people of each State granted the name its incidents, which Virginia's definition (Section II.G.4) limits to what individuals and partnerships lack, and nothing else. Nowhere is there a third, and the third is not a grant to spend money, which no one needs, since a person spends the same promise on speech under the freedom of speech; it is a grant of a place in the deliberation, which the text gives to persons, by the vote and the voice, and to no name. What the excepted treasury spends in a federal election is therefore the people's twice over: the money, which to the extent of the exception is the whole people's promise paid by persons the exception did not reach, and the deliberation, which is the whole people's vote. For the second it holds a grant from no one; the first the grantor may condition as its own product, as Section VI.A showed. The text that excludes the States from the instrument says so in one sentence: "No State shall … coin Money; emit Bills of Credit; make any Thing but gold and silver Coin a Tender in Payment of Debts; pass any … Law impairing the Obligation of Contracts."[^371] The monetary exclusions and the Contract Clause that Section IV.E read as the people's rule about their servants stand in the same breath: the people withdrew from the States both the national instrument and the "boundless" power over the grant at once. *McCulloch* reaches past the letter, the means of the Union "should be subjected to that government only which belongs to all" (Section II.E); a State is not that government, and a corporation is not any government. Grier's sentence closes it: "State laws, by combining large masses of men under a corporate name, cannot repeal the Constitution,"[^372] and a State's grant cannot give the name a place in the people's deliberation either. In the law of fictions Section II.D set out, the wrong has a name at each step. It is a grantee acting beyond the grant, in the grantor's own deliberation. It is the usurpation Section V.D described, at its widest, since the consent usurped is the whole people's and the deliberation deceived is theirs. And it is a fiction held as law, the one Section VI.G identified: the treasury's money treated as the name's own speech in the people's deliberation, under the word "speakers."

The Court closed the door on the person who bears this burden, in the form Section II.E found in Blackstone. In *DaimlerChrysler Corp. v. Cuno* the Court held that "state taxpayers have no standing under Article III to challenge state tax or spending decisions simply by virtue of their status as taxpayers," because to hold otherwise "would interpose the federal courts as 'virtually continuing monitors of the wisdom and soundness' of state fiscal administration."[^373] That is the fiction of no redress at this exact door: a person with the burden and no hearing, the wrong supposed, out of decency, not to exist. The people kept another door open. Of the seventy-three instruments in Appendix A, fifty-two reserve in terms the power to alter, amend or repeal the grant,[^374] and the reservation is the people's own statement that the exception, like the existence, is theirs to withdraw.

The remedy is measurement, in the forms Section IX.C states for Congress, for the people of a State and for a court. Section 441b was the people conditioning the political use of a treasury they had excepted; the Court at page 351 asked whether a speaker may be silenced for the wealth behind it, and answered that it may not. The question the two bonds put is a different one, and the Court has not reached it: whether a grantee may spend the grantor's instrument, in the grantor's own deliberation, against the terms the grantor set.

## VII. The Removal Test: A Holding That Rests on a Coined Term Alone Supersedes Article V

Part Two has so far measured: the census found how each term entered, the two sets found what a corporation can hold, and Section VI found a right conferred on a name. What remains is the instrument that turns those findings into a rule a court can apply. This section derives it from *Marbury*, states it, refines its one qualifying word, names its two forms, and says why the remedy is not an amendment.

### A. The Premise, from *Marbury*: A Constitution "Unchangeable by Ordinary Means," and a Holding Is an Ordinary Means

The Constitution, Chief Justice Marshall wrote in *Marbury v. Madison*, "is either a superior paramount law, unchangeable by ordinary means, or it is on a level with ordinary legislative acts, and, like other acts, is alterable when the legislature shall please to alter it."[^375] Written constitutions, he continued, are "attempts, on the part of the people, to limit a power, in its own nature, illimitable," and "the theory of every such government must be, that an act of the legislature, repugnant to the constitution, is void."[^376] The sentence is usually read for what it says about legislatures. It says the same thing about courts. A judicial holding is an ordinary means. Article V is the only extraordinary one. If a holding changes what the Constitution grants, it has done by ordinary means what *Marbury* says cannot be done by them, and the fact that the means was a court rather than a legislature does not alter the arithmetic.

Why Article V is the only extraordinary means, and why a court cannot be another, is the second claim of this article, stated in Section II.E and applied here. The corporate form the Reports inherited was built for a sovereign of one will, who could grant a right in the name case by case, in his own person, and answer for it, and above whom stood a Parliament whose power over the body Blackstone called "boundless in it's operations."[^377] The people of the United States grant nothing in their own person; they consent to a corporation's existence in advance and in general, through their constitutions and general laws (Appendix A; Section IV.E), and a right in the name against themselves they can grant in one way only, by the instrument's own amendment. A holding that gives the name such a right therefore supplies a consent the people have no organ to give, and a court that does so has not interpreted the people's grant but made one, in the king's place. The ordinary means has done the extraordinary thing, and *Marbury*'s sentence is the measure of it.

Two Justices said this in the census's own case, and the test is their complaint made operable: Black's sentence of 1938[^378] and Douglas's of 1949,[^379] quoted in Section III.E, each say of the corporate "person" that it was an amendment by holding, and each name the people as the only hand that could have made it.

The strongest answer to the premise is that a holding is not an ordinary means at all. Article III vests "the judicial Power," and the judge of the seventy-eighth *Federalist*, preferring the Constitution to a statute, exercises a power the people vested; Marshall's sentence contrasts the Constitution with "ordinary legislative acts," not with judgments. And Madison wrote in the thirty-seventh that all new laws "are considered as more or less obscure and equivocal, until their meaning be liquidated and ascertained by a series of particular discussions and adjudications";[^380] on that view a century of corporate "persons," acquiesced in by every legislature, is the way the Amendment's meaning was settled, and the test would unsettle every liquidated meaning in the Reports. The article agrees that interpretation is the judicial power. The test is a way of telling interpretation from grant, and a grant is what that judge is forbidden to make, since a judge who grants by a word does what the seventy-eighth essay says the legislature may not. Liquidation fixes meaning among readings the text bears; Madison's sentence is about obscurity, and the claim here is that "person" read to a name is not a reading the Amendment's text bears at all, for the reasons Section III.E gave from the Amendment's own words. A practice cannot liquidate what the words exclude. Nor is acquiescence an act of the people in the sense *McCulloch* names when it locates the Constitution's whole authority in the people's conventions, "the only manner in which they can act safely, effectively and wisely, on such a subject";[^381] and *McCulloch* itself allows that "a bold and daring usurpation might be resisted, after an acquiescence still longer and more complete than this."[^382]

So the sentence stands, earned: a holding that grants is an ordinary means, whatever the power under which it was uttered. The same answer reaches stare decisis. A precedent is a holding, and a holding is an ordinary means; a line of precedents cannot supply what no one of them had, and a result that has moved from ground to ground through the Reports is not settled by the length of the line but by whether the last ground is a clause. Section VII.B states that as a rule.

The question is how to tell when a holding has done that, as opposed to interpreting the text, which is what holdings are for. The census in Section III supplies the materials for an answer. Every term on which a corporate constitutional right rests entered the Reports as a figure of speech or a transplant. But so did many terms on which no one would say the Constitution had been amended. "Strict scrutiny" is a coinage. So is "state action," so is "standing," so is "clear and present danger." If every coinage in a holding superseded Article V, there would be no constitutional law, and the claim would prove too much. A test is needed that distinguishes the coinage that interprets from the coinage that grants.

### B. The Test: Strike the Term and Look for the Clause

The test is removal. A holding that rests upon a coined term alone supersedes Article V. Article V is the one path the people provided for changing the text, and the Court has read it as the people's grant and as complete. "The Fifth Article is a grant of authority by the people to Congress," and its "language … is plain, and admits of no doubt in its interpretation"; ratification under it "is not an act of legislation within the proper sense of the word."[^383] It "subjects this power to only two restrictions."[^384] Marshall had named its machinery in 1833, "procuring a recommendation from two-thirds of congress, and the assent of three-fourths of their sister states," as the mode by which the federal instrument is changed, and set it against a State's own convention.[^385] Strike the term and look for the clause. If the result can be restated in the Constitution's own words, the term was a name for an interpretation, and the holding stands on the text. If, with the term struck, there is no path from any clause to the result, the term was the holding, and what it granted was granted by ordinary means. The measure has a name older than the test. The common law annexed to every incorporation the terms of its grant, and when the body exceeded them "the law judges that the body politic has broken the condition upon which it was incorporated"; the sovereign's writ then asked "by what warrant the members now exercise their corporate power."[^386] The removal test asks of the holding what the writ asked of the body: by what warrant in the text does the name hold what the holding gave it.

What the test detects can be stated in the common law's own vocabulary, recovered in Section II.D. A coined term is a tool of construction until it is held as law. A tool disposes of nothing: strike it and the holding stands on a clause, so the term carried the court's reasoning and granted nothing. A term held as law disposes of something: strike it and the holding falls, so the term was the disposition; and a disposition of the law against the truth, in a possible matter and from a just cause, is Godefroy's definition of a fiction, while one against the truth in an impossible matter is what the common law refused to make. The test detects the moment of conversion. Marshall's "considered as" a person "when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it" is the tool, with its purpose attached;[^387] *Santa Clara*'s remark is the same word, said from the bench in 1886 and held as law by citation from *Beckwith* in 1889, the purpose dropped and argument refused, and the census records the conversion before the test measures it, in the next citation that calls the term a holding (Section III.J); and Stone named the conversion when he wrote that to use the fiction's word to decide a constitutional question "is to beg the question to be decided" (Section II.D). What falls with the term was therefore a fiction held as law, and it fails each of Section II.D's three bounds, as that section showed. The common law kept its lawful fictions as fictions, named them, and bounded them. The Court's error was not that it used one but that it stopped calling it one.

This is also why the remedy of Section VII.E is measurement and not proof. The civil law's rule that Blackstone carried into the Commentaries, *contra fictionem non admittitur probatio*, is not a quirk of the law of fictions but its logic: a fiction is a disposition against the truth, so proof of the truth cannot be allowed to reach it, or it would not be a fiction (Section II.D). A fiction held as law is therefore immune to the ordinary argument of a case. Two centuries of argument that a corporation has no conscience, casts no vote and is born of a grant were answered, when they were answered at all, with the term's age and with citation of the term, Jackson in 1949 and the majority in 2010, and never with the clause (Section III.E); the Court declined to hear the argument at the fiction's birth in 1886, and the holding's own law has excused it from answering since. The only act that reaches a fiction held as law is to set it aside and read the text without it, which is what the test does. It does not prove the term false. It removes the term and asks for the clause.

One more feature of a fiction held as law must be stated here, because it governs how the test is run on a line of cases rather than on one. The maxim says that equity always subsists in a fiction of law; a fiction is equity's instrument, and in a judge-made holding the result is the constant and the ground the variable. Strike the ground and the result looks for another. The census in Section III is the record of that migration in one doctrine: "person" said for the shareholders' property in 1886 and held by citation in 1889, refused for liberty in 1906 and 1907, taken up again as "speaker" and as "associations of citizens," each ground taking the result over from the last; and the Court's path from the civil-rights cases to the treasury, traced in Section IV.B, is the same movement, a description of the NAACP in 1963 cited as a holding in 1978 and again in 2010.[^388] Stare decisis then freezes whichever ground the result last reached. The precedent does not hold the ground; the result does. That is the fragility of a line of holdings not grounded in the text, and the test meets it with a rule in three sentences. Respite in a clause is interpretation: if the result, struck from its coined ground, can be restated from a clause argued from its own word, the test has nothing against it and the ordinary question is open, which is the case of *Griswold*'s judgment in Section VIII, where the holding's coinages fall and two concurrences supply the Ninth Amendment and "liberty." Respite in a fiction is the same fiction under another name: if the new ground is another coined term, the test strikes it in turn, and a result that can find respite only in coined terms has, by that fact, no ground in the text at all. And stare decisis cannot supply a ground, because a precedent is itself an ordinary means. The test is run on each ground as the result reaches it, and each re-grounding is a new disposition to be measured.

The word that does the work is *alone*. A holding that rests on the text plus a coined method does not rest on the coinage alone. Strike "strict scrutiny" from a decision voiding a law that abridges a person's speech, and what remains is a law abridging the speech of a person the First Amendment names, judged more or less demandingly; the term organized the analysis and granted nothing. Strike "state action" and what remains is "No State shall," which is in the text. These terms survive removal because the text carries the result without them. The terms the census found carrying a right do not survive it. Strike "person" as extended to the corporation and there is no clause of the Fourteenth Amendment that reaches a corporation; the chain's only argument is *Pembina*'s, that the corporation is "merely associations of individuals,"[^389] which reaches the individuals and stops. Strike "corporate democracy" and there is no ground for denying the shareholder the compelled-subsidy protection the Court gives a union member.[^390] Strike "speaker" as applied to the name in *Citizens United*, which is where that holding's coinage sits, and the Speech Clause is left restraining Congress from abridging a freedom no natural person lost (Section VI.G). The holding's one respite in a clause is the hearers': the freedom of speech read to the natural persons who would have heard, which the Court's listener cases state, *Virginia Pharmacy* first, and which Section VI.G calls first-set; on that ground the test has nothing against the holding, and whether the hearers lost anything is the ordinary question, which Section VI.G answers from the statute's own exemptions and not from the test. In each the term is the only path from the text to the name's own right. Without it there is no argument for the name, only the result. The control is *Dartmouth*: strike "artificial being" and its incidents "incidental to its very existence" and the Contract Clause holding loses nothing it needed, because it stands on the trustees' charter and on *Fletcher*'s rule that a grant is a contract; the test has nothing against it, and what the strike removes is the holder the opinion named (Section IV.E).

The test is removal, not novelty, and that matters. It does not ask whether a term is in the Constitution; "strict scrutiny" is not. It does not ask whether the term was coined by a judge; nearly all of them were. It asks what falls when the term is struck. A term that falls alone, leaving the holding standing on a clause, was interpretation. A term that takes the holding down with it was the grant.

### C. "Alone," Refined: A Term Naming a Necessary Implication Survives Removal; a Term Supplying an Extension, a Rank or a Combination Does Not

Section VIII runs the test on a case whose judgment the article's author would keep, and the running of it produced a refinement of "alone" that should be stated here. Some coined terms name a *necessary implication* of a clause: the clause cannot mean what it says unless the implied thing is also protected. Those terms survive removal, because the implication can be restated as the clause's own meaning. Other coined terms supply an *extension* of a clause beyond the thing it names, or a *rank* for that extension that gives it constitutional force outside the words, or a *combination* of extensions into a new right the clauses do not separately contain. Those terms do not survive removal, because without them the clause means only what it says and the result has no source.

The distinction is not invented for the occasion. It is Marshall's own, in *Deveaux*, where the name is "considered as" a person "when the general spirit and purpose of the law requires it," as Section VII.B quoted it, and it is the Court's own in *Griswold*, reading *NAACP v. Alabama*: the right of association, "while it is not expressly included in the First Amendment," exists because "its existence is necessary in making the express guarantees fully meaningful."[^391] That is a necessity argument. The right to assemble is defeated if the State may punish the membership; the express guarantee entails the unexpressed one; the implication can be stated in the text's own terms with no figure of speech at all. Section VIII shows the same opinion that states this argument abandoning it two pages later.

The refinement has a second part, and it answers the objection the test most invites. "Person" is in the Fourteenth Amendment. When Section VII.B strikes "person" as extended to the corporation, it strikes a reading of a word inside the clause, not a word outside it, and an adversary will say that the test is then deciding what the word bears, which is interpretation, with Article V as rhetoric; and that if every reading past a word's first instances fails removal, "speech" read to film superseded Article V too. The answer is that removal performs two operations, and the article names them. For a coinage, "speaker," "corporate democracy," "strict scrutiny," it strikes a word outside the text and asks whether any clause carries the result. For a transplant, a word carried from one body of law into the text's word of the same spelling, it strikes the imported sense and asks two questions the Court never asked in the chain Section III.E traces. Was the sense argued for? That is a fact about the Reports, and the census answers it: never. Does the text's own structure bear it? That is interpretation, and the article says so without embarrassment; Section III.E's reading of the Amendment's first sentence, "All persons born or naturalized," is the ordinary question, answered. The Article V step follows only when both fail. A sense never argued for, which the text's structure excludes, has nothing under it but the word; that is "alone" in the literal sense, the sentence "corporations are persons within the meaning of the clause" with nothing beneath it in the Reports. The test's work is to force the question the Court assumed. It does not pretend to answer it by striking.

The sense was never argued for because the Court declined to hear argument. The transplant's founding moment is the statement before argument in *Santa Clara*, quoted in Section III.E: counsel's brief had put the point "at length," and the Chief Justice said that the Court did not wish to hear it and that "We are all of opinion that it does."[^392] Under the law of fictions recovered in Section II.D that is the maxim enacted at the birth of the fiction held as law: against a fiction no proof is admitted, and here the proof was refused before the fiction had been held at all. The two questions the test asks are the two things the common law's own law required of a fiction, a cause and a possible matter, and the Court omitted both.

The transplant's own history is the proof that the word stood alone. Property travels through the name as the incident of the members' holding (Section IV.B), so *Santa Clara*'s result for the shareholders' property needed no "person" at all; the word was unnecessary where it was first used. The Court then refused the same word for liberty, in *Riggs*, *Western Turf* and *Berea*, and later took it for the treasury not by argument but by citation of the civil-rights cases: *Bellotti*'s sixteenth footnote cited *NAACP v. Alabama* and *Button* against the argument that the Amendment's liberty belongs to natural persons, and *Citizens United* cited *Button* and *Grosjean* as the "explicit holdings" by which First Amendment protection "has been extended" to corporations.[^393] Unnecessary, then refused, then inherited: at no point argued from the Amendment's words.

The structure excludes the sense, and the two bonds and the three bounds say how. The Amendment's first sentence defines the settled bond of Section VI.H: born or naturalized, therefore citizens. Its second sentence reaches past them, as Section III.E showed, to any natural person who can be deprived of life and liberty; a name is born of a grant, naturalized by nothing, and has no life or liberty to lose. The transplant moved the name into the bond's word without the bond. And the imported sense fails the three bounds on its face, as Section II.D showed of the corporate "person": not of a possible matter, not necessary, and working an injury. The Court itself stated the Article V step in 1854, in the citizenship line, when it refused to let the corporate name carry a jurisdictional consequence the Constitution did not grant.
